Week 1: Foundations of Soft Skills and Communication

  1. Lecture 1: Introduction to Soft Skills

Lecture 1: Introduction to Soft Skills

Week 1 · Course book PDF pages 5–23 (printed pages 4–22)

Learning objectives

  • Understand what soft skills are and why the term is called an umbrella term.
  • Distinguish soft skills from hard skills.
  • Explain why soft skills matter for employability and career growth.
  • Recall the working definition and the key skills named in the lecture (self-awareness, flexibility, persistence).

Important definitions

Soft skills

An umbrella term for people skills, life skills and behavioural skills. Working definition: soft skills comprise personality traits, social graces, facility with language, personal habits, friendliness and optimism that mark people to varying degrees.

Hard skills

Subject knowledge and technical expertise gained through college or university study; part of the formal curriculum.

Employability

The ability to get and keep a job; the lecturer calls it the “keyword”, since degrees alone do not make a person employable.

Core concepts

Soft skills are more than communication skills

Many people think soft skills = communication skills. Communication is only one part. Others include interpersonal skills, management skills, leadership skills and team dynamics. Soft skills cannot be learned in a day: they are a lifelong, continuous learning process.

The lecturer’s two illustrations

  • Gift-shop manager: a young manager with no degree won the customer by helping, analysing the need, being friendly and reassuring. Another salesman who also spoke did not have the “extra edge”, so communication alone is not the answer.
  • Two doctors on one road: both had foreign degrees and were toppers, yet one clinic was crowded. The difference lay in how they talked to patients, their use of simple language (the doctor who said “hypochondria” confused the patient) and how friendly the staff were.

Behavioural skills the lecture names

  • Self-awareness – awareness of present-day needs of the world and of one’s own abilities.
  • Flexibility.
  • Persistence and perseverance.
  • Self-confidence, optimism, cultural sensitivity, being accommodating and a good listener.

Why soft skills matter

  • They help us come out of difficult situations and give reassurance and reliability.
  • Two equally qualified people can get different packages because of soft skills.
  • Job requirements are changing: employers want people who take initiative, make decisions, negotiate and innovate.
  • There is a gap between academia and industry; organisations often train new recruits in soft skills.
  • Soft skills make an employee more cordial, accountable and able to take decisions without the boss’s direction.
  • Needed in meetings, group discussions, drafting business documents, telephone deals, electronic writing and representing the organisation.
  • In a globalised “global village”, cultural sensitivity and listening are essential.

Evidence cited (employability gap)

  • Census 2011 data reported by The Hindu: unemployment is high among the better qualified.
  • Report: 23 lakh people applied for a peon’s job in U.P., including engineers and doctors.
  • Survey in Aspiring Minds: many professionals are unemployable because of poor communication skills.
  • Kiran Karnik (former NASSCOM president): we must groom qualified students to make them employable.
  • ILO report: India faces “jobless growth”; Rituparna Chakraborty (Vice President, Indian Staffing Federation): “We need to invest in and build skills among the youth.”
  • Government initiatives mentioned: Skill India, Start-up India, Make in India; NPTEL itself aims to bridge the gap.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Hard skills versus soft skills (as described in the lecture)
BasisHard skillsSoft skills
NatureSubject knowledge, technical expertisePersonality traits, people skills, life skills, behavioural skills
SourceTaught in college curriculumNot taught as a college subject; learned through exposure, practice and training programmes
RoleGet you the job (technical competence)Make you excel and shine in the job; “performance skills”
RelationshipNeed soft skills to be effectiveComplement hard skills: “drive your hard skills through the engine of soft skills”

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Swami Vivekananda’s mantra — Quoted in the lecture

“Arise, awake, and stop not till the goal is reached.” Arise = become self-aware; awake = become self-confident; do not stop = become dynamic through soft skills.

Examples and applications

Example

Gift-shop manager who lacked an MBA yet persuaded the customer through helpfulness and need analysis.

Example

Two doctors with identical foreign degrees: the crowded clinic is the one with better patient dealing and friendlier staff.

Example

A recruiter’s remark: “I would love to hire a girl with a pleasant smile and better communication skills than one having a very good CGPA and doing nothing.”

Comparison tables

Soft skills versus communication skills
PointSoft skillsCommunication skills
ScopeUmbrella term covering many skillsOne component of soft skills
IncludesInterpersonal, management, leadership, team dynamics, self-awareness, flexibility, persistenceSpeaking, listening, writing, non-verbal communication

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Soft skills is an umbrella term; they are also called life skills, people skills and behavioural skills.
  • Estimates of the number of soft skills vary (28, 60 or 10) because the skills overlap.
  • Working definition: personality traits, social graces, facility with language, personal habits, friendliness and optimism.
  • Behavioural skills named first: self-awareness, then flexibility, then persistence and perseverance.
  • Soft skills are performance skills and a process of continuous learning.
  • Hard skills are part of the college curriculum; soft skills are not taught as a college subject.
  • Recruiter quote: pleasant smile and communication skills preferred over a very good CGPA by someone doing nothing.
  • Swami Vivekananda’s mantra is “Arise, awake, and stop not till the goal is reached.”

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Soft skills are an umbrella term for people, life and behavioural skills such as self-awareness, flexibility, persistence, friendliness and optimism. They complement hard (technical) skills, make people employable and are learned continuously. Communication is only one part of soft skills. The widening gap between academia and industry, and rising unemployment among qualified people, show why employers value them.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ Which statement best describes soft skills?
  1. Technical expertise gained in college
  2. An umbrella term for people, life and behavioural skills
  3. Only communication skills
  4. Skills taught in every university syllabus
Answer: B
Why: The lecture calls soft skills an umbrella term and cautions that they are not merely communication skills.
Q2 MCQ Which of these is a hard skill?
  1. Persistence
  2. Friendliness
  3. Technical expertise in a subject
  4. Optimism
Answer: C
Why: Hard skills are subject knowledge and technical expertise.
Q3 MSQ Select the behavioural skills named in the lecture.
  1. Self-awareness
  2. Flexibility
  3. Typing speed
  4. Persistence and perseverance
Answer: A, B, D
Why: These are the personal charms listed; typing speed is not mentioned.
Q4 T/F Communication skills and soft skills mean exactly the same thing.
Answer: False
Why: Communication is only a part of the wider set of soft skills.
Q5 Fill Soft skills are performance skills, while hard skills are part of the ______.
Answer: college curriculum
Why: Soft skills are not taught in colleges the way subject knowledge is.
Q6 Short Why were the two doctors in the lecture’s example differently successful?
Answer: Their soft skills differed.
Why: One used simple language, took the case history and had friendly staff; the other confused patients with jargon.

Revision checklist

  • I can state the working definition of soft skills.
  • I can list three differences between hard and soft skills.
  • I know why communication skills alone are not soft skills.
  • I can quote Vivekananda’s mantra and explain its three parts.
  • I remember the employability evidence (Census 2011, 23 lakh peon applicants, NASSCOM remark).

Lecture 2: Aspects of Soft Skills

Week 1 · Course book PDF pages 24–40 (printed pages 23–39)

Learning objectives

  • List the main aspects (components) of soft skills: communication, attitude, adaptability, teamwork, conflict management, leadership, time and space skills, mannerism and cultural sensitivity.
  • Explain the four communication skills (listening, speaking, reading, writing) and why listening comes first.
  • Apply De Bono’s Six Thinking Hats to problem solving.
  • Recall the quotations and thinkers used in the lecture.

Important definitions

Effective communication

Communication that serves the requisite purpose (e.g. you order a book and receive it in time). It needs more than words: structure, sentence construction, non-verbal cues and the receiver’s point of view.

Communication

From the Latin communicare, meaning to share an idea or experience through a medium.

Attitude

A tendency or willingness displayed to get work done in the right direction and in the right way.

Adaptability

Ability to adjust to differing situations, tools, versions and ways of working.

Homophily

Common experiences or familiarity between sender and receiver, which make communication faster and more vital.

Conflict management

Using soft skills to resolve a conflict so that it does not turn into a quarrel; healthy conflict is functional and promotes creative thinking and team decisions.

Core concepts

Aspects of soft skills

  1. Effective communication – listening, speaking, reading, writing.
  2. Attitude.
  3. Adaptability.
  4. Teamwork and conflict management.
  5. Leadership – a trait developed over time; leaders emerge from critical situations (e.g. rescue a group discussion from being sidetracked).
  6. Time and space skills – everyone has only 24 hours; effective managers manage time.
  7. Mannerism – learnable; organisations look for well-mannered people.
  8. Cultural sensitivity – respect others’ culture, faith and beliefs.
  9. Problem solving (e.g. Six Thinking Hats).

The four communication skills

  • Listening – first and most neglected; 60–75 per cent of working time is spent listening. All hearing is not listening: hearing needs no conscious effort, listening is a mental activity (active/perceptive listening).
  • Speaking – needed in interviews, group discussions, data collection, product launches, negotiations, presentations. A communication starts with a message and ends in a response.
  • Reading – on the decline because of screens; “Reading maketh a full man”.
  • Writing – the most challenging; business writing is meant to express and not to impress; avoid pompous words; follow the organisation’s structure.

Attitude: the five Cs to avoid

Organisations suffer from “emotional cancers”: criticizing, complaining, comparing, competing and contending instead of concentrating on one’s own work. (“My shirt is whiter than yours.”)

Teamwork and conflict

  • Projects come as team projects; a leader should bind, cohere and accommodate different natures.
  • Own both defeat and victory: no blame game; say “team’s victory”.
  • Suzanne Bidlake: “In most models of future organization, teams, not individuals function as the primary performance unit.”
  • David Hume: “Truth springs from argument amongst friends” – let there be argument, not quarrel. Emotions should not enter conflict resolution.
  • Advantages of conflict management: functional, healthy, ensures creative thinking and promotes team decisions.

Culture

The lecture quotes “Culture is a code we learn and share” (the transcript says Adam Smith; the quiz attributes the definition to Alfred G. Smith, Communication and Culture). Accept, accommodate and assimilate; communication should be a binding, not a dividing, force. Not everything in your culture is good, nor everything in another’s bad.

Leadership

Not all can be leaders. A leader emerges because of merit and constructive suggestions, builds support logically, and saves the group from deviating. Leadership is developed through awareness and interaction.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Six Thinking Hats (Edward De Bono)
HatThinking mode
WhiteObjective, facts and information
RedEmotions and feelings
YellowPositive mindset, optimism
GreenGenerating ideas, creativity
BlueControlling and organising the thinking process
BlackCaution, fighting against and preventing mistakes (not detailed in this lecture’s text)

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Six Thinking Hats — Edward De Bono

A technique for problem solving: when all members take turns wearing different coloured hats, different kinds of ideas emerge, giving more creative and conclusive solutions.

Quotations to remember
  • David Hume – “Truth springs from argument amongst friends.”
  • Suzanne Bidlake – teams, not individuals, are the primary performance unit.
  • “Reading maketh a full man.”
  • Culture is “a code we learn and share” (Alfred G. Smith).

Examples and applications

Example

Missed calls used by youngsters as a code: repeated missed calls lead to confusion, showing communication can fail.

Example

Cricket: the captain says “it is the team’s victory”, not the individual’s.

Example

In a group discussion a person with no points at first can contribute later if he keeps listening patiently.

Comparison tables

Hearing versus listening
HearingListening
Physiological (physical) activityMental activity
Needs no conscious effortNeeds conscious participation and extra effort
PassiveActive/perceptive

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Latin communicare = to share.
  • 60–75 per cent of one’s time in any job is spent in listening.
  • Four communication skills: listening, speaking, reading, writing; writing is the most challenging.
  • Business writing: express, do not impress.
  • Five negative Cs: criticizing, complaining, comparing, competing, contending.
  • Green hat = generating ideas; white = objective; red = emotion; yellow = positive; blue = control.
  • Hume: truth springs from argument amongst friends.
  • Culture is a code we learn and share (Alfred G. Smith in the quiz).
  • Homophily = common experience between communicators.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Soft skills have several aspects: effective communication (listening, speaking, reading, writing), attitude, adaptability, teamwork, conflict management, leadership, time and space skills, mannerism and cultural sensitivity. Listening is the foundation; business writing aims to express, not impress. Teams, not individuals, are the performance unit, and constructive conflict helps. De Bono’s hats support creative problem solving.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ Which hat is associated with generating ideas?
  1. White
  2. Red
  3. Green
  4. Blue
Answer: C
Why: Green hat = idea generation.
Q2 MCQ The word ‘communication’ comes from communicare, meaning:
  1. To speak
  2. To share
  3. To write
  4. To command
Answer: B
Why: Communication is sharing ideas or experiences.
Q3 MSQ Select the four communication skills discussed.
  1. Listening
  2. Speaking
  3. Reading
  4. Writing
  5. Painting
Answer: A, B, C, D
Why: LSRW are the four skills.
Q4 T/F Hearing and listening require the same amount of conscious effort.
Answer: False
Why: Hearing is physical and needs no conscious effort; listening is mental and conscious.
Q5 Fill Business writing is meant to express and not to ______.
Answer: impress
Why: Avoid pompous words; be clear.
Q6 Short Name the five negative Cs that harm attitude in organisations.
Answer: Criticizing, complaining, comparing, competing and contending.
Why: They replace concentration on one’s own work.

Revision checklist

  • I can list the aspects of soft skills.
  • I know the four communication skills and why listening comes first.
  • I can match each Six Thinking Hat to its function.
  • I remember Hume, Bidlake and the culture quotation.
  • I can state the 60–75 per cent listening fact.

Lecture 3: Effective Communication Skills

Week 1 · Course book PDF pages 41–54 (printed pages 40–53)

Learning objectives

  • Define communication and explain why it is called a process.
  • Describe the elements of the communication process: sender, encoding, message, channel, receiver, decoding, feedback, frame of reference, noise.
  • State the qualities of effective communication (the 7 Cs as presented).
  • Differentiate general-purpose and organisational communication, and internal and external communication, including the grapevine.

Important definitions

Communication

To express an idea, desire, expectation or experience; from Latin communicare (to share). It is effective only when it meets its desired goal.

Common frame of reference

Shared background (age, experience, knowledge, tastes) between sender and receiver that makes communication successful.

Encoding

The sender’s act of putting an idea into a message (words, symbols, language) before sending.

Decoding

The receiver’s act of extracting the meaning from the received message.

Feedback

The receiver’s response; only feedback shows that the communication process is complete.

Grapevine

An informal channel of organisational communication, often a rumour mill; information may be unauthentic or inadequate, though it may also forewarn management.

Homophily

Common experiences between communicators.

Core concepts

Illusion of communication

George Bernard Shaw: “The greatest problem in communication is the illusion that it has been accomplished.” The real test is whether the message sent has been received properly.

Communication as a process

  1. The sender/source has an idea, experience, desire or fact.
  2. The sender considers the receiver’s background, encodes the idea in a message and selects a channel.
  3. The message is transmitted; obstruction (noise, wrong medium, difficult language) may change it.
  4. The receiver decodes the message.
  5. The receiver gives feedback, which completes the process.

Absence of a response does not always mean failure; there may be an obstruction.

Factors that affect success

  • Perception differs between sender and receiver.
  • Environment (e.g. a noisy marketplace spoils a phone call).
  • Medium (the teacher who did not have email access); choose a medium the receiver is accustomed to.
  • Language and vocabulary (Marathi to a Tamil speaker fails; difficult English words fail because nobody sits with a thesaurus).
  • Receiver’s attention and background.

Qualities of effective communication (the 7 Cs as presented)

  • Clarity – be clear and avoid difficult words.
  • Completeness – no half sentences; written messages cannot be clarified on the spot.
  • Conciseness – use short sentences yet be specific; the “hallmark of communication”.
  • Correctness – grammatical and accurate.
  • Courtesy – consideration and sympathy for the receiver, especially seniors and elders.
  • Candidness – integrity, honesty and fairness.
  • Consideration of the receiver’s background and knowledge (the text names the seven but spells out these six plus consideration; the exact seventh appears only in the original slide).

Organisational communication

Differs from casual communication; words must be specific because people of different faiths, beliefs and positions work together. Think of collective responsibility.

  • Internal – within the organisation: formal (meetings, policies) and informal. Flow: upward, downward, horizontal (lateral), vertical, diagonal/across departments.
  • External – with clients, customers, government agencies and the public; builds a positive image (promotion, website, pamphlets, letters, internet).

The grapevine

Formal communication is to the point, so employees seek an informal channel. Grapevine information is often unauthentic and can create factionalism; managers sometimes use it carefully for early warnings. Use it not to discard or discredit, and stay away from rumour.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Direction of internal communication
DirectionMeaning / example
DownwardFrom management to employees (new policy, DA hike)
UpwardFrom employees to superiors (feedback, requests)
Horizontal / lateralBetween people of the same status or post
Diagonal / across departmentsBetween different departments and levels

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Process model of communication

Sender (idea) → encode → channel → receiver → decode → feedback. A common frame of reference and absence of noise help it succeed.

Examples and applications

Example

A child who cries when hungry communicates without words; only the mother knows the cause.

Example

Sharing a book or movie is easiest with a friend of similar age, experience and knowledge.

Example

An email sent to a teacher who has no email access never gets a response; a phone call fixes it.

Comparison tables

General-purpose versus organisational communication
PointGeneral-purpose (casual)Organisational
ParticipantsFriends and relativesEmployees at different positions, clients, public
LanguageCasual, loose word controlSpecific, careful, considers beliefs and positions
AimSocial sharingSpecific organisational goal
Internal versus external communication
InternalExternal
Within the organisation and with employeesWith clients, customers, government agencies, the public
Formal and informal (incl. grapevine)Product promotion, website, letters; builds image

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Bernard Shaw: the greatest problem in communication is the illusion that it has been accomplished.
  • Communication is a process; feedback completes it.
  • A common frame of reference is essential for success.
  • Conciseness is the hallmark of communication.
  • Grapevine = informal channel, often rumour-based and not always authentic; it may be used carefully.
  • Internal flow: upward, downward, horizontal, vertical, diagonal.
  • Extracting meaning from a message is decoding; forming it is encoding.
  • Latin communicare = to share.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Communication is a process in which a sender encodes an idea, sends it through a channel, the receiver decodes it, and feedback completes the cycle. A shared frame of reference, a suitable medium and environment, and a message that is clear, complete, concise, correct, courteous and candid improve effectiveness. Organisational communication may be internal (upward, downward, horizontal, diagonal, including the grapevine) or external.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ The receiver’s task of extracting meaning from a message is called:
  1. Encoding
  2. Decoding
  3. Feedback
  4. Channelling
Answer: B
Why: Receivers decode; senders encode.
Q2 MCQ Shared background of experience between sender and receiver is the:
  1. Common frame of reference
  2. Grapevine
  3. Channel
  4. Feedback loop
Answer: A
Why: It makes communication successful and effective.
Q3 MSQ Which are directions of internal communication?
  1. Upward
  2. Downward
  3. Horizontal
  4. Subterranean
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Diagonal is also mentioned; ‘subterranean’ is not.
Q4 T/F The grapevine always gives authentic information.
Answer: False
Why: It is informal and may be rumour-based and inadequate.
Q5 Fill Communication is complete only when ______ is received.
Answer: feedback
Why: Feedback shows the process has been completed.
Q6 Short Name two factors that can obstruct a message.
Answer: Noise in the environment and an unsuitable medium or language.
Why: Perception differences also matter.

Revision checklist

  • I can draw and explain the communication process.
  • I can explain the Shaw quotation.
  • I know the qualities of effective communication.
  • I can distinguish internal, external and grapevine communication.
  • I know the directions of internal communication.

Lecture 4: Classification of Communication

Week 1 · Course book PDF pages 55–69 (printed pages 54–68)

Learning objectives

  • Compare oral and written communication.
  • Classify communication by number of participants: intrapersonal, interpersonal, extra-personal, group and mass/media.
  • Explain verbal, non-verbal and meta-communication, and print vs electronic media.
  • Understand cross-cultural, ethical and legal aspects (plagiarism, libel, slander).

Important definitions

Intrapersonal communication

Communication within oneself: you talk to yourself; the eyes act as transmitter, the central nervous system as medium and the brain as receiver, with feedback in bodily reactions. Helps self-discovery and recovery.

Interpersonal communication

Communication between two individuals (or two groups), usually conversation or dialogue; also called dyadic communication and ‘relationship communication’.

Extra-personal communication

Communication between humans and animals (or animals among themselves) without language; responses come through movements and sounds.

Group communication

Communication among members of a group (classroom lecture, group discussion, meeting).

Mass and media communication

Communication to large audiences through electrical and mechanical devices (newspaper, television); receivers are largely passive.

Meta-communication

Communication beyond words, including unintentional meanings that listeners find in what is said.

Libel and slander

Criticism of an organisation or person in writing is libel; in speech it is slander. Both are unethical and may be illegal.

Plagiarism

Using someone’s words or ideas without acknowledgment; to be avoided with misquoting.

Core concepts

Oral and written communication

  • Oral: spontaneous, warm, immediate feedback and clarification, uses voice to carry emotion, can be informal or formal, reaches small or large groups; forms include briefing, talk, speech, group discussion, paper presentation, meetings, interviews and telephone.
  • Written: organised, formal, a record; feedback is delayed; follows standards of grammar and structure; cannot be easily changed once sent; can reach a very large audience across the world. Business writing includes letters, reports, memos, notices, newsletters, circulars, brochures and research papers.

Four forms by number of participants

Humans communicate in four (five) ways depending on the number of participants: intrapersonal, interpersonal, extra-personal, group and mass/media.

  • Intrapersonal examples: soliloquy and aside in plays (Hamlet’s “To be or not to be”).
  • Interpersonal depends on the relationship with the other party (e.g. boss). Interviews are interpersonal.
  • Group: silence in a meeting also communicates.
  • Mass/media: less chance of clarification and less personal rapport; sender cannot control a wide audience.

Verbal, non-verbal and meta-communication

  • All spoken or written communication is verbal (uses words). Meaning lies with the sender, not the word.
  • Non-verbal cues supplement words (body movements, face); the lecture says there are more than 6 lakh non-verbal cues. “Whatever you say is written on your face.”
  • Meta-communication is beyond words and may carry unintended meaning.

Print and electronic media

Written words keep their importance in print and electronic form (email, chat). Think before writing: messages can be interpreted differently and may have impact in a crisis.

Cross-cultural communication

  • Respect religious sentiments, etiquette, tastes, habits and the concept of time.
  • In Latin America meetings may start late because people first discuss and eat.
  • Gifts: giving a Chinese person a clock may suggest inviting evil.
  • Do not assume your culture’s understanding is universal; allow room to receivers; mind status patterns.

Ethical and legal aspects

  • Every organisation has an ethical code and policy statements; employees must respect them (e.g. using organisational funds to travel for a personal interview is unethical).
  • Avoid misquoting, plagiarism and inaccurate information; acknowledge sources.
  • Do not criticise your organisation to get another job; remember you are its ambassador.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Classification of human communication by participants
TypePartiesKey features / examples
IntrapersonalOne person (self)Self-talk, soliloquy; self-discovery; brain gives feedback
Interpersonal (dyadic)Two persons or two groupsConversation, dialogue, interview; depends on relationship
Extra-personalHumans and animalsSounds, movements, wagging tails; no language
GroupMembers of a groupLecture, GD, meeting; formalities; silence also communicates
Mass and mediaOne source to a large audienceNewspaper, TV; passive receiver; little personal rapport

Examples and applications

Example

A student who missed a good grade talks to himself and realises the lapses: intrapersonal communication.

Example

Hamlet’s soliloquy “To be or not to be, that is the question.”

Example

Presenting a clock to a Chinese colleague may be read as inviting evil.

Example

Latin American meetings begin after discussion and food.

Libel versus slander
LibelSlander
Defamatory statement in writingDefamatory statement in speech

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Interpersonal communication is also called dyadic communication.
  • Intrapersonal: brain acts as receiver; soliloquy and aside are examples.
  • Mass/media receivers are passive; media include newspapers and television.
  • More than 6 lakh non-verbal cues exist.
  • Meaning lies with the sender, not in words.
  • Written criticism = libel; spoken = slander.
  • A clock as a gift may be considered bad omen in Chinese culture.
  • Latin American meetings often start late after discussion and food.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W1 Q15p.5Thinkers and theories of personalityMatch the columns

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Communication may be oral or written and, by participants, intrapersonal, interpersonal, extra-personal, group or mass/media. It is also verbal, non-verbal or meta-communication, carried in print or electronic media, and shaped by culture, ethics and law. Effective communicators avoid plagiarism, libel and slander and communicate ‘to bind, not to divide’.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ Which form of communication is also called dyadic?
  1. Intrapersonal
  2. Interpersonal
  3. Mass
  4. Group
Answer: B
Why: It involves two parties.
Q2 MCQ A defamatory spoken remark is called:
  1. Libel
  2. Slander
  3. Plagiarism
  4. Soliloquy
Answer: B
Why: Written = libel; spoken = slander.
Q3 MSQ Select the examples of mass communication.
  1. Newspaper
  2. Television programme
  3. A conversation between two friends
  4. Talking to oneself
Answer: A, B
Why: They reach large audiences through devices.
Q4 T/F In written communication, feedback is usually immediate.
Answer: False
Why: Feedback in writing is delayed.
Q5 Fill Soliloquies and asides are examples of ______ communication.
Answer: intrapersonal
Why: The speaker talks to himself.
Q6 Short Give two advantages of oral over written communication.
Answer: Spontaneity and immediate feedback and clarification.
Why: Voice also conveys emotion.

Revision checklist

  • I can list the five forms by number of participants.
  • I can compare oral and written communication.
  • I know what meta-communication means.
  • I can distinguish libel, slander and plagiarism.
  • I can give cross-cultural examples from the lecture.

Lecture 5: Personality Development

Week 1 · Course book PDF pages 70–83 (printed pages 69–82)

Learning objectives

  • Explain the origin and meaning of ‘personality’ and why outward appearance does not define it.
  • Describe Freud’s three systems of personality: id, ego and superego, and their principles.
  • Explain Alfred Adler’s ideas: social interest, fictional finalism, striving for superiority, inferiority complex and creative self.
  • Compare Freud and Adler and relate personality to workplace behaviour.

Important definitions

Personality

From Latin persona, meaning mask. A set of qualities that make an individual distinct: emotional qualities, attitudes, values, beliefs, motivations, communication skills, ways of thinking and behaviour. Personality is an ‘inside thing’.

Id

The reservoir of instinctive energy; the true psychic reality and matrix of the ego and superego; a world of subjective experience that operates on the pleasure principle and cannot tolerate tension.

Ego

The executive of the personality and the organised portion of the id; follows the reality principle, differentiates memory and image and mediates between instincts and the environment.

Superego

The moral guardian or moral agency; the internal representative of good and evil; strives for perfection rather than pleasure and opposes both id and ego.

Fictional finalism

Adler’s idea that humans are guided by hypotheses or ‘fictions’ that cannot be tested (e.g. ‘honesty is the best policy’, ‘the end justifies the means’) but help them deal with reality.

Inferiority complex / striving for superiority

Every human feels some inferiority and compensates by striving for superiority, i.e. perfection.

Core concepts

What personality is not

Good looks, dress or fluency are only outward features. Shakespeare: “One may smile and smile and be a villain.” Two people who look identical may think very differently.

Why personality matters at work

  • Helps understand the mindset of people from different backgrounds and faiths.
  • Develops flexibility, persuasion, compassion and diplomacy.
  • Builds competence and career success; explains why people react the way they do.

Freud’s three systems

  • Id – pleasure principle; does not distinguish right from wrong; relieves tension through reflex action (sneezing, blinking) and primary process / wish fulfilment (e.g. a hungry person imagines food; repressed wishes appear in dreams – Interpretation of Dreams).
  • Ego – reality principle; asks whether an experience is true or false; finds realistic ways (how to get food); mediates between id and environment.
  • Superego – moral guardian; ideal rather than real; persuades the ego to substitute moralistic goals for realistic goals; inhibits impulses.

Every action passes through these three layers. “The child is the father of the man”: childhood habits and social influences continue; thwarted psychological growth can lead to introversion, pessimism or depression.

Alfred Adler’s theory (opposed Freud)

  • Humans are primarily social creatures, not sexual ones; social interest motivates behaviour.
  • Personality is shaped by expectations of the future, not only past experience.
  • Fictional finalism guides conduct.
  • Striving for superiority = striving for perfection; the inferiority complex drives compensation.
  • Humans identify with others (Freud’s term for imitation).
  • Each person has a unique style of life; “Le style, c’est l’homme” – the style is the man.
  • Masculine protest: both men and women seek power.
  • People can become a creative self and socially useful.
  • Example: Mariyappan Thangavelu, Paralympic gold medallist, compensated for a damaged leg. Adler himself was a sickly child who became a doctor.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Freud’s three systems of personality
SystemPrincipleNatureFunction
IdPleasure principlePsychological (psychic) force; subjectiveWants immediate satisfaction; reflex and wish fulfilment
EgoReality principleBiological; executive of personalityRealistic thinking; mediates between id and surroundings
SuperegoPerfection / moralityMoralistic force; moral guardianOpposes id and ego; seeks ideal and right

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Freud’s psychoanalytic structure of personality — Sigmund Freud

Personality = id + ego + superego. Behaviour results from their interaction, often at an unconscious level.

Individual psychology — Alfred Adler

Social interest, fictional finalism, striving for superiority, creative self.

Examples and applications

Example

A hungry person imagines food (id, wish fulfilment); the ego plans how to get food; the superego checks whether it is morally right.

Example

A gruff person may hide great kindness; a smiling person may be cruel.

Example

Mariyappan Thangavelu turned a physical weakness into Paralympic success.

Comparison tables

Freud versus Adler
PointFreudAdler
Human natureDriven by instincts (sexual); id, ego, superegoSocial creature; social interest
Time orientationPast experience and childhoodExpectations of the future
GoalPleasure and relief of tensionPerfection / superiority
Key termsId, ego, superego, wish fulfilment, identificationFictional finalism, inferiority complex, creative self
Id, ego and superego
IdEgoSuperego
Pleasure principleReality principlePerfection (moral) principle
Pleasurable or painful?True or false?Right or wrong?

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Persona = mask.
  • Id: pleasure principle, true psychic reality, matrix of ego and superego.
  • Ego: executive of the personality and organised portion of the id; reality principle.
  • Superego: strives for perfection rather than pleasure; opposes both id and ego; moral guardian.
  • Id relieves tension by reflex action and wish fulfilment.
  • Freud’s book: Interpretation of Dreams.
  • Adler: fictional finalism, striving for superiority, creative self, social interest.
  • “The child is the father of the man”; “Le style, c’est l’homme.”
  • Shakespeare: one may smile and smile and be a villain.

W1 Q15p.5Thinkers and theories of personalityMatch the columns

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Personality (from persona, mask) is the inner set of qualities, attitudes, values and behaviours that make people distinct. Freud explained it through the id (pleasure), ego (reality) and superego (perfection). Adler, opposing Freud, stressed social interest, fictional finalism and striving for superiority, with the possibility of becoming a creative self.

Lecture 6: Positive Thinking

Week 2 · Course book PDF pages 84–99 (printed pages 83–98)

Learning objectives

  • Define positive thinking and distinguish optimists from pessimists.
  • Explain Eric Berne’s four life positions (I’m OK, you’re OK, etc.).
  • Describe the Johari Window and its four areas, and how each area can be expanded.
  • State ways of developing positivism using examples from the lecture.

Important definitions

Positive thinking

Norman Vincent Peale (The Power of Positive Thinking): a positive mental attitude that firmly believes things will soon be better and so overcomes difficulty.

Optimist / pessimist

An optimist looks at the bright side (glass half full) and creates opportunities; a pessimist looks at the dark side (glass half empty) and finds negativity in every opportunity.

Johari Window

A model devised by American psychologists Joseph Luft and Harry Ingham in 1955 to illustrate and improve self-awareness and mutual understanding in a group through disclosure and feedback.

Open area

Known to the person and known to others; it can be expanded through open, positive communication, feedback and interaction.

Blind area

Not known to the person but known to others; reduced through non-judgmental feedback.

Hidden area

Known to the person but kept hidden from others; reduced through self-disclosure, which builds trust.

Unknown area

Unknown to the person and unknown to others (latent talents); uncovered through mutual or collective discovery.

Core concepts

Why positive thinking helps

  • A positive person believes ‘I can do it’ and so makes a good leader, entrepreneur and risk-taker.
  • One who never takes risks never gains.
  • William James: “We are only half awake” – we use only a small part of our physical and mental resources; discover your limits and hidden powers.
  • A new player who faces the game positively can win.

Eric Berne’s attitudes (life positions)

  • I’m not OK, you’re not OK – introvert; cannot proceed.
  • I’m not OK, you’re OK – negative.
  • I’m OK, you’re not OK – imposing (only he matters).
  • I’m OK, you’re OK – positive and co-operative; ideal for teamwork.

How to develop positivism

  • Believe in your abilities and be yourself; you have immense untapped potential.
  • Do not blindly imitate others; imitate only qualities that help you, without losing your own identity (story of the singer who hid her teeth).
  • Concentrate on your goals and follow the right action; Gita: “Karmanye vadhikaraste ma phaleshu kadachana”.
  • People change: Roosevelt was physically weak; Demosthenes stuttered yet became a great orator.
  • Dhammapada: “All that we are is the result of what we have thought.”

Johari Window in groups

When a new person enters a group, not all of his skills are known to others or to himself. Group members should help him expose them. The window has four quadrants (areas). Feedback and self-disclosure move information into the open area; the open area grows both horizontally and vertically. Managers can promote honest, non-judgmental feedback. Better self-awareness improves communication, relationships and productivity.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Johari Window quadrants
QuadrantKnown to self?Known to others?How to expand / reduce
1. Open (arena)YesYesOpen positive communication, feedback, solicitation
2. BlindNoYesNon-judgmental feedback from managers/members
3. HiddenYesNoSelf-disclosure; reduce hidden area to build trust
4. UnknownNoNoMutual discovery, new experiences
Berne’s life positions
AttitudeType of person
I’m not OK, you’re not OKIntrovert
I’m not OK, you’re OKNegative
I’m OK, you’re not OKImposing
I’m OK, you’re OKPositive and co-operative

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Transactional analysis attitudes — Dr. Eric Berne

Four conclusions about human attitudes, ranging from ‘I’m not OK, you’re not OK’ to the healthy ‘I’m OK, you’re OK’.

Johari Window — Joseph Luft and Harry Ingham (1955)

The name combines the first names Joe and Harry. Four areas: open, blind, hidden, unknown.

Positive mental attitude — Norman Vincent Peale

Author of The Power of Positive Thinking.

Examples and applications

Example

Half-full versus half-empty glass of water.

Example

A singer who hid her teeth was told to rely on her voice and talent.

Example

Demosthenes’ stutter and Roosevelt’s weakness were overcome through practice.

Example

Latent talent: someone discovers a good singing voice only when a situation draws it out (unknown area).

Comparison tables

Optimist versus pessimist
PointOptimistPessimist
Glass of waterHalf fullHalf empty
ViewBright side; creates opportunityDark side; negativity in every opportunity

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Glass half empty = pessimist; half full = optimist.
  • Peale wrote The Power of Positive Thinking.
  • William James: we are only half awake.
  • ‘I’m OK, you’re OK’ = positive and co-operative; ‘I’m OK, you’re not OK’ = imposing; ‘I’m not OK, you’re not OK’ = introvert; ‘I’m not OK, you’re OK’ = negative.
  • Johari Window: Luft and Ingham, 1955; four areas: open, blind, hidden, unknown.
  • Blind area = unknown to self but known by others.
  • Ways to develop positivism: believe in your abilities; concentrate on your goals.
  • Gita quotation: Karmanye vadhikaraste ma phaleshu kadachana.
  • Dhammapada: All that we are is the result of what we have thought.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Positive thinking is the belief that one can succeed; optimists create opportunities while pessimists see obstacles. Eric Berne’s four attitudes show why ‘I’m OK, you’re OK’ suits teamwork. The Johari Window (Luft and Ingham, 1955) uses four areas – open, blind, hidden, unknown – to show how feedback and disclosure increase self-awareness. Positivism is built by believing in one’s abilities, not imitating blindly and pursuing one’s goals.

  2. Blind area
  3. Hidden area

Lecture 7: Telephonic Communication Skills Part I

Week 2 · Course book PDF pages 100–114 (printed pages 99–113)

Learning objectives

  • Explain the importance of telephonic communication in life and business.
  • List the special features of telephonic communication: clarity, courtesy, consideration, brevity.
  • Apply the etiquette of making, receiving, transferring and closing calls.
  • Handle complaints and voicemail professionally and avoid negative language.

Important definitions

Telephonic communication

Sending and receiving messages through telephone instruments; the fastest and a low-cost means of contact across distance.

Netiquette versus etiquette

Netiquette is etiquette for internet communication; the lecturer notes that we forget basic etiquettes in the age of netiquettes.

Voicemail

A recorded message left when the call cannot be completed; it should be complete.

Core concepts

Why telephones matter

  • Bridge distance and time; fast, saves travel, cost-effective.
  • Used to place orders, book tickets, give feedback, check quality, contact students, sellers and distributors.
  • The reputation of an organisation depends on how its people communicate by phone.

Limitations

No face-to-face contact and no immediate visual feedback; the only cue is the voice. Video-calls are costly and not for everyone. Voice must carry assurance, sincerity, intimacy and enthusiasm.

Features of telephonic communication

  1. Clarity – the first requirement.
  2. Courtesy – right time, right address and tone; you cannot see the other person.
  3. Consideration – empathy; the other party may be in a noisy or congested place; do not make them repeatedly ask “can you be loud?”.
  4. Brevity – be brief and specific because someone is paying and time is limited.

While making a call

  • Decide whether the call is necessary; if it can be avoided, avoid it.
  • Place your own call instead of asking a secretary.
  • Plan an agenda (purpose; keep pen and paper) to avoid repeated calls.
  • Identify yourself and your affiliation (name, department, institute) and state the purpose.
  • Address the person by name; avoid small talk such as “How is the weather?” in official calls.
  • Speak clearly at a moderate volume; imagine you are speaking face to face.
  • Close politely (“It was nice talking to you…”); never end abruptly.
  • If the call is cut or you must stop, say you will call back, and call back when promised; leave a complete voicemail if needed.

While receiving a call

  • Answer immediately, not later than three rings.
  • Stop other conversation; identify yourself at once (“I am … from …, how may I help you?”).
  • Respond positively, even if you dislike the caller; “good gestures always mean good business”.
  • Take the message carefully: note caller’s name (verify spelling), number and message and pass it on.
  • To transfer a call, explain why and ask consent (“May I put you on hold?”).
  • For an angry complainant: stay calm, listen, note the concern, assure a response, and let the caller hang up first.

Language to use and avoid

  • Avoid negatives such as “No, he is not here”, “I cannot do that”, “You have to…”, “Why did you not…?”.
  • Use “Tell me what I can do for you”, “I would like you to…”, “Will/would you please…”.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Four features of telephonic communication
FeatureMeaning
ClarityClear message and speech, whether giving or seeking information
CourtesyRight time, right address, proper orientation without seeing the person
ConsiderationEmpathy for the other party’s surroundings and state of mind
BrevityShort and specific because of cost and the other person’s time

Examples and applications

Example

Opening a call: “Binod Mishra, Department of HSS, IIT Roorkee. May I speak to Mr X? The purpose of this call is…”

Example

Closing a call: “It was nice talking to you, Mrs D’Souza. I have got the required information.”

Example

Receiving: “I am Jeethendra from NPTEL, how may I help you?”

Comparison tables

Making a call versus receiving a call
Making a callReceiving a call
Plan an agenda, place own callAnswer within three rings
Identify yourself and purposeIdentify yourself immediately
Avoid small talk; be briefRespond positively; take message accurately
Close courteously; call back if promisedTransfer only with consent; let the caller hang up

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Four features: clarity, courtesy, consideration, brevity.
  • Clarity is the first requirement of telephonic communication.
  • Answer a call within three rings.
  • Place your own call and plan an agenda.
  • Identify yourself with name and affiliation when calling and when receiving.
  • Avoid small talk and negative words such as ‘No’ or ‘You have to’.
  • Voice conveys reassurance, intimacy, sincerity; there is no immediate visual feedback.
  • Telephone calls are fast and low cost, bridging distance.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W2 Q6p.8Features of telephonic communicationMCQ

W2 Q13p.9Importance of telephonic communicationMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Telephonic communication bridges time and distance quickly and at low cost but lacks visual feedback, so clarity, courtesy, consideration and brevity are essential. A caller should plan an agenda, place his own call, identify himself, state the purpose and close politely; a receiver should answer within three rings, identify himself, respond positively and take accurate messages. Negative language is avoided.

  1. One minute
  2. Ten rings
  3. Three rings
  4. Whenever convenient

Q3 MSQ Select the guidelines for sending a call.
  1. Place your own call
  2. Plan an agenda
  3. Avoid identifying yourself

Answer: A, B

Q4 T/F In an official call it is good to begin with small talk about the weather.
Answer: False

Revision checklist

  • I can list the four features of telephonic communication.
  • I know the steps for making and receiving a call.
  • I can rephrase negative sentences positively.
  • I know the three-ring rule.
  • I can describe how to take messages and transfer calls.

Lecture 8: Telephonic Communication Skills Part II

Week 2 · Course book PDF pages 115–126 (printed pages 114–125)

Learning objectives

  • Explain voice mail, its advantages and limits, and how to leave and answer a voice message.
  • State mobile (cell phone) etiquette.
  • Differentiate teleconferencing (audio) and video conferencing, including the three types of video conferencing.
  • Know when teleconferencing is ineffective and the cautions for video conferencing.

Important definitions

Voice mail

A recorded-message facility that acts as a substitute for short memos and short phone calls, solves time-zone difficulties and reduces paperwork; messages are digitised and stored.

Automated attendant

A menu system (e.g. “for account balance press 1”) through which callers get information or record messages.

Teleconferencing

A telephonic meeting with more than two people, requiring a leader or facilitator; an alternative to a face-to-face meeting. Can be audio or video.

Video conferencing

Audio plus video link so that communication appears face to face; a tool of business and management for people separated by time and distance.

Core concepts

Voice mail: use and etiquette

  • Advantages: substitute for short memos and phone calls; solves time-zone difficulties; reduces interoffice paperwork (paperless office); easy and low cost; can be replayed; can carry goodwill messages.
  • Limitation: no immediate feedback; automated responses are general, not specific.
  • When leaving a message: state your name clearly, give the point of the message, your phone number and the time/date you are available; avoid interrupting.
  • When replying: reply quickly; listen again and write your response before returning the call.

Cell phone etiquette

  • Speak in a low, modulated tone; do not shout (the Delhi bus example).
  • Switch off or use silent mode in meetings, conferences, theatres, cinemas, temples, churches and classrooms; send a short message if a call is urgent.
  • Do not call at odd hours unless urgent.
  • Avoid talking in poor signal; suspend and resume later.
  • Do not multitask (laptop plus call plus instructions) at the cost of courtesy; dial correctly to avoid wrong numbers.
  • Phones are sensitive, so privacy is limited; keep private calls separate from official ones.

Teleconferencing

  • Needs a leader/facilitator; can be arranged at short notice; connects geographically dispersed people; saves travel cost.
  • Audio conferences give good sound quality when conference rooms and microphones are used.
  • Ineffective for negotiations and decision-making because facial expressions and gestures cannot be read; little scope for secondary conversation; poor signals can defeat the purpose.

Video conferencing

  • Emerging major tool for business and management; cheaper than journeys; top executives use it for decisions.
  • Three types: one-way video and one-way audio; two-way audio with one-way video; two-way video and two-way audio (e.g. Skype, news-channel discussions).
  • Cautions: check microphones and camera; introduce yourself (name, post, affiliation); control body movements; avoid parallel whispers; look at the camera; avoid distracting clothes and excessive movement.

Man is the master

“Technology should not become the master; man should become the master.” Be sensitive to others’ reactions, culture, time and energy.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Types of video conferencing (as described)
TypeDescription
One-way video and one-way audioAudio and video flow from one side to the other
Two-way audio with video one sideBoth sides talk; one side sees
Two-way video and two-way audioBoth sides see and hear each other (Skype-like)

Examples and applications

Example

Automated bank menu: “Welcome to SBI Credit Card… for account balance press 1.”

Example

A passenger shouting instructions on a bus journey to Delhi.

Example

News channel anchors connecting people at several locations through video conferencing.

Comparison tables

Audio teleconferencing versus video conferencing
PointTeleconferencing (audio)Video conferencing
MediumVoice onlyVoice plus video
CuesCannot read facial reactions or gesturesBody language visible; must control movements
ArrangementCan be arranged at very short noticeNeeds equipment; costlier but cheaper than travel
UseWeak for negotiationsUsed for decisions by busy executives

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Voice mail substitutes for short memos and phone calls and solves time-zone difficulties.
  • Voice mail lacks immediate, specific feedback.
  • Teleconferencing is ineffective for negotiations.
  • Teleconferencing can be arranged at short notice and connects dispersed people.
  • Three types of video conferencing (one-way, mixed, two-way).
  • Speak in a low, modulated tone on cell phones; silent mode in meetings and worship places.
  • Do not call at odd hours unless urgent.
  • Teleconferencing needs a leader or facilitator.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W2 Q7p.8TeleconferencingMCQ
W2 Q9p.8Voice mailFill-in-the-blank
W2 Q12p.9Voice mailMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Voice mail replaces short memos, overcomes time zones and cuts paperwork but gives no immediate feedback. Mobile phone etiquette stresses low voice, silence in public places and no odd-hour calls. Teleconferencing connects dispersed people at short notice but is poor for negotiation, while video conferencing adds visuals and has three types; users must manage equipment, speech and body language.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ Teleconferencing is generally ineffective for:
  1. Saving travel costs
  2. Negotiations
  3. Arranging meetings at short notice
  4. Connecting dispersed people
Answer: B
Why: Facial reactions and non-verbal cues cannot be read.
Q2 MCQ Voice mail helps solve ______ difficulties.
  1. Spelling
  2. Time zone
  3. Language
  4. Dress code
Answer: B
Why: Messages can be left when the person cannot be reached.
Q3 MSQ Select correct statements about voice mail.
  1. It substitutes short memos and calls
  2. It solves time-zone problems
  3. It increases paperwork
  4. It gives immediate specific feedback
Answer: A, B
Why: It reduces paperwork and lacks immediate feedback.
Q4 T/F In a video conference you should look at the camera and avoid parallel whispers.
Answer: True
Why: These are listed cautions.
Q5 Fill On a cell phone one should speak in a ______ modulated voice.
Answer: low
Why: Loud calls disturb others.
Q6 Short What should a voice mail message include?
Answer: Name, point of the message, phone number and time/date of availability.
Why: Keep it focused on the essentials.

Revision checklist

  • I can state advantages and limits of voice mail.
  • I know cell phone etiquette.
  • I can contrast teleconferencing and video conferencing.
  • I know the three types of video conferencing.
  • I remember why teleconferencing fails for negotiation.

Lecture 9: Communicating Without Words

Week 2 · Course book PDF pages 127–147 (printed pages 126–146)

Learning objectives

  • Define non-verbal communication and explain why it matters.
  • Quote the Birdwhistell and Mehrabian percentages.
  • List the types of non-verbal communication: kinesics, proxemics, chronemics, paralanguage, haptics, meta-communication.
  • Describe the forms of kinesics: personal appearance, facial expression, eye contact, movement, posture and gesture, with cultural differences.

Important definitions

Non-verbal communication

Communication without words, through symbols and cues such as expression, gesture, posture and eye behaviour. It is spontaneous and unintentional, and it supplements and complements verbal communication.

Kinesics

The study of body movement or body language; includes personal appearance, facial expression, eye contact, movement, posture and gesture.

Proxemics

Use of space and distance in communication (from proximity).

Chronemics

The role of time in communication.

Paralanguage

How words are spoken, beyond the words themselves (voice qualities).

Haptics

Communication through touch.

Posture

Positioning: how you sit, stand and hold your arms and shoulders.

Core concepts

Verbal and non-verbal proportions

  • Ray Birdwhistell: 35% of communication is verbal and 65% non-verbal.
  • Mehrabian: 7% verbal (words), 38% vocal, and the remaining (55%) non-verbal.
  • One study counts more than 6 lakh non-verbal cues, so they are categorised.

Nature of non-verbal cues

  • Cannot substitute words (e.g. ‘happiness’, ‘silence’) but have more impact because they are spontaneous.
  • They are unintentional and unstructured; posed expressions become stereotyped (as with actors).
  • Work at two levels: the verbal level (words) and the non-verbal level.
  • People are communicating all the time; even silence has meaning (differs across cultures).
  • Symbols and emoticons in chat and email are non-verbal cues.

Types of non-verbal communication

  1. Kinesics – body language
  2. Proxemics – space
  3. Chronemics – time
  4. Paralanguage – voice
  5. Haptics – touch
  6. Meta-communication

Kinesics: sign language (posed, stereotyped), action language (body behaviour) and object language.

Personal appearance and face

  • The face is a junction of emotions; ‘face is the index of the mind’, but ‘a false face must have a false heart’ when one manipulates.
  • Liars find it hard to hide body language (child with clamped hands in pockets).
  • Dress should be clean, ironed and suitable; it creates the first impression. It need not be expensive.
  • Freud: “He who has eyes to see and ears to hear can convince himself that no mortal can keep a secret.”
  • Chesterton: “There are no uninteresting topics, there are uninteresting people.”

Eye contact

  • Eyes are ‘the mirror of the heart and windows of the soul’; they show sincerity, honesty and conviction and establish rapport and credibility.
  • Culture-specific: Arabs, Latin Americans and southern Europeans gaze at the face; Asians, Indians, Pakistanis and northern Europeans prefer peripheral gaze or none (Harper, Wiens and Matarazzo).
  • Eye contact should be neither a gaze nor a stare.
  • Herodotus: “Men trust their ears less than their eyes.”
  • Glowering looks and checking watches show the audience is bored.

Movement and posture

  • Do not rush; avoid excessive or distracting movement; move gracefully.
  • Dale Carnegie: people form an impression from how you dress, walk, sit and stand.
  • Posture: do not recline; avoid hands in pockets, clamped hands, playing with key rings, shuffling.

Gestures

  • Gestures back up language; do not repeat the same gesture (e.g. thumping the desk).
  • Types: enumerative and symbolic gestures.
  • Limp hands – boredom; flat hands – silent questions; open hands – welcome; clenched hands – tension/frustration; fist – determination; crossed arms – negative; crossed legs – insulting to the audience.
  • Handshakes: glove handshake (politician); dead-fish handshake (formal/unwilling).
  • Gestures differ by culture: ‘come here’ in the US may mean ‘go away’ in Italy.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Types of non-verbal communication
TypeConcerned with
KinesicsBody movement
ProxemicsSpace and distance
ChronemicsTime
ParalanguageHow words are spoken
HapticsTouch
Meta-communicationMeaning beyond words
Forms of kinesics
FormNotes
Personal appearanceDress, grooming; first impression
Facial expressionJunction of emotions
Eye contactRapport, credibility; culture-specific
Movement / postureGraceful, not excessive; alert positioning
GestureSupports speech; meanings vary across cultures

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Verbal versus non-verbal share — Ray Birdwhistell

35% verbal, 65% non-verbal.

7-38-55 split — Mehrabian

7% verbal, 38% vocal, remainder non-verbal.

Three languages of kinesics — Author cited in lecture

Sign language, action language and object language.

Examples and applications

Example

A mute beggar’s face conveyed happiness on receiving a coin.

Example

A child who lies hides clamped hands in his pockets.

Example

‘Come here’ gesture in the US means ‘go away’ in Italy.

Example

A speaker whose audience keeps checking watches should change delivery.

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip

  • Mehrabian: 7% verbal, 38% vocal, rest non-verbal.
  • More than 6 lakh non-verbal cues.
  • Non-verbal communication is spontaneous and unintentional.
  • Kinesics = body language; proxemics = space; chronemics = time; haptics = touch.
  • Kinesics forms: appearance, facial expression, eye contact, posture, gesture, movement.
  • Eye contact: Arabs, Latin Americans and southern Europeans gaze; Asians prefer peripheral gaze.
  • Herodotus: men trust their ears less than their eyes.
  • Dale Carnegie: people judge how you dress, walk, sit and stand.
  • Glove handshake: politician; dead-fish handshake: formal or unwilling.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Non-verbal communication complements words and is spontaneous and unintentional. Birdwhistell put it at 65% and Mehrabian at 55% with 38% vocal. Its categories are kinesics, proxemics, chronemics, paralanguage, haptics and meta-communication. Kinesics covers appearance, face, eyes, movement, posture and gestures, all of which vary by culture and must be controlled to create a favourable impression.

Lecture 10: Paralanguage

Week 2 · Course book PDF pages 148–167 (printed pages 147–166)

Learning objectives

  • Define paralanguage and list the features of voice: quality, volume, rate, pitch, pronunciation, pauses and silence.
  • Identify voice faults: throatiness, breathiness, nasalisation, harshness, muffling and hoarseness.
  • State the ideal speaking rate and how to improve articulation.
  • Explain intonation, stress, pauses, silence and the quotations of Mark Twain and Pythagoras.

Important definitions

Paralanguage

The study of how words are spoken: the vocal characteristics (quality, volume, rate, pitch, pronunciation, pauses) that add meaning beyond the words.

Articulation

Producing speech sounds clearly through the speech organs; clarity is the hallmark of communication.

Audibility

The ability of the listener to hear the speaker; related to volume.

Intonation

The rises and falls in the voice.

Inflection

Changes in pitch produced by vibration of the vocal cords while speaking; they convey feelings.

Pause

A short silence in speech that separates units of thought; a longer pause becomes silence.

Received Pronunciation (RP)

The standard pronunciation recommended for clear, intelligible speech instead of imitating Americans or Britons.

Core concepts

Why voice matters

  • Everyone’s voice is unique and carries intentional and unintentional emotions.
  • The quality of voice can be improved by training (Lincoln and Churchill had voice problems).
  • Speech production passes through stages starting with the articulatory–respiratory process using lips, teeth, nasal passage, windpipe.
  • English as a second or third language causes translation lapses and pronunciation problems.

Features of voice

  1. Quality – check audibility; neither too loud nor too slow.
  2. Volume – degree of loudness; adjust to audience size and room acoustics (no shouting for 20–30 people).
  3. Rate – adjust to the audience’s background.
  4. Pitch – variations and inflections.
  5. Pronunciation.
  6. Pauses and silence.

Rate of speech

  • Ideal average: 140–150 words per minute (the lecturer also advises at least 130–150).
  • Too fast: you forget and run out of material; too slow: boredom. Follow the ‘madhyam marga’ (middle path).
  • Imagine an audience of ordinary, moderately educated people.

Faults of voice

  • Throatiness – deep resonance in the throat.
  • Breathiness – too much air through the vocal cords.
  • Nasalisation – air released through the nose.
  • Harshness – uninhibited flow of air.
  • Muffling – lazy lip and jaw movement.
  • Hoarseness – inflammation of the larynx; a medical problem, consult a speech therapist.

Improving the voice

  • Speak distinctly; avoid chopping, slurring and mumbling (‘gonna’, ‘wanna’, ‘dunno’, ‘otta’, ‘dint’) and fillers (ah, um).
  • Record yourself on a mobile phone and rehearse.
  • Use a dictionary, thesaurus and phonetic notation for difficult words.
  • Follow Received Pronunciation; learn from good news anchors; listen actively.
  • Vary pitch to avoid monotony; use intonation.

Stress and intonation

  • Content words (nouns, adjectives, verbs, adverbs) take stress; articles, prepositions, conjunctions, auxiliary verbs and personal pronouns usually do not.
  • Stress the correct syllable (use a dictionary).
  • Falling tone for commands and wh-questions (‘Bring me a cup of tea’, ‘Where had you been?’).
  • Rising tone for requests and yes/no questions and question tags.
  • Modulate voice to express intended meaning (the late student seeking permission).

Pauses and silence

  • Pauses separate units of thought; begin and end a speech with a pause.
  • Mark Twain: “The right word may be effective, but no word was ever as effective as a rightly timed pause.”
  • A longer pause becomes silence; silence is a non-verbal cue with different meanings across cultures (approval, disapproval, disagreement).
  • Silence helps thinking, introspection and decision-making for both speaker and listener.
  • Pythagoras: “Silence is better than unmeaning words.”

Types, classifications and characteristics

Voice faults
FaultCause
ThroatinessDeep resonance in the throat
BreathinessToo much air through vocal cords
NasalisationAir released through the nose
HarshnessUninhibited air flow
MufflingLazy lips and jaw
HoarsenessInflammation of the larynx (medical)
Features of voice
FeatureMeaning
QualityOverall character and audibility
VolumeLoudness or softness
RateSpeed (140–150 wpm)
PitchRise and fall; inflection

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Mark Twain on the pause — Mark Twain

“The right word may be effective, but no word was ever as effective as a rightly timed pause.”

Silence is better than unmeaning words — Pythagoras

Quoted at the close of the lecture.

Examples and applications

Example

Lincoln and Churchill overcame voice problems through training.

Example

Late student: “May I come in, sir, though I am late, sir?” shows intended meaning through voice.

Example

BBC English, Hindi and Bangla services as models of pleasant voices.

Example

Falling tone: “Where had you been?”; rising tone: “Is it time to wind up, isn’t it?”

Comparison tables

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Ideal rate is about 140–150 words per minute.
  • Pitch changes are called inflections; rises and falls in the voice are intonation.
  • Voice faults: throatiness, breathiness, nasalisation, harshness, muffling, hoarseness.
  • Hoarseness results from inflammation of the larynx; consult a speech therapist.
  • Articles, prepositions, conjunctions, auxiliaries and personal pronouns do not take stress.
  • Mark Twain on the rightly timed pause.
  • Pythagoras: Silence is better than unmeaning words.
  • Clarity is the hallmark of communication.
  • Follow Received Pronunciation rather than imitating Americans or British.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Paralanguage covers voice quality, volume, rate, pitch, pronunciation and pauses. Speakers should speak at about 140–150 words per minute, vary pitch to avoid monotony, articulate each sound distinctly, follow Received Pronunciation, stress content words and use pauses and silence intentionally. Voice faults such as throatiness, breathiness, nasalisation, harshness, muffling and hoarseness can be reduced by training, except hoarseness, which is medical.

Week 3: Space, Touch, Meta-communication and Listening

Lecture 11: Proxemics

Week 3 · Course book PDF pages 168–179 (printed pages 167–178)

Learning objectives

  • Define proxemics and chronemics.
  • State the four space zones with distances and who may enter each.
  • Relate space and time to status, power and culture; distinguish high-context and low-context cultures.
  • Recall examples and quotations from the lecture.

Important definitions

Proxemics

The study of physical space in communication; from proximity (nearness). It is called relationship communication because it reflects behavioural norms.

Chronemics

The study of time in communication: how time is perceived, used and valued across cultures and professions.

Air bubble

The personal space every human (and animal) needs around the body.

Low-context culture

A culture that relies on written, explicit messages and attaches less importance to non-verbal cues.

High-context culture

A culture that derives meaning from non-verbal cues and context and prefers short written messages.

Core concepts

Why space matters

  • Space shows warmth, need for freedom, friendliness and power.
  • Freed animals feel relaxed; humans too need breathing space.
  • Violation of space makes people feel threatened.
  • Mehrabian: “Status manifests itself by relaxed posture and ways of interacting.”

Four space zones

  1. Intimate zone – up to 18 inches; family, close relatives, intimate people. Touch is allowed.
  2. Personal zone – 18 inches to 4 feet; friends, colleagues, peers.
  3. Social zone – 4 to 12 feet; strangers and occasional visitors such as plumbers, electricians, milkmen.
  4. Public zone – 12 to 30 feet or more; public speakers and leaders addressing audiences; feedback is hard to read.

Many Europeans keep 15–25 cm in the intimate zone. A person allowed one zone must not enter an inner zone.

Space, status and power

  • Bosses have large rooms or corner offices; employees have smaller cabins; furniture signals status.
  • In the US non-verbal communication at work reinforces power; space denotes control and dominance.
  • Going very close to a boss makes him feel threatened.

Cultural examples

  • Japanese hosts gave a US guest space by sitting with the driver in the front seat; the guest felt lonely and embarrassed. Later British students sat away from him: “you and I are close friends”.
  • Arabs of the same sex stand closer than North Americans; Indians often stand close.
  • A Danish couple violated American space at an American club.
  • An American and a Japanese adjusted distances like dancing: the Japanese moved close and the American stepped back.
  • A violation of space at a political rally led to a tragic incident.

Low- versus high-context cultures

  • Low context: North America, Germany, England, France, Italy, Scandinavia – put things in writing; ignore non-verbal cues; Americans write long emails.
  • High context: Japan, China, Arab countries, Greece, Mexico, Spain – read non-verbal cues; write short messages.
  • Americans may find the French withholding; the French may find Americans dominant.

Chronemics

  • Time is a commodity; punctuality earns respect and reveals personality and attitude.
  • Missing deadlines suggests insincerity.
  • Latin American and Mexican meetings may start late; the UK expects punctuality; India talks about time but is often late.
  • Robert E. Hall: “Time talks and space speaks.”
  • Plan a presentation by time: do not let the introduction be long; give beginning, development and end proper time.
  • Leaders decide how much time to give to whom; this shows the importance they give.
  • Gather facts about the other culture’s view of space and time to avoid miscommunication.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Space zones
ZoneDistanceWho is allowed
IntimateUp to 18 inchesFamily, close relatives, intimates
Personal18 inches – 4 feetFriends, colleagues, peers
Social4 – 12 feetStrangers, occasional visitors, service people
Public12 – 30 feet or morePublic speakers, leaders, audiences

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Time talks and space speaks — Robert E. Hall

Time and space are non-verbal cues that communicate.

Status and posture — Mehrabian

“Status manifests itself by relaxed posture and ways of interacting.”

Examples and applications

Example

Japanese escorts seated in front with the driver, leaving the US guest alone in the back.

Example

Danish couple at an American club violating American space.

Example

Latin American meetings starting late after chat; British punctuality.

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip

  • Proxemics derives from proximity.
  • Robert E. Hall: Time talks and space speaks.
  • Low-context: North America, Germany, England, France, Italy, Scandinavia.
  • High-context: Japan, China, Arab countries, Greece, Mexico, Spain.
  • Bosses occupy larger or corner spaces.
  • Plumbers, electricians and milkmen belong in the social zone.
  • Arabs stand closer than North Americans.
  • Meetings in Latin America often begin late.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Proxemics studies how space communicates. Four zones – intimate (to 18 in), personal (to 4 ft), social (4–12 ft), public (12–30 ft+) – are linked to relationship and status, and vary with culture. High-context cultures read non-verbal cues while low-context cultures rely on written words. Chronemics treats time as a commodity; punctuality and timing show professionalism, and ‘time talks and space speaks’.
