Lecture 12: Haptics: The Language of Touch

Week 3 · Course book PDF pages 180–192 (printed pages 179–191)

Learning objectives

  • Define haptics and its Greek origin.
  • Explain meanings of touch: reassurance, warmth, regard, blessing, and how they vary by culture and setting.
  • Describe types of handshakes (dominant, submissive, equal, dead fish, glove) and palm/finger gestures.
  • Use cultural examples: Thailand, Spain, New Zealand hongi, India, Japan.

Important definitions

Haptics

The systematic study of touch behaviour in communication; from a Greek word meaning ‘I touch’.

Dead fish handshake

A limp, lifeless handshake showing lack of enthusiasm or encouragement; it may signal that a deal or negotiation will not go well.

Dominant handshake

A handshake where one party takes control by pressing the other’s hand (palm on top) or gripping only the fingers; shows power.

Submissive handshake

A handshake in which one offers an open hand with the palm up, giving control to the other.

Hongi

A New Zealand greeting in which two people touch noses, treated as sharing breath.

Enumerative and locative gestures

Gestures that count points or point to locate an object.

Core concepts

Meanings of touch

  • Touch generates feelings: warmth, friendliness, regard, togetherness, affection, reassurance (mother and child).
  • In professional settings it may have a negative impact; a pat on the back may encourage a younger person but may be unwelcome from a stranger.
  • Touching elders’ feet in India shows regard and brings blessings.
  • Greetings: hugging, folding hands (namaste), kissing the forehead (Latin America), handshakes.
  • Mulk Raj Anand’s Untouchable: the protagonist Bakha announces “an untouchable is coming” — touch once reflected class and caste.

Cultural differences

  • India: handshake or folded hands; restrictions between men and women.
  • US and UK: handshake is the commonest form; it troubles Indian women unused to shaking hands with the opposite sex.
  • Spain: a handshake should last at least seven strokes; withdrawing may be seen as rejection.
  • Arab and Asian countries: handshakes restricted.
  • Thailand and Laos: touching a child on the head is rude because the head is sacred and holds the spirit.
  • Japan: a woman covers her mouth while smiling; public display of feelings is impolite. Germans smile less.
  • New Zealand: hongi.
  • A senior’s pat on a female junior’s back and a forehead kiss led to debates and a court case.
  • Advice: when unsure of local culture, restrict touch.

Handshake types

  • Dominant: hand on top, palm down, or gripping only the fingers; used by those in higher positions.
  • Submissive: palm up, giving control.
  • Equal: hands meet in a shake of equality between peers.
  • Dead fish: lifeless, discouraging.
  • Glove / politician’s: clasping with both hands; friendly show with a hidden meaning.
  • Timing (beginning or end) also carries meaning.

Palms and fingers

  • Flat hands may show depression or nothingness.
  • Open palm = submission; palm on top of another’s hand = dominance.
  • Pointing or raising a finger = aggression and is often unwelcome.
  • Finger-thumb circle = ‘OK’ but may be insulting in some cultures.

Verbal and non-verbal congruence

If a positive verbal message is accompanied by a negative non-verbal cue, there is conflict and the deal may fail. When verbal and non-verbal behaviour blend, the message is positive. Give others more room and respect their cultures.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Handshake types
TypeFeaturesMessage
DominantHand on top; palm down; fingers onlyControl and power
SubmissivePalm up; open handGiving control
EqualHands meet equallyEquality between peers
Dead fishLimp and lifelessLack of interest; poor deal
Glove (politician)Both hands claspCheerful, friendly show

Examples and applications

Example

Bakha in Mulk Raj Anand’s Untouchable warns others of his approach.

Example

Thailand: touching a child’s head is rude.

Example

Spain: seven-stroke handshake.

Example

New Zealand: hongi, touching noses.

Example

Japanese woman covers her mouth when smiling.

Comparison tables

Touch customs across cultures
PlacePractice / meaning
IndiaFolded hands; touching elders’ feet gives respect and brings blessings
US/UKHandshake is the common greeting
SpainHandshake lasts about seven strokes
Thailand/LaosDo not touch a child’s head
New ZealandHongi nose greeting
Latin AmericaKissing forehead

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Haptics comes from a Greek word meaning ‘I touch’.
  • Mulk Raj Anand wrote Untouchable; the protagonist is Bakha.
  • In Spain a handshake should last at least seven strokes.
  • In Thailand and Laos touching a child’s head is rude because the head is sacred.
  • Hongi is the New Zealand nose-touching greeting.
  • Three handshake types: dominant, submissive and equal; also dead fish and glove.
  • Raised or pointed fingers are aggressive.
  • If verbal and non-verbal cues conflict, trust the non-verbal.
  • Restrict touch when unfamiliar with the local culture.

W3 Q2p.11HapticsMCQ
W3 Q8p.12Cultural meanings of touchMCQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Haptics is the language of touch and it varies by culture, gender and setting. Handshakes may express dominance, submission or equality; a dead-fish shake signals disinterest. Gestures with palms and fingers also carry meaning. Because touch can be misread (Thailand’s head taboo, Spanish seven-stroke handshakes), professionals should learn local norms, restrict touch when unsure and ensure verbal and non-verbal messages agree.

Lecture 13: Meta-communication

Week 3 · Course book PDF pages 193–208 (printed pages 192–207)

Learning objectives

  • Define meta-communication (meta = beyond) and explain implied meaning.
  • Show how word choice, tone and positioning change meaning.
  • Explain the uses and advantages of silence and pauses and the disadvantages of non-words (vocalised pauses).
  • Distinguish pauses from silence and recall the quotations (Adrienne Rich, Kennedy).

Important definitions

Meta-communication

Communication beyond words (meta = beyond): the implied meaning carried by choice of words, tone, hesitation, silence and context. It may be intentional or unintentional.

Non-words

Fumbling for words and vocalised pauses (I mean, O.K., ah, um) that mar the flow of speech and suggest doubt or ambiguity.

Vocalised pause

A filler sound or phrase used when one lacks words.

Pause

A short break in speech that separates units of thought; a longer pause becomes silence.

Shared assumptions

Common understanding on which successful communication depends, together with unspoken arguments.

Core concepts

What meta-communication includes

  • Choice of words and why a speaker chooses them in a context.
  • Tone of voice, temper and pitch.
  • Hesitation, fumbling, breaks and silence.
  • Implied meaning: ‘You are looking smart today’ may be praise or sarcasm depending on tone.
  • It may be unintentional.

Positioning of words

  • The same words, differently broken, change meaning: “Leave him, not kill him” versus “Leave him not, kill him”.
  • “Try to reach the venue well in time” may be advice, anxiety or good wishes.
  • A question like “Would you mind having a cup of coffee with me?” is a polite request.

Silence in meta-communication

  • Silence can mean neutrality, impartiality, hiding feelings, non-participation, disagreement, dissonance or disinterest.
  • A boss’s silence can be positive or negative: you must read non-verbal cues.
  • Meaning varies across cultures. Silence can be loud, frightening or rewarding.
  • Success in communication comes from shared assumptions and unspoken arguments.
  • Adrienne Rich: “Lying is done with words and also with silence.”

Advantages of silence

  • Relieves monotony and gives the audience time to digest and revise.
  • Gives the speaker time to think; ignites thoughts.
  • Draws back an inattentive listener.
  • Shows consideration; listeners love speakers who place silence among words.
  • Helps coherence and connection.

Non-words and their harm

  • Fast speakers use vocalised pauses (I mean, O.K., ah, um) which mar the flow and signal doubt, ambiguity or lack of preparation.
  • Fast delivery removes space between clauses and phrases; respect for the speaker falls.
  • Better to be silent than to produce confused noises. Distinguish providing silence from becoming silent.
  • Lengthy silence is disliked; keep it regulated.
  • Written speeches use punctuation to place pauses (comma, semicolon, full stop, dash).
  • The lecture mentions a delivery rate of 125–130 words per minute (earlier, 140–150).

Pauses

  • Brief breaks that divide thoughts, build rapport and goodwill, gain control and feedback.
  • Listeners’ minds move faster than words; pauses let them analyse and digest.
  • Only a confident, well-prepared speaker uses pauses well; actors are trained in them.
  • Opening: assert presence by looking at the audience in silence — a mutual, unspoken agreement.
  • Example: Kennedy’s “Ask not what your country can do for you — ask what you can do for your country” is effective because of the pause.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Uses of silence
UseEffect
Neutrality / hiding reactionAvoids revealing opinion
Non-participation / disagreementSignals dissonance
Relief from monotonyAudience digests ideas
Time to thinkSpeaker generates new ideas
AttentionDraws in distracted listeners

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Lying with silence — Adrienne Rich

“Lying is done with words and also with silence.”

Pause in speech — John F. Kennedy (quoted)

The famous line gains force from a pause.

Examples and applications

Example

“Leave him, not kill him” versus “Leave him not, kill him”.

Example

A friend saying “You are looking damn smart today” may imply you earlier dressed badly.

Example

A boss who stays silent on your request.

Example

A meeting member silent to remain neutral.

Comparison tables

Pause versus silence versus non-words
PauseSilenceNon-words
Short break dividing thoughtsLonger break with meaningFumbling, ah/um, I mean
Confident and plannedIntentional or obligatorySignals doubt and poor preparation
Improves clarityCan relieve, reflect or hideMars flow of speech

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Meta means beyond.
  • Meta-communication may be unintentional.
  • ‘Leave him, not kill him’ example shows the effect of word positioning.
  • Silence can hide meaning or show disagreement.
  • Non-words include ah, um, ‘I mean’, O.K.
  • Adrienne Rich: lying is done with words and also with silence.
  • Pauses are short breaks; longer breaks become silence.
  • Silence ignites thoughts and relieves monotony.
  • Kennedy’s sentence works because of its pause.

W3 Q9p.12Silence as communicationMCQ
W3 Q13p.13Silence and pauses in speechMSQ
W3 Q14p.13Non-words and vocalised pausesMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Meta-communication is communication beyond words: choice of words, tone, positioning, hesitation and silence. Silence and pauses relieve monotony, give time to think, build rapport and add emphasis, while non-words (ah, um, I mean) harm clarity and show poor preparation. Speakers should provide silence deliberately, keep it short and read others’ silences in context and culture.

Lecture 14: Listening Skills

Week 3 · Course book PDF pages 209–225 (printed pages 208–224)

Learning objectives

  • Distinguish hearing from listening.
  • List the stages of the listening process: hearing, filtering, interpreting, responding, remembering.
  • Explain perceptive listening (Ernest D. Nathan) and the advantages of listening.
  • Identify barriers: prejudging, ambiguity, bias/illusion, rigidity, premature evaluation.

Important definitions

Listening

A conscious, mental skill and art of paying attention, understanding and remembering what is said; involves ears and brain together.

Hearing

A physiological activity requiring no extra effort; sound reaches the ear without conscious attention.

Perceptive listening

Ernest D. Nathan: a conscious cognitive effort involving primarily the sense of hearing reinforced by other senses, leading to understanding and inspired by a sincere desire to understand.

Prejudging

Forming an opinion before listening completely; a barrier to listening.

Rigidity

Unwillingness to change one’s view, which blocks listening (e.g. old-timers resisting the paperless office).

Core concepts

Listening versus hearing

  • Hearing is physiological; listening is mental and needs conscious participation.
  • Examples: radio playing while solving sums (hearing); remembering grandparents’ stories (listening).
  • Researchers: 60 to 75 percent of time is spent in listening, yet we are not trained to listen.
  • People listen to what interests them.
  • Listening and hearing: “You never listen” means you do not pay attention.

Process of listening

Hearing → Filtering → Interpreting → Responding → Remembering.

  • Hearing: physical; make it mental.
  • Filtering: words, expressions, idioms; unknown words block listening.
  • Interpreting: understanding the message (e.g. a GM’s instruction passed through channels).
  • Responding: decision or reply; impossible without interpreting.
  • Remembering: retaining data and quotations.

Advantages of listening

  • Knowledge: a means of gathering information; saves time and money (lectures instead of buying books).
  • Learning new things and inspiring creativity and sharing.
  • Sound relationships (parents, friends, colleagues).
  • Better performance: follow the boss’s instructions and colleagues’ ideas; helps solve problems.
  • Vocabulary and pronunciation: hearing words in context with tone and pronunciation.
  • Avoids miscommunication; helps in exams through remembered lectures.
  • Supports a paperless office.

Barriers to listening

  1. Prejudging – drawing conclusions early.
  2. Ambiguity – unclear speaker or content.
  3. Bias or illusion – ‘I already know this subject’.
  4. Rigidity – refusing change (demonetisation and paperless office examples).
  5. Premature evaluation – judging the speaker by appearance.
  6. Unfamiliar words; lack of interest; distractions (music while driving).

How to listen well

  • Follow “SLANT”: Sit up, Listen, interpret and then think (as phrased in the lecture).
  • Hear adequately; filter, interpret, respond, remember.
  • Solve the problems affecting your listening.
  • Respect the speaker’s experience; judge thoughts, not appearance.
  • Be patient: “patience not only pays, patience prevails.”

Types, classifications and characteristics

Stages of the listening process
StageMeaning
HearingPhysiological reception of sound
FilteringSorting words and expressions mentally
InterpretingUnderstanding and relating meaning
RespondingReacting, deciding or replying
RememberingRetaining the information

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Perceptive listening — Ernest D. Nathan

A conscious cognitive effort, involving mainly hearing reinforced by other senses, leading to understanding and inspired by a sincere desire to understand.

Examples and applications

Example

Radio plays while a student solves sums: hearing, not listening.

Example

Remembering grandparents’ stories because of intent listening.

Example

A train-journey friendship built through listening.

Example

Listening to music while driving can cause accidents.

Example

Demonetisation and the paperless office as instances of rigidity.

Comparison tables

Hearing versus listening
PointHearingListening
NaturePhysiologicalMental, conscious
EffortNo extra effortNeeds attention and purpose
InvolvementOnly earsEars and brain
RetentionOften forgottenRemembered

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Listening process: hearing, filtering, interpreting, responding, remembering.
  • 60–75% of time is spent in listening.
  • Hearing is physiological; listening is mental.
  • Ernest D. Nathan defined perceptive listening.
  • Barriers: prejudging, ambiguity, bias, rigidity, premature evaluation.
  • Listening builds knowledge, relationships and vocabulary.
  • Perceptive listening is inspired by a sincere desire to understand.
  • SLANT: sit up, listen, interpret, think (as given in lecture).

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Listening is a conscious mental skill, unlike hearing. It passes through hearing, filtering, interpreting, responding and remembering. Perceptive listening, as defined by Ernest D. Nathan, needs a sincere desire to understand. Listening brings knowledge, relationships, better performance and vocabulary, but prejudging, ambiguity, bias, rigidity and premature evaluation block it.

Lecture 15: Types of Listening

Week 3 · Course book PDF pages 226–239 (printed pages 225–238)

Learning objectives

  • Name and explain the three basic types of listening: combative, attentive, reflective.
  • Explain content, empathetic, appreciative and analytical listening with uses.
  • Identify external and internal factors affecting listening and prejudiced listening.
  • Apply tips for effective listening and recall Hemingway’s quotation.

Important definitions

Combative listening

Listening with protest or opposition in mind; the element of protest damages the listening process.

Attentive listening

Paying equal attention to every word throughout, without analysing or interpreting in the process.

Reflective listening

The most important form: listening and analysing in order to relate and gain knowledge.

Content listening

Listening carefully to bosses, leaders or chairpersons, often to take notes, so that instructions can be passed on or acted on.

Empathetic listening

Listening to a person’s problem to share feelings, relieve stress and perhaps advise, so that the person feels motivated.

Appreciative listening

Listening to ideas, performance or achievements and appreciating them.

Analytical listening

Listening to all parties (e.g. in a dispute) and analysing before deciding.

Core concepts

Three basic types

  1. Combative – listen to protest.
  2. Attentive – listen to every word.
  3. Reflective – listen and analyse; the most important.

Types in the workplace

  • Content – meetings, new policies, instructions (upward and downward flow).
  • Empathetic – employees with stress or personal crisis; boss listens, shares and advises.
  • Appreciative – new ideas, papers, songs, policies need an appreciative audience.
  • Analytical – listen to both parties in a dispute or while negotiating or inviting bids.

Use the right type for the purpose; analysing a person who only wants to unburden is wrong.

Prejudiced listening

  • When something opposes our deeply rooted prejudices, the brain becomes over-stimulated and stops listening.
  • We mentally plan a rebuttal or a question designed to embarrass the speaker.
  • Criticism about colour, dress or a ‘hasty argument’ may block the listener.

Situations needing listening

  • Conversation — good conversation needs patience in listening.
  • Group discussion — listening makes a leader; even an idea-less member can contribute by listening.
  • Organisational activities — coordination depends on listening.
  • Negotiation — weigh pros and cons.
  • Interview — do not jump to answer before the question is complete.
  • Meetings — avoid whispering and parallel talk.

Factors affecting listening

  • External: noise, venue, time, culture (hence schools in isolated places).
  • Internal: state of mind (preoccupation), ego (“incurable disease”), language barriers/unknown words, different perceptions, ego clashes, rigid categories, ambiguity.

Tips for effective listening

  • Be generous and go with an open mind.
  • Concentrate on the speaker; judge thoughts, not appearance.
  • Take brief notes while listening and summarise mentally.
  • Do not interrupt; note doubts and ask politely afterwards.
  • Show interest non-verbally; keep critical thinking to yourself.
  • Avoid parallel talk; read the speaker’s non-verbal cues, pauses and silences.
  • Use gaps to relate ideas. Listen to learn and to unlearn.
  • Hemingway: “I like to listen. I have learned a great deal from listening carefully. Most people never listen.”

Types, classifications and characteristics

Types of listening by purpose
TypePurposeTypical situation
ContentGather information / instructionsBoss’s announcement, meeting
EmpatheticShare feeling; relieve stressEmployee with personal problem
AppreciativeAcknowledge and enjoyNew idea, paper, song
AnalyticalEvaluate and decideDispute, negotiation, bids
Basic three types
TypeKey feature
CombativeListening with protest
AttentiveAttention to every word
ReflectiveListening plus analysis; most important

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Listening quotation — Ernest Hemingway

“I like to listen. I have learned a great deal from listening carefully. Most people never listen.”

Examples and applications

Example

A boss criticises a project as ‘hasty’ and the author stops listening.

Example

An over-excited interviewee answers before the question ends.

Example

Educational institutions are located away from noise.

Example

A manager listens empathetically to an employee’s crisis.

Comparison tables

ExternalInternal
NoiseState of mind
VenueEgo
TimeLanguage and perceptions
CultureAmbiguity, rigidity, clashes

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip

  • Reflective listening is the most important.
  • Types by purpose: content, empathetic, appreciative, analytical.
  • External factors: noise, venue, time, culture.
  • Internal factors: state of mind, ego, language, perception.
  • Do not interrupt, whisper or hold parallel talks in meetings.
  • Take notes while listening.
  • Hemingway quote: most people never listen.

Summary

Listening may be combative, attentive or reflective, with reflective listening the most valuable. In organisations content, empathetic, appreciative and analytical listening serve different purposes. Prejudice, noise, ego, preoccupation and language hamper listening, whereas an open mind, concentration, note-taking, patience and attention to non-verbal cues improve it.

Week 4: Negotiation, Culture and Organisational Communication

  1. Lecture 16: Negotiation Skills I

Lecture 16: Negotiation Skills I

Week 4 · Course book PDF pages 240–254 (printed pages 239–253)

Learning objectives

  • Define negotiation and list its ingredients.
  • Differentiate formal and informal negotiation.
  • Explain factors affecting negotiation: place, time, atmosphere, subjective factors, fear, influence.
  • State the qualities of a negotiator and the stages of negotiation; define BATNA, WATNA and ZOPA.

Important definitions

Negotiation (Alan Fowler)

A form of meeting or discussion in which the parties use argument and persuasion to reach an agreed decision or action. (The transcript spells the name ‘Alan Flower’.)

Negotiation (process view)

A process of combining conflicting positions into a common position, turning conflict into agreement.

BATNA

Best Alternative To a Negotiated Agreement: your fallback if the negotiation fails.

WATNA

Worst Alternative To a Negotiated Agreement: the worst result that you must avoid accepting out of fear or influence.

ZOPA

Zone Of Possible Agreement: the range in which the parties’ interests overlap and agreement is possible.

Win-win situation

An agreement that benefits both parties.

Core concepts

Nature of negotiation

  • Arises when there is a conflict or deadlock or difference of opinion.
  • Needs two parties with interests; discussion must not be one-sided.
  • Works through persuasion and instruction, not authority.
  • Outcome should benefit both parties; sometimes lose-win, ideally win-win.
  • Examples: buying a car; employee seeking a raise from the boss.

Formal versus informal negotiation

  • Formal: pre-announced date, venue, time and agenda; parties know the subject; usually three people: two parties and a negotiator/observer.
  • Informal: not announced; casual, friendly; two persons; little preparation; position and familiarity can create pressure or deadlock; need extra restraint.

Factors affecting negotiation

  • Place: own place gives comfort, confidence and access to documents.
  • Time: mutually suitable; not imposed.
  • Atmosphere: free exchange; no threat; seek common ground.
  • Subjective factors: personal relationships, bias of observer, fear, influence and mutual obligation, personal ambition.
  • Past negotiations may help.
  • Kennedy: “Let us never negotiate out of fear, but let us never fear to negotiate.”

Qualities of a negotiator

  • Confidence from preparation; have a plan B.
  • Appearance – dress with dignity.
  • Outlook – flexible, ready to concede but never agree unless you have gained.
  • Honesty and reliability – know your subject and both sides’ strengths and weaknesses.
  • Competence – communication and reading meta-communication and facial reactions.
  • Flexibility and positivity; no agreement can be totally fair.

Stages and tactics

  • Preparation (study the issue, gather facts, know upper and lower limits) → Negotiation/interaction (simple language, stay cool, conducive atmosphere) → agreement.
  • Use polite language: “I think we need some more clarity in this regard”; “Can you repeat what you said earlier?”
  • Facts must be factual and consistent; many negotiations fail because of poor control of vocabulary.
  • Upper limit = how much you concede; lower limit = loss you can accept.
  • Prepare BATNA; avoid WATNA; aim for ZOPA and a win-win outcome.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Formal versus informal negotiation
PointFormalInformal
AnnouncementDate, venue, time and agenda announcedNot announced
PreparationPrepared in advanceLittle or no time
PersonsTwo parties plus negotiator/observerTwo persons
AtmosphereOfficialFriendly, may be influenced by position
Key acronyms
AcronymExpansion
BATNABest Alternative To a Negotiated Agreement
WATNAWorst Alternative To a Negotiated Agreement
ZOPAZone Of Possible Agreement

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Definition of negotiation — Alan Fowler (‘Flower’ in transcript)

A meeting or discussion where argument and persuasion lead to an agreed decision.

Fear and negotiation — John F. Kennedy

“Let us never negotiate out of fear, but let us never fear to negotiate.”

Examples and applications

Example

Haggling over the price of a car; the seller must also benefit.

Example

An employee negotiating a raise with the boss.

Example

Students negotiating marks with a faculty member (fear factor).

Example

Using ‘Can you repeat what you said earlier?’ to buy time.

Comparison tables

BATNA versus WATNA
BATNAWATNA
Best fallback if talks failWorst outcome to avoid
Gives confidence and strengthAccepted only under fear or influence
Prepare in advanceNever accept

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Negotiation arises from conflict and needs two parties.
  • Formal negotiation announces date, venue, time and agenda; three persons involved.
  • Informal negotiation is not pre-announced.
  • Negotiation works by persuasion, not authority.
  • BATNA, WATNA, ZOPA acronyms.
  • Qualities: confidence, appearance, outlook, honesty, competence, flexibility.
  • Kennedy’s fear quotation.
  • Facts must be consistent; know upper and lower limits.
  • Ideal outcome is win-win.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Negotiation turns conflict into agreement through argument and persuasion. Formal negotiations are pre-announced with an agenda; informal ones are casual. Place, time, atmosphere, personal relations and fear affect results. A negotiator needs confidence, dignity, flexibility, honesty and competence, must prepare facts and limits, secure a BATNA, avoid WATNA and seek a ZOPA that yields a win-win outcome.

Answer: A, B, D
Why: Informal is unplanned.
Q4 T/F Negotiation should be conducted through authority rather than persuasion.
Answer: False
Why: It works through persuasion.
Q5 Fill BATNA stands for Best ______ To a Negotiated Agreement.
Answer: Alternative
Why: It is the fallback option.
Q6 Short State three qualities of a good negotiator.
Answer: Confidence, honesty and flexibility (also appearance, outlook, competence).
Why: Preparation builds confidence.

Revision checklist

  • I can define negotiation.
  • I can compare formal and informal negotiation.
  • I can list factors and qualities.
  • I can expand BATNA, WATNA, ZOPA.
  • I remember Kennedy’s fear quote.

Lecture 17: Negotiation Skills II

Week 4 · Course book PDF pages 255–269 (printed pages 254–268)

Learning objectives

  • Explain persuasion factors in negotiation: style, you-attitude, body language, summarising.
  • Describe the three phases: preparation, interaction (negotiation), implementation.
  • Distinguish red, blue and purple negotiation styles.
  • List negotiation approaches (collaborate, accommodate, compromise, control, avoid) and dos and don’ts.

Important definitions

You-attitude

Convincing the other party that they will benefit; telling them the ‘sunny side’ of the deal and letting them talk while you listen.

Sunny side of the deal

The advantages to the other party that you highlight to persuade them.

Red style

Self-centred, competitive negotiation style.

Blue style

Cooperative, less competitive style.

Purple style

A mix of red and blue; the recommended style.

Win-win / win-lose / lose-win

Possible outcomes of a negotiation; no negotiation gives a total win to one party.

Core concepts

Persuasion factors

  • Style: language, convincing approach, presenting strengths and weaknesses; each party presents its case first.
  • You-attitude: highlight the benefit to the other; combine talking and listening.
  • Body language: glances, eyes and face complement words.
  • Summarising: “I have said this, you have said this… something still needs discussion.”

Agreement is the culmination (“sum and substance”) of exchanged views.

Three phases of negotiation

  1. Preparation – plan, study pros and cons, know your strength and the other’s weakness, upper and lower limits, anticipate (plan B), discover the real issue, be realistic. Tim Hindle: it is almost impossible for a negotiator to do too much preparation.
  2. Interaction / negotiation – introductions (often by the observer), exchange initial views, test the initial position, look for common ground (ZOPA), small sacrifices, watch time, note down the points of agreement, control vocabulary.
  3. Implementation – decide roles after agreement and ensure the agreement sustains.

Negotiation styles by colour

  • Red: self-centred, competitive.
  • Blue: cooperative.
  • Purple: blend; best choice.

Approaches / qualities

  • Collaborate – avoid confrontation.
  • Accommodate – polite, allow more room, especially with other cultures.
  • Compromise – do not remain rigid.
  • Control – be forgiving, conceding, convincing; ask for clarification advantageously.
  • Avoid – keep emotion out of sight, avoid confrontational tone.

Dos and don’ts

  • Keep some key points in reserve; do not say everything at once.
  • Do not get personal; read the tone, not only the words.
  • Do not prolong discussion after agreement; sign at the right time and record points.
  • Acknowledge the other party’s cooperation.
  • Be ready for deadlock, defeat and lose-win outcomes; hide signs of loss and stay optimistic.
  • Allow other cultures more time and space.
  • “In business you do not get what you deserve, you get what you negotiate.”
  • Negotiation is cooperation, not competition.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Three phases of negotiation
PhaseKey tasks
PreparationStudy issue, strengths/weaknesses, limits, plan B
InteractionExchange views, test positions, find common ground, agree, note points
ImplementationAssign roles, carry out the agreement
Negotiation styles
StyleNature
RedCompetitive, self-centred
BlueCooperative
PurpleBlend of red and blue (preferred)

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Preparation — Tim Hindle (‘Handle’ in transcript)

It is almost impossible for a negotiator to do too much preparation.

Examples and applications

Example

A schoolboy negotiates a bike with parents: bike for a 9-point CGPA.

Example

Companies selling skill-based labs show the sunny side.

Example

Cross-cultural delay: patient negotiators can win more concessions.

Comparison tables

Red, blue and purple styles
StyleOrientationResult
RedSelf-centredDeadlock risk
BlueCooperativeSmooth but may concede too much
PurpleMixedBalanced and recommended

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Three phases: preparation, interaction, implementation.
  • Red = competitive; blue = cooperative; purple = mix.
  • You-attitude means telling the sunny side.
  • Tim Hindle: almost impossible to over-prepare.
  • Agree and write down the points; decide roles.
  • No negotiation gives a total win to only one party.
  • Get what you negotiate, not what you deserve.
  • Be patient with cultures that delay; allow concessions.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W4 Q7p.16Colour-based negotiation stylesMCQ
W4 Q9p.16ZOPAFill-in-the-blank

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Negotiation strategy depends on persuasion: style, you-attitude, body language and summarising. It runs through preparation, interaction and implementation. The purple style, blending cooperation and competition, is preferred, supported by collaboration, accommodation, compromise, control and emotional avoidance. Negotiators keep some points in reserve, record agreements, acknowledge cooperation and accept that outcomes can be win-win, win-lose or lose-win.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ The recommended negotiation style is:
  1. Red
  2. Blue
  3. Purple
  4. Green
Answer: C
Why: It mixes cooperation and competition.
Q2 MCQ ‘You-attitude’ means:
  1. Talking only about yourself
  2. Showing the other party their benefit
  3. Remaining silent
  4. Using authority
Answer: B
Why: Tell them the sunny side.
Q3 MSQ Which are phases of negotiation?
  1. Preparation
  2. Interaction
  3. Implementation
  4. Elimination
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Elimination is not a phase.
Q4 T/F A negotiator should reveal all key points at the start.
Answer: False
Why: Keep some in reserve for concessions.
Q5 Fill A style that is self-centred and competitive is the ______ style.
Answer: red
Why: Blue is cooperative.
Q6 Short Why should points of agreement be written down?
Answer: So that the agreement is clear and sustains in implementation.
Why: Vocabulary affects meaning.

  • I know the three phases.
  • I can contrast red, blue and purple.
  • I can list approaches and dos and don’ts.
  • I understand you-attitude.
  • I remember outcome types.

Lecture 18: Culture as Communication

Week 4 · Course book PDF pages 270–288 (printed pages 269–287)

Learning objectives

  • Define culture and explain how it affects communication.
  • Explain cultural shock, diaspora and globalisation (GATT, NAFTA).
  • List the dimensions of culture: context, individualism, formality, communication style, time orientation.
  • Compare communication styles of Americans, Japanese, Arabs, Mexicans, Germans, Europeans and others, and cite translation blunders.

Important definitions

Culture

A combination of traits, behaviours, values, myths and traditions of a group; the totality of a group’s thoughts, experiences and patterns of behaviour and their contact with other cultures. It is learnt and dynamic.

Cultural shock

The feeling of isolation or being a loner that arises when one is unfamiliar with a different culture’s way of living and responding.

Global village

The world seen as interconnected through technology, trade and travel.

Diaspora

People of one country settled in another country.

Cultural sensitivity

Awareness and respect for differences between cultures when communicating.

Deference vs difference

The lecture advises showing deference (respect) rather than difference.

Core concepts

Why culture matters

  • We assume the world behaves as we do; exposure to other cultures shows differences.
  • Culture shapes eating, dress, vocabulary, festivals, thinking and identity; it gives a sense of belonging.
  • Globalisation: GATT (General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade) and NAFTA (North American Free Trade Agreement), IT and transport exchange cultures; workplaces are multicultural. Example: idli–dosa in the north.
  • Cultures are logical (a cat crossing the road is an omen to Indians) and dynamic; learn symbols, rituals, values, heroes and worships.
  • Components to understand: language, gestures, appearance, religion, philosophy, values, family customs, communication systems.
  • Example of cultural shock: the film/novel The Namesake (childbirth abroad alone).
  • Gandhi: different ways to reach God, but the aim is one.

Major dimensions of culture

  1. Context (high versus low)
  2. Individualism
  3. Formality
  4. Communication style
  5. Time orientation

Formality and style

  • Americans: casual, direct, first names or titles, uncomfortable with silence and delay, take words literally; ‘hamburger style’ criticism (praise bun, criticism meat, praise bun).
  • Europeans: formal; do not use first names without invitation.
  • Japan/China: use titles; Japanese treat card exchange and signing as ritual.
  • Mexicans: value courtesies and food before business; hurt if pushed to business.
  • Arabs, South Americans and Asians: friendship first; poetic and indirect language.
  • Germans: direct but understated; Nigerians: quiet and clear; Japanese: subtle.
  • Asians use silence to gain concessions.

Contracts and context

  • Americans (low context): contracts are specific and word-based.
  • Greeks: a formal statement.
  • Japanese: a statement of intention that can improve.
  • Mexicans: an artistic exercise achievable in an ideal world.
  • Arabs (high context): contract is insulting; stress non-verbal cues.

Space and time

  • US and Canada keep about five feet; this is too close or too far for others.
  • Boss in the corner (US) versus middle (Mexico).
  • North Americans treat time as a commodity; Asians deliberate long; some cultures are relaxed (an American waited two hours for a purchase).

Translation blunders

  • Swiss restaurant: “Our wines will make you forget everything / leave you nowhere.”
  • “Women are not allowed to have their children at the bar.”
  • “Our water has been passed by the manager himself.”

Understand the essence, not the literal words.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Dimensions of culture
DimensionContrast
ContextHigh versus low
IndividualismIndividual versus group
FormalityTradition, titles, rituals
Communication styleDirect versus indirect
Time orientationTime as commodity versus flexible

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Hamburger style of management — Observation on Americans

Open with warm talk, deliver criticism in the middle, close with reassurance (the buns).

Examples and applications

Example

Breakfast in Kuala Lumpur and dinner in Delhi.

Example

Brother returns from abroad with changed behaviour.

Example

Cat crossing the road as an omen in India.

Example

The Namesake and cultural shock.

Example

Hotel advertisement translation blunders.

Comparison tables

Communication styles by culture
CultureStyle
AmericanDirect, literal, casual, impatient with silence
GermanDirect but understated, formal
JapaneseSubtle, ritual cards, statement of intention
Arab/South AmericanPoetic, friendship first, high context
MexicanCourtesy first; contract is artistic ideal
NigerianQuiet, clear

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Culture is learnt and dynamic.
  • GATT and NAFTA promote trade across cultures.
  • Dimensions: context, individualism, formality, communication style, time orientation.
  • Americans: hamburger style; low context; time is money.
  • Mexicans prefer courtesies before business.
  • Arabs consider contracts insulting (high context).
  • US/Canada keep about five feet of space.
  • Cultural shock causes isolation (The Namesake).
  • Europeans do not use first names without invitation.

Summary

Culture is the learnt set of traits, values and behaviours of a group and strongly shapes communication. Globalisation brings cultural contact and, sometimes, cultural shock. Cultures differ in context, individualism, formality, communication style and time orientation; low-context Americans and Germans are direct and word-based, while high-context Arabs, Japanese and Asians stress relationship, silence and non-verbal cues. Respect, deference and learning are the keys to cross-cultural success.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ Which is a dimension of culture mentioned?
  1. Climate
  2. Time orientation
  3. Latitude
  4. Currency
Answer: B
Why: Context, individualism, formality, style, time orientation.
Q2 MCQ The ‘hamburger style’ is associated with:
  1. Japanese
  2. Americans
  3. Arabs
  4. Mexicans
Answer: B
Why: Praise, criticism, praise.
Q3 MSQ Select high-context cultures from the lecture’s discussion.
  1. Arab
  2. Japanese
  3. American
  4. German
Answer: A, B
Why: Americans and Germans rely on words.
Q4 T/F GATT and NAFTA are trade agreements.
Answer: True
Why: They helped goods and cultures move.
Q5 Fill Feeling isolated in an unfamiliar culture is called cultural ______.
Answer: shock
Why: As in The Namesake.
Q6 Short Why should translations of advertisements be checked?
Answer: Literal translation can produce absurd meanings, e.g. ‘our wines will leave you nowhere’.
Why: Convey the essence.

Revision checklist

  • I can define culture.
  • I know the five dimensions.
  • I can contrast American, Japanese, Arab and Mexican styles.
  • I can explain cultural shock.
  • I remember translation blunders.

Lecture 19: Communicating across Cultures

Week 4 · Course book PDF pages 289–304 (printed pages 288–303)

Learning objectives

  • Define communicating across cultures and Alfred G. Smith’s definition of culture.
  • Contrast low-context and high-context cultures.
  • Distinguish intercultural, cross-cultural and global communication contexts.
  • Explain ethnocentrism, stereotyping and the movement toward ethno-relativism; recall cultural examples.

Important definitions

Culture (Alfred G. Smith)

“Culture is a code we learn and share, and learning and sharing require communication.” Communication requires coding and symbols that must be learned and shared. (From Communication and Culture.)

Intercultural communication

Face-to-face interaction among people of diverse cultures (also between subcultures of one country).

Global context

Trans-border transfer of information by institutions and groups.

Cross-cultural communication

Comparing a phenomenon across cultures; problems arise when meanings differ.

Ethnocentrism

The belief that one’s own culture is the best.

Ethno-relativism

Accepting that cultures differ and adapting to them; reached through stages from denial to acceptance and adaptation.

Stereotyping

An oversimplified view of a culture or community, judging individuals by their group.

Core concepts

Low versus high context

  • Low context (North America, Scandinavia, France, Germany): logic, written contracts, words, analytical and action-oriented language, individualism, little role for silence and non-words.
  • High context (Japan, China, Arab countries): meaning from context, intuition, silence and non-verbal cues; contemplative; social harmony and saving face; prefer friendship to contract.
  • Example: a Japanese buyer paused, folded arms and closed eyes while the American seller grew impatient.

Communication contexts

  • International: face-to-face among diverse cultures.
  • Global: information across borders.
  • Cross-cultural: comparing across cultures.
  • Intercultural: interaction between people of different cultures, including subcultures (Indian Bengali versus Bangladeshi Bengali; American versus Indian English).

Cultural variations

  • Superstition: a worm in pulque is fortunate in Mexico; the Japanese Maneki neko (beckoning cat) attracts customers.
  • Numbers: 8 is lucky in China and Hong Kong; 4 sounds like death (shi) so no room 4 in hospitals.
  • Space: Arabs of the same sex stand closer; North Americans keep five feet.
  • Silence: Americans cannot bear long silence.
  • Colour, greeting and touch differ.
  • Saying no: USA/Canada shake head; Bulgaria nods up and down; Japan moves the right hand; Sicily raises the chin.
  • An Indian politely refusing food saying ‘no’ went hungry among Americans.
  • HP: French engineers wrote shorter e-mails, which Americans read as withholding information.
  • Mazda: casual baseball caps hurt Americans’ feelings.
  • Gifts: a clock in China invites evil; do not gift an Arab’s wife; a rose to a German lady may signal romance. ‘How was your weekend?’ may seem intrusive.
  • Gestures: Thatcher’s victory sign and the thumbs-up may be negative elsewhere; Asian men holding hands is friendly.

Variables to watch

Ethnocentrism, education, religious faith, country laws, language and use of space are variables in cross-cultural communication.

Bridging cultural gaps

  • Tolerance, empathy, patience; do not judge; give others space and time.
  • Avoid stereotypes (a basket of mangoes: not all are rotten).
  • Move from ethnocentrism to ethno-relativism through six stages (denial, defence, minimisation, acceptance, adaptation, integration), seeking commonalities.
  • Indians care about image; Mexicans and Asians about harmony and saving face.
  • Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam (“Ayam nijah paro veti…”): the world is one family.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Low versus high context cultures
FeatureLow contextHigh context
CountriesNorth America, Scandinavia, France, GermanyJapan, China, Arab countries
Meaning inWordsContext, silence, non-verbal cues
ContractsWritten and specificRelationship, trust
OrientationIndividualism, actionHarmony, contemplation
Ways to say ‘no’
PlaceGesture
USA/CanadaShake head side to side
BulgariaNod up and down
JapanMove right hand
SicilyRaise chin

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Culture as a code — Alfred G. Smith

Culture is a code we learn and share; sharing requires communication. (The Lecture 2 note records the transcript’s ‘Adam Smith’ attribution as a discrepancy.)

Ethnocentrism to ethno-relativism — Cross-cultural model (six stages)

Denial → defence → minimisation → acceptance → adaptation → integration.

Examples and applications

Example

Japanese buyer’s silence versus an American seller’s impatience.

Example

Worm in the drink: lucky in Mexico, unlucky elsewhere.

Example

Indian politely says ‘no’ to second helpings and is not offered more.

Example

HP’s French and US engineers; Mazda baseball caps.

Comparison tables

Ethnocentrism versus ethno-relativism
EthnocentrismEthno-relativism
My culture is the bestCultures are different but equal
Denial and defenceAcceptance and adaptation
Judges by own standardsUnderstands others’ viewpoint

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Low context: North America, Scandinavia, France, Germany.
  • High context: Japan, China, Arab countries.
  • Number 8 is lucky; 4 sounds like death in Hong Kong/China.
  • Maneki neko is the Japanese beckoning cat.
  • North Americans keep about 5 feet of space.
  • Sicily raises the chin to say no; Bulgaria nods for no.
  • A clock as a gift is bad in China.
  • Ethno-relativism has six stages.
  • Vasudhaiva Kutumbakam shows the Asian ideal.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W4 Q5p.15Culture and numbersMCQ
W4 Q6p.16Definition of cultureMCQ
W4 Q13p.17Low-context culturesMSQ
W4 Q15p.17Cultural differences in gestures and styleMatch the columns

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Communication across cultures requires understanding that culture is a shared code. Low-context cultures trust words and contracts, while high-context cultures read silence, context and relationships. Differences in gestures, numbers, gifts, space, colour and ways of saying no cause misunderstanding. Avoid ethnocentrism and stereotypes; practise tolerance, empathy and adaptation, moving towards ethno-relativism.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ Which culture is low context?
  1. Japan
  2. China
  3. Germany
  4. Arab countries
Answer: C
Why: North America, Scandinavia, France and Germany are low context.
Q2 MCQ The belief that one’s own culture is best is:
  1. Ethno-relativism
  2. Ethnocentrism
  3. Stereotype
  4. Diaspora
Answer: B
Why: It must be avoided.
Q3 MSQ Which are correctly matched ways of saying no?
  1. Bulgaria – nod up and down
  2. Sicily – raise chin
  3. USA – nod up and down
  4. Japan – move right hand
Answer: A, B, D
Why: In the USA people shake the head.
Q4 T/F In Hong Kong the number 4 is lucky.
Answer: False
Why: It resembles the word for death; 8 is lucky.
Q5 Fill A Japanese cat figure that attracts customers is ______.
Answer: Maneki neko
Why: The beckoning cat.
Q6 Short How can cultural gaps be bridged?
Answer: By tolerance, empathy, patience, avoiding judgments and stereotypes, and adapting to others’ norms.
Why: Move to ethno-relativism.

  • I can compare low and high context.
  • I know the ways of saying no.

  • I remember gift and number examples.

Lecture 20: Organizational Communication

Week 4 · Course book PDF pages 305–321 (printed pages 304–320)

Learning objectives

  • Define organisation and organisational communication (William Scott).
  • State functions and objectives of organisational communication.
  • Explain managerial roles (interpersonal, informational, decisional) and Maslow’s hierarchy at work.
  • Describe McGregor’s management styles, line and staff management, formal and informal channels, and types of workplace communication.

Important definitions

Organisation

A social unit of people structured and managed to meet a need or pursue collective goals.

Organisational communication (William Scott)

A process involving transmission and accurate replication of ideas, their dissemination, promotion and distribution, ensured by feedback, to elicit actions that accomplish the goals of the organisation.

Information Management System (IMS)

The core system by which information entering an organisation is managed and used.

Line management

People responsible for basic company activities such as manufacturing and sales.

Staff management

People who support line management with services such as accounting, distribution and personnel.

Grapevine

An informal channel of casual talk, whispers and rumours.

Crisis communication

Urgent communication among core managers when something goes wrong; combines persuasion with caution.

Core concepts

Why communication matters in an organisation

  • Carry out management, allocate jobs, disseminate information and clarify roles.
  • The communication environment shows a person’s calibre and is an asset.
  • Employees come from different backgrounds, ages, genders and faiths; communication must suit everyone.
  • Communication flows to bosses, groups and other departments.

Functions of communication

  1. Forecasting – what we want to do and where we want to be.
  2. Organising – distributing work and responsibilities among divisions (quality control, HR, accounts, inventory).
  3. Instructing / persuading – leader instructs, persuades or orders.
  4. Coordinating – across divisions.
  5. Controlling – the leader controls and is a role model.

Objectives

  • Improve job performance and update knowledge (workshops, conferences).
  • Create belonging; make employees feel heard and appreciated.
  • Convince employees about decisions; set daily, monthly and annual aims jointly.
  • Assign roles and empower employees with information.

Managerial roles

  • Interpersonal – talking with individuals and groups.
  • Informational – receiving and giving information; be like an ambassador who knows everything.
  • Decisional – listen to problems, interpret information and decide.

Maslow’s hierarchy at work

Physiological (food, shelter, salary) → safety/security → social (belonging, love, appreciation) → esteem/ego → self-actualisation (morality, creativity, spontaneity, purpose). “Ambition is everyone’s slave.”

McGregor’s two styles

  • Traditional (Theory X): workers need only basic needs; treated as labour; language of control.
  • Modern (Theory Y): balance of control and freedom; workers treated as humans with higher needs; language of persuasion; manager must understand their needs.

Communication flow and channels

  • Vertical (downward from top, upward), horizontal, crosswise (diagonal), and spiral for policy announcements (hike, bonus).
  • Line and staff management coordinate; staff support line.
  • Formal and informal channels (casual chat, grapevine). Managers may use the grapevine for warnings or to caution disgruntled employees; filter its information.
  • Koontz and O’Donnell: “The most effective communication results when managers utilise the informal organisational channels to supplement the formal organisational channels.”

Types of communication at workplace

  • Statutory – norms, terms and conditions, policies, bulletins.
  • Crisis management – urgent meetings of core members; persuasion and caution.

A satisfied employee is more productive than a dissatisfied one.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Functions of organisational communication
FunctionMeaning
ForecastingPlanning goals and future position
OrganisingDistributing tasks and divisions
Instructing / persuadingDirecting employees
CoordinatingLinking divisions
ControllingMonitoring performance; leader as role model
Managerial roles
RoleDescription
InterpersonalDealing with people
InformationalReceiving and sharing information
DecisionalInterpreting and deciding

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Organisational communication — William Scott

Transmission and replication of ideas ensured by feedback to accomplish organisational goals.

Hierarchy of needs — Abraham Maslow

Needs move from physiological to self-actualisation.

Traditional versus modern management — Douglas McGregor

Control-oriented versus freedom and persuasion-oriented styles.

Informal channels — Koontz and O’Donnell

Use informal channels to supplement formal ones.

Examples and applications

Example

Bank staff gather before opening to hear the day’s plan.

Example

Workshops for electricity workers to update knowledge.

Example

A manager plants messages on the grapevine to caution disgruntled staff.

Comparison tables

PointTraditionalModern
View of workersLabour or resources with basic needsHumans with higher needs
LanguageControlPersuasion
FreedomLittleBalance of control and freedom
Line versus staff management

Manufacturing, salesAccounting, distribution, personnel
Core activitiesSupport services

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Scott’s definition emphasises feedback and action.
  • Functions: forecasting, organising, instructing, coordinating, controlling.
  • Roles: interpersonal, informational, decisional.
  • Maslow’s hierarchy applies to employees.
  • McGregor: traditional versus modern.
  • Flows: vertical, horizontal, crosswise, spiral.
  • Types: statutory and crisis communication.
  • IMS means information management system.
  • Satisfied employees are more productive.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W1 Q8p.4Grapevine (informal channel)MCQ
W4 Q8p.16McGregor's management stylesMCQ
W4 Q14p.17Maslow's hierarchy of needsMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Organisational communication carries ideas through formal and informal channels so that goals are achieved. Its functions include forecasting, organising, instructing, coordinating and controlling; managers play interpersonal, informational and decisional roles. Understanding Maslow’s needs and McGregor’s styles helps leaders persuade rather than only control. Statutory and crisis communication, line–staff coordination and a careful use of the grapevine complete the picture.

Week 5: Communication Breakdown and Business Writing

  1. Lecture 21: Communication Breakdown Part I
  2. Lecture 22: Communication Breakdown Part II
  3. Lecture 23: Advanced Writing Skills
  4. Lecture 24: Principles of Business Writing

Lecture 21: Communication Breakdown Part I

Week 5 · Course book PDF pages 322–339 (printed pages 321–338)

Learning objectives

  • Define communication barrier or breakdown with the lecture’s examples.
  • Classify barriers: at the source, between people, and in the organisation.
  • Explain source barriers: faltering with words, uncertain content, background, wrong inference, perception, rigid categories, frozen evaluation, allness.
  • Explain interpersonal barriers: limited vocabulary, emotions, distraction, poor listening, selectivity, noise, verbal–non-verbal imbalance, semantic gap.

Important definitions

Communication barrier / breakdown

Anything that blocks, restricts or interferes with the smooth flow of a message so that it fails to achieve its purpose.

Homophily

A common frame of reference between sender and receiver (two doctors, two engineers).

Wrong inference

Drawing a mistaken conclusion, like mistaking a rope for a snake at dusk.

Frozen evaluation

Believing a past situation will repeat unchanged.

Allness (categorical thinking)

Using ‘all’ and ‘every’; believing one knows everything or that everyone is against one.

Communication selectivity

Picking out only the part of a message that interests you and ignoring the rest.

Semantic gap

A language or meaning gap caused by different uses of words.

Core concepts

Illustrative cases

  • A. G. Gardiner’s On Saying Please: the lift man throws out a passenger who says ‘top’ without ‘please’.
  • Crying baby: bottle offered, but the bed was wet.
  • Train ticket: date changes after 12 midnight; passenger missed the train.
  • Doctor’s ‘SOS’ misread: it means take as needed.
  • E-mail to a foreign friend: too long or too short, no reply.

Three kinds of breakdown

  1. At the source (sender).
  2. Between people (interpersonal).
  3. In the organisation (discussed in later lectures).

Remedy: find the cause, choose the best solution, get feedback.

Breakdown at the source

  • Faltering for words (leave applications, in an age of channels).
  • Uncertain content about why you are writing or speaking.
  • Different backgrounds (a book unread by the listener); lack of homophily.
  • Wrong inferences (rope or snake).
  • Different perceptions.
  • Rigid categories (‘I know nanotechnology; nothing new’).
  • Frozen evaluation (reaching early because you missed the train once).
  • Categorical thinking / allness (‘everyone is against me’).

“Change is the law of nature.”

Breakdown between people

  • Different perceptions of reality.
  • Limited vocabulary and multiple meanings (‘you are very fast’).
  • Emotional outbursts and tiredness.
  • Distraction from gadgets (laptop, phone, colleagues).
  • Poor listening; noise from adjacent rooms.
  • Communication selectivity (answering only a phrase in an interview).
  • Noise (also used as an excuse).
  • Imbalance between verbal and non-verbal messages.
  • Cultural differences (Kenya: son-in-law and mother-in-law do not face each other; silence).
  • Language as code: Mark Twain – the difference between the right word and the almost right word is that between lightning and a lightning bug.
  • Semantic gap: ‘loyal’ misused for ‘kind’; sidewalk (US), pavement (UK), platform (India); ‘table a motion’ – UK: bring up for discussion; US: postpone.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Barriers by origin
LevelExamples
SourceFaltering for words, unclear content, background, wrong inference, rigid categories, frozen evaluation, allness
Between peoplePerception, vocabulary, emotions, distraction, poor listening, selectivity, noise, semantic gap, cultural differences
OrganisationDiscussed in the next lectures (information overload and others)

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Right word versus almost right word — Mark Twain

The difference is like lightning and a lightning bug.

Examples and applications

Example

The lift man and ‘top please’.

Example

Rope mistaken for a snake.

Example

Doctor’s SOS tablet taken regularly.

Example

‘Table a motion’ means bring it up in Britain but postpone in the US.

Example

‘Sidewalk’ versus ‘pavement’ versus ‘platform’.

Comparison tables

British versus American usage
WordBritish / IndianAmerican
FootpathPavement / platformSidewalk
Table a motionTake up for discussionPostpone

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • A. G. Gardiner’s ‘On Saying Please’.
  • Three types: at the source, between people, in the organisation.
  • Homophily means a common frame of reference.
  • Allness uses words like all and every.
  • SOS in a prescription means ‘if required’.
  • Mark Twain’s lightning and lightning bug.
  • ‘Table a motion’ differs in UK and US.
  • Communication selectivity: hearing only what interests you.
  • Rope-and-snake example of wrong inference.

W5 Q6p.19Definition of communication breakdownMCQ
W5 Q8p.20Sources of communication breakdownMCQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Communication breakdown occurs when something blocks the flow of a message. Barriers arise at the source (poor word choice, uncertain content, background, inference, perception, rigid thinking, frozen evaluation, allness), between people (vocabulary, emotion, distraction, poor listening, selectivity, noise, imbalance of verbal and non-verbal cues, semantic and cultural gaps) and in organisations. Awareness, feedback and careful choice of words reduce them.

  1. Homophily
  2. Selectivity
  3. Allness
  4. Noise

Why: Two doctors talk smoothly.

  1. Few words
  2. ‘All’ and ‘every’
  3. Silence
  4. Jargon
Answer: B
Why: It is categorical thinking.

  2. Noise

Answer: A, B, C
Why: Clear content helps.

Q5 Fill Mistaking a rope for a snake is a wrong ______.

Lecture 22: Communication Breakdown Part II

Week 5 · Course book PDF pages 340–353 (printed pages 339–352)

Learning objectives

  • List the barriers to communication in organisations.
  • Explain transfer stations, media richness, jargon, physical distractions, ethics and misquoting.
  • Apply the remedies: non-judgmental language, empathy, listening, audience-centred messages, open climate, fewer levels, feedback.
  • Recall the lecture’s examples and Whitman’s quotation.

Important definitions

Transfer station

An intermediate level through which a message passes in a hierarchy; at each station the message may be reframed and distorted.

Media richness

The variety of channels available; the lecture stresses choosing the channel suited to the receiver’s background and facilities.

Lean media

Channels or messages for technical and objective information, which people often attend to least.

Proper channel

Following the prescribed route of reporting rather than approaching the top directly.

Misquoting

Reporting what another said inaccurately; an ethical lapse.

Audience-centred approach

Viewing the message from the receiver’s point of view (‘you’ attitude).

Core concepts

Organisational barriers

  • Organisational structure: tall hierarchy, many units; ‘proper channel’ restricts upward communication.
  • Fear of superior’s perception – juniors withhold information.
  • Negative attitudes of employees and managers destroy candour.
  • Status gap between senior and junior; lack of trust; grapevine functions as a ‘purgatory force’ (release).
  • Poor communication skills make peers avoid talking.
  • Groups and subgroups based on religion, faith, interest threaten channels.
  • Too many transfer stations distort messages: ‘the message sent is not always the message received’ (example: ‘a friend is passing away’ became ‘he is no more’).
  • Wrong choice of media, ignoring the receiver’s facilities; information overload.
  • Technical jargon used with non-experts.
  • Physical distractions: noise, poor acoustics, lighting, climate, power cuts, venue.
  • Ethical issues: misquotes, far-fetched predictions, rumours that go viral.

Overcoming the barriers

  • Be non-judgmental; select words carefully so they are not open to another meaning.
  • Put yourself in the other’s shoes; be empathetic; avoid unnecessary assumptions.
  • Be a good listener.
  • Avoid jargon; keep sentences and paragraphs short; consider cultural bias.
  • Keep non-verbal behaviour consistent with words.
  • Adopt an audience-centred approach.
  • Create an open communication climate.
  • Reduce the number of levels; use direct communication when important.
  • Seek immediate feedback.
  • Commit to ethical communication; avoid extreme statements; create lean, efficient messages.
  • Select the most suitable medium for the audience background.

The assumption story

A woman waiting at a bus stop smiled at a man's baby; he asked “Are you waiting for me?” — a wrong assumption. She replied that she had a baby at home. People misconstrue messages because they do not understand others’ assumptions and perceptions.

Walt Whitman (“Song of Myself”): “I celebrate myself, and sing myself, and what I assume you shall assume, for every atom belonging to me as good belongs to you.”

Types, classifications and characteristics

Organisational barriers and remedies
BarrierRemedy
Fear of superiorOpen communication climate
Negative attitudeEmpathy, non-judgmental language
Transfer stationsReduce levels; direct communication
Wrong media / overloadChoose suitable medium; lean messages
JargonPlain language; short sentences
Physical noiseSuitable venue, acoustics, lighting
Misquoting / rumoursEthical communication

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Song of Myself — Walt Whitman (American poet quoted)

“What I assume you shall assume” — think of how the message would affect you as receiver.

Examples and applications

Example

‘A friend is passing away’ transmitted as ‘he is no more’.

Example

A woman and a man with a baby at the bus stop.

Example

Tall hierarchy forces use of proper channel.

Example

Newly recruited managers require plain words; senior peers may use jargon.

Comparison tables

Closed versus open communication climate
Closed / restrictiveOpen
Fear of superiorsCandid exchange
Many transfer stationsDirect and fewer levels
Distrust, grapevineTrust and business thrives

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • The message sent is not always the message received.
  • Too many transfer stations distort messages.
  • Media richness: choose the channel suited to the receiver.
  • Reduce levels and encourage direct communication.
  • Take immediate feedback.
  • Whitman: what I assume you shall assume.
  • Grapevine acts as a purgatory force.
  • Use of jargon with non-experts is a barrier.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Organisational barriers include tall hierarchies, fear of superiors, negative attitudes, mistrust, subgroups, many transfer stations, wrong media, information overload, jargon, physical distractions and unethical practices such as misquoting. They are overcome by empathy, careful words, active listening, audience-centred messages, an open climate, fewer levels, suitable media and prompt feedback.

Lecture 23: Advanced Writing Skills

Week 5 · Course book PDF pages 354–375 (printed pages 353–374)

Learning objectives

  • Explain why writing matters and the features of advanced writing (conceptualise, research, draft, revise, reader-centred).
  • Differentiate informative and persuasive writing.
  • Apply directness, simple words and short sentences, and outlining.
  • List the nine writing patterns with examples.

Important definitions

Advanced writing

Professional, technical and scientific writing (proposals, reports, technical descriptions, business letters) based on a concept, research, drafting and revision, and centred on the reader.

Reader-centred writing

Writing planned from the reader’s point of view; it uses the ‘you’ tone.

Informative writing

Writing that conveys information as it is (e.g. stock verification form).

Persuasive / argumentative writing

Writing that explains, contradicts, counters arguments and convinces, with an assertive tone (thesis, dissertation).

Outline

A tentative plan with main topics, sub-topics and supporting points, like the tailor’s cutting plan.

Directness

Expressing the point plainly, without circumlocution.

Core concepts

Why writing matters

  • Francis Bacon: “Reading maketh a full man, conference a ready man, and writing an exact man.”
  • Reveals personality, grasp of language and reasoning in your absence.
  • Serves as a record (now through word processing and databases).
  • Professional work needs proposals, reports, manuals, technical descriptions and letters (credit, collection, complaint, adjustment).

Stages of advanced writing

  1. Conceptualise – have an idea; testify with truth and objectivity.
  2. Research – books and papers to support the concept.
  3. Draft – structure suited to report, letter, proposal or description.
  4. Revise – add, delete, check against the audience.
  5. Check that the writing is reader-centric.

Ask first: Why am I writing and for whom? Aims: express, inform, convince, persuade.

Style rules

  • Directness – plain, candid language, no circumlocution.
  • Short sentences (6–8 words are simple; 20–30 words are hard).
  • Familiar words; avoid jargon with lay readers; avoid pompous or wordy language; do not sacrifice meaning for brevity.
  • Make an outline; ensure unity, coherence and logic.
  • Put the verb early: “Please submit the report to me no later than 5 p.m. tomorrow” (better than “You will do me a personal favour…”).
  • Keep SVA (subject–verb agreement) and active voice; use ‘you’ tone.
  • Alexander Pope: “True ease in writing comes from art, not chance, as those move easiest who have learned to dance.”

Nine writing patterns

  1. Direct plan – good or routine news first: “Congratulations! Your proposal…”.
  2. Indirect plan – bad news: buffer, reasons, negative in the middle, positive ending (job-rejection example).
  3. Order of importance – most important reason first (recommendation letter).
  4. Chronology – time sequence (resume, incident report).
  5. Problem–solution.
  6. Causal – cause to effect or the reverse (Pali losing speakers).
  7. Spatial – by geography (sales north, south, east, west).
  8. Structural – by parts or structure (bonus, hike, compensation; course objective).
  9. Topical – breaks topics into subtopics.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Nine patterns of writing
PatternUse
Direct planGood or routine news
Indirect planBad or unpleasant news
Order of importanceRecommendations; ranked reasons
ChronologyResume, incident report
Problem–solutionProposals
CausalCause–effect analysis
SpatialGeographical description
StructuralParts of a plan or course
TopicalSubtopics

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Writing makes an exact man — Francis Bacon

Called the father of the essay; his essays are aphoristic.

Ease in writing — Alexander Pope

Art, not chance, produces ease.

Examples and applications

Example

“Congratulations! Your proposal for the workshop has evoked interest.” (direct)

Example

“Thank you for your application… Because of our size and reputation we review…” (indirect rejection)

Example

Resume year by year (chronology).

Example

“Pali is losing 60% significance because of fewer users.” (causal)

Comparison tables

Informative versus persuasive writing
PointInformativePersuasive
PurposeConvey facts as they areConvince, counter arguments
ToneNeutralAssertive, argumentative
ExampleStock verification formThesis, dissertation
Direct versus indirect plan
DirectIndirect
Main point firstBuffer first, bad news later
Good/routine newsUnpleasant news
Pleasant toneGuarded with positive ending

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Bacon: writing maketh an exact man.
  • Stages: conceptualise, research, draft, revise.
  • Ask why and for whom.
  • Nine patterns: direct, indirect, order of importance, chronology, problem–solution, causal, spatial, structural, topical.
  • Bad news goes in the indirect plan with a positive ending.
  • Short sentences of 6–8 words are simple.
  • Pope: true ease in writing comes from art, not chance.
  • Writing serves as a record.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Advanced writing is concept-based, researched, drafted, revised and reader-centred. It should be direct, simple and short-sentenced, planned with an outline. Informative writing reports facts; persuasive writing argues. Choose among nine patterns – direct, indirect, order of importance, chronology, problem–solution, causal, spatial, structural, topical – and put bad news in the indirect plan with a positive close.

Lecture 24: Principles of Business Writing

Week 5 · Course book PDF pages 376–392 (printed pages 375–391)

Learning objectives

  • List the 5 Cs of business writing: clarity, courtesy, consideration, conciseness, cordiality.
  • Explain how to achieve clarity, conciseness, courtesy and consideration with before-and-after examples.
  • Recall the quotations of T. S. Eliot and Oscar Wilde and the maxim ‘express, not impress’.
  • Rewrite negative and wordy sentences using the you-attitude.

Important definitions

Clarity

Writing that is unambiguous, plain and easily understood so that action can follow.

Courtesy

Respect shown to the reader in tone and word choice, even when information is dry.

Consideration

Writing from the reader’s viewpoint (the ‘you’ approach), so no one is hurt.

Conciseness

Being brief without sacrificing meaning; removing redundant words and flabby phrases.

Cordiality

Warmth and goodwill sustained in writing, since business is dealing with people.

Foggy writing

Unclear, cloudy messages produced by unskilled writers who have not learnt to communicate clearly.

Core concepts

The 5 Cs

  1. Clarity – ambiguity is an unwelcome guest in business.
  2. Courtesy
  3. Consideration
  4. Conciseness
  5. Cordiality

Goal: express, not impress; impress through simplicity and clarity.

Clarity

  • Unskilled writers produce foggy messages; use familiar words.
  • Example: “Employees have not been made sufficiently aware of the potentially adverse consequences…” → “Warn your employees about these chemicals.”
  • It is better to use a few more words than fewer words that are difficult.
  • T. S. Eliot: “Do not use big words too big for the subject; do not say ‘infinitely’ when you mean ‘very’.”

Conciseness: shorter expressions

  • a number of → many
  • at your earliest possible convenience → soon
  • fullest possible extent → fully
  • it would be unreasonable to assume → I assume
  • “There are three things I want you to do” → “I want you to do three things.”
  • Remove needless repetitions and redundant phrases; avoid excessive noun habit and pompous words; revise last. “Brevity is the soul of wit.”

Courtesy and consideration: rewriting

  • “You cannot meet the director; don’t you know he is out of station?” → “Thanks for your interest; today he is out of station. We will call you when he returns.”
  • “Will you send me 125 geysers at the official rate?” → “We require 125 geysers and expect to buy at the official rate.”
  • “The executive meeting is postponed.” → “We regret the inconvenience; the meeting has been postponed for unavoidable reasons.”
  • “You must know that PAN alone can make online tax procedure easy.” → “We would appreciate your PAN number; it will make tax procedure smoother.”
  • “Your project is returned; no more funds this year.” → “Thanks for submitting; we regret we lack funds this year, but will let you know once funds are available.”
  • Complaint about ACs: “We have received several complaints about the ACs supplied by us; we look forward to knowing what can be done.” (you-attitude)
  • Put the negative in the middle and end positively; give hope.

Cordiality

  • Avoid negative starts: “You are not a member, we cannot entertain you” → “We entertain our members; we expect you to join.”
  • “Your report is unsatisfactory” → “We appreciate a detailed report.”
  • “You should not ignore the official instructions” → “All of us follow the official timing: reports are due Friday.”
  • Oscar Wilde: “If you pretend to be good, the world takes you very seriously.” Keep hope alive, since a bad deal with a customer can cost an organisation heavily.

Types, classifications and characteristics

The 5 Cs
CMeaningTip
ClarityUnambiguous messageUse familiar words
CourtesyRespect for readerPolite tone
ConsiderationReader’s viewpointYou-attitude
ConcisenessBrief but completeCut redundancy
CordialityWarm goodwillPositive framing

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Big words — T. S. Eliot

“Do not use big words too big for the subject.”

Pretending to be good — Oscar Wilde

Quoted to encourage optimism in writing.

Examples and applications

Example

“Warn your employees about these chemicals.”

Example

“We grew hot and cold” (flowery) → “We have received complaints about the ACs.”

Example

Rejecting a project politely with hope.

Example

“Many” replaces “a number of”.

Comparison tables

Wordy versus concise
WordyConcise
A number ofMany
At your earliest possible convenienceSoon
Fullest possible extentFully
It would be unreasonable to assumeI assume
There are three things I want you to doI want you to do three things
Express versus impress
ExpressImpress
Make the reader understandShow off difficult words
Goal of business writingNot the goal

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • 5 Cs: clarity, courtesy, consideration, conciseness, cordiality.
  • Goal of business writing: to express, not to impress.
  • Eliot: do not use big words; do not say infinitely for very.
  • Put negative news in the middle and end positively.
  • Brevity is the soul of wit.
  • Revision is the last task in writing.
  • Wilde quote on pretending to be good.
  • ‘Warn your employees about these chemicals’ example.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W5 Q10p.20Stages of the writing processOrdering (MCQ)
W5 Q15p.21Principles of business writingMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Good business writing follows the 5 Cs. Express rather than impress: use plain, familiar words, cut redundant phrases and avoid foggy sentences. Show courtesy and consideration by taking the reader’s viewpoint, soften negatives, put bad news in the middle and end with hope, and keep cordiality so that relationships and business continue.

Practice questions
