  1. Impress
  2. Express
  3. Confuse
  4. Entertain
Answer: B
Why: Clarity over show.
Q2 MCQ ‘At your earliest possible convenience’ can be replaced by:
  1. Soon
  2. Never
  3. Someday
  4. Quickly please
Answer: A
Why: Shorter and clear.
Q3 MSQ Which are among the 5 Cs?
  1. Clarity
  2. Courtesy
  3. Criticism
  4. Cordiality
Answer: A, B, D
Why: Criticism is not a C.
Q4 T/F Eliot advised using the biggest words available.
Answer: False
Why: He advised against big words.
Q5 Fill Put negative news in the ______ and end positively.
Answer: middle
Why: Indirect plan.
Q6 Short How do you make a complaint letter considerate?
Answer: State the facts politely from the receiver's viewpoint and ask what can be done, avoiding anger.

Revision checklist

  • I can rewrite rude sentences politely.
  • I remember Eliot and Wilde.

Lecture 25: Types of Business Writing Part I

Week 5 · Course book PDF pages 393–410 (printed pages 392–409)

Learning objectives

  • List the types of business writing: letters, reports, memos, letter reports, circulars, e-mails, technical proposals, research papers.
  • Define a memo, state its purposes, tone and format.
  • Differentiate memo, memo report, letter report and circular.
  • Describe the types of circulars.

Important definitions

Memo (inter-office memorandum)

A short, informal piece of business writing circulated within an organisation to confirm, suggest, inform, explain, announce, report or warn.

Memo report

A memo written in report form for a longer explanation.

Letter report

A report written in business-letter format; less informal than a memo, longer (about 2–3 pages), may go inside or outside the organisation, with an inside address, salutation and complimentary close.

Circular

A document circulated within (or sometimes outside) an organisation to inform, create awareness or announce policy, products or programmes.

cc (carbon copy)

A line at the end on the left, listing persons who receive a copy.

Core concepts

Types of business writing

  • Business letters (sell, buy, order, remind)
  • Reports
  • Memos
  • Letter reports
  • Circulars
  • Electronic mails
  • Technical proposals
  • Research papers

Follow the standards and practices of your organisation.

Purposes of a memo

  • Confirm (meeting outcomes)
  • Suggest action
  • Inform / explain (why something went wrong)
  • Announce policies, hikes, DA, products
  • Request / report information
  • Warn (late coming, misuse of photocopier) without offending, because the tone is informal

A memo is not always a threat.

Format of a memo

  • Letterhead (organisation name) at the top centre.
  • To and From on the left; From shows the designation, not the name.
  • Reference number and date on the right.
  • Subject line in the middle, explicit.
  • No salutation and no complimentary close; direct, short, specific, conversational, informal.
  • Body: problem → explanation → action required.
  • Writer’s name at the end (right); cc on the left.

Sample memo: from Dean (Admission) to all heads about dissatisfactory campus placement; proposes faculty development training and meeting heads; cc Dean Academics.

Letter report

  • Letterhead, date, inside address, subject, formal salutation (‘Dear Sir’), introduction, findings, complimentary close, signature, enclosures (with number of pages).
  • Longer than a memo (1 page memo, 2–3 pages letter report); includes discussion and illustrations; less formal than a full formal report.
  • Example: report on a faculty development programme.

Circular

  • Purpose: inform, create awareness, matters of general interest.
  • Use a memo for people within the organisation; use a circular for larger audiences including outside.
  • Structure: introduction, details, relevant information; short unless required.
  • Letterhead on top centre, ‘Circular’, content, signature.
  • Revise carefully for spelling and grammar because reputation is at stake.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Types of circulars
TypeAudience / purpose
Informative circularFactual; introduction, details, relevant information
Public circularGeneral awareness to large, spread-out audiences (e.g. child labour policy)
Partnership / company circularPartners, shareholders and stakeholders
Official circularWide audience in organisations; longer and carefully worded

Examples and applications

Example

Dean (Admission) memo on placements and faculty development.

Example

Letter report on faculty development training.

Example

Warning memo about misuse of the photocopier.

Comparison tables

Memo versus letter report versus circular
PointMemoLetter reportCircular
AudienceWithin organisationInside or outsideWithin; sometimes public
ToneInformalLess informalInformal yet formal
LengthShort (about one page)2–3 pagesShort unless needed
Salutation / closeNoneYesNone
AddressTo / FromInside addressLetterhead

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Memo = inter-office memorandum.
  • A memo has no salutation or complimentary close.
  • From line shows designation.
  • Reference number and date on the right.
  • cc line at the end left.
  • Letter report may have 2–3 pages and enclosures.
  • Four circular types: informative, public, partnership/company, official.
  • Memo purposes: confirm, suggest, inform, explain, announce, report, warn.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W5 Q2p.19Goal of business writingFill-in-the-blank
W5 Q13p.21Internal business documentsMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Business writing includes letters, reports, memos, letter reports, circulars, e-mails, proposals and research papers. A memo is a short, informal intra-office document with letterhead, To/From (designation), reference and date, subject line and body but no salutation or close. A letter report is a longer, letter-format report that may go outside; a circular informs larger audiences and has four types.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ A memo does NOT contain:
  1. Subject line
  2. Salutation
  3. Date
  4. Reference number
Answer: B
Why: No salutation or complimentary close.
Q2 MCQ In a memo, the ‘From’ line shows:
  1. Name only
  2. Designation
  3. Address
  4. Phone number
Answer: B
Why: Designation is written, not name.
Q3 MSQ Which are types of circulars?
  1. Informative
  2. Public
  3. Official
  4. Private diary
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Partnership/company is also a type.
Q4 T/F A letter report is always shorter than a memo.
Answer: False
Why: It is longer (2–3 pages).
Q5 Fill A memo is also called an ______ memorandum.
Answer: inter-office
Why: It circulates within the organisation.
Q6 Short State two purposes of a memo.
Answer: To confirm a meeting outcome and to announce a policy (also suggest, explain, report, warn).
Why: Tone remains informal.

Lecture 26: Types of Business Writing Part II

Week 6 · Course book PDF pages 411–428 (printed pages 410–427)

Learning objectives

  • Draft a notice, agenda and minutes of a meeting with correct format and language.
  • Write professional e-mails following netiquette.
  • Differentiate research paper types: journal paper, conference paper, review article, thesis, term paper, technical paper.
  • Plan a research paper: title, literature review, outline, statement of purpose, revision, references.

Important definitions

Notice

Pre-information about a meeting stating the issuer, venue, date, time and agenda; sent several days in advance.

Agenda

From agendum: the list of items to be discussed in a meeting, prepared with the chairman; usually attached to the notice.

Minutes

A sequential written record of the happenings in a meeting, prepared by the secretary in consultation with the chairman, in the past tense.

Note of dissent

A written disagreement a member may submit when he does not agree with the minutes or decisions.

Research paper

A document presenting a new finding or analysis, supported by evidence and references.

Review article

An article that examines and evaluates a new book, film or development in a field.

Core concepts

Notice of a meeting

  • Organisation name at top centre; heading NOTICE.
  • State the number of the meeting (e.g. 10th meeting), body (board of directors / executive committee), date, day, time, venue and mention that the agenda is attached.
  • Issue well in advance (4–5 days) unless urgent.
  • Signed by the secretary (hard copy).

Agenda

  • Items numbered as 5.1, 5.2 (meeting number.item number).
  • First item: confirmation of the minutes of the previous meeting.
  • Last item: any other matter with the permission of the chair.
  • Prepared with the chairman; not attached for urgent or secret meetings.

Minutes

  • Written by the secretary after the meeting (a day or two later), finalised with the chairman and circulated.
  • Heading: organisation, Minutes of the Nth meeting, date and venue; chairman first, then members present; absentees listed after the secretary.
  • Language: simple, specific, past tense, often passive (“was discussed”).
  • Items follow the agenda sequence: 10.1 confirmation of minutes, 10.2 chairman’s report, 10.3 provision of laptops for office staff, … any other matter (canteen) ... “The meeting ended with a vote of thanks to the chair.”
  • Secretary signs, chairman signs on the left, with date.
  • Not everything is recorded (off-the-record remarks); minutes serve as a record for future reference.
  • An e-mail is only a medium; minutes are authentic when signed.

E-mail etiquette

  • Use proper salutation for official mail; keep it short and specific; no sarcasm.
  • Avoid capital letters (they are like shouting); use correct punctuation.
  • Limit cc to those concerned (information load).
  • Keep the signature line brief; check that the attachment is really attached.
  • Avoid long emotional e-mails; remember that e-mails can be official records.

Research papers

  • Organisations use R&D and incubation centres.
  • Types: technical paper, conference paper (presented to peers), journal paper (for publication), review article, thesis, term paper.
  • Steps: select a specific title → literature review in libraries → outline → statement of purpose → analysis and findings → conclusion → revision (language, grammar, addition, deletion) → bibliography/references.
  • Title capitalisation: main words only, not prepositions or connectives.
  • Examples: “Importance of soft skills at workplace”; “Role of media in women empowerment”.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Types of research writing
TypePurpose
Technical paperDetailed technical content, jargon allowed
Conference paperPresented to peers for feedback
Journal paperSubmitted for publication per journal style
Review articleEvaluate a new book, film or work
Thesis / term paperAcademic requirement

Examples and applications

Example

Notice of the 10th meeting of the board with agenda attached.

Example

Minutes 10.1 – confirmation of previous minutes; 10.2 chairman’s report; any other matter – canteen arrangement.

Example

E-mail sent with the attachment missing.

Comparison tables

Notice versus agenda versus minutes
DocumentTimeContent
NoticeBefore meetingDate, time, venue, issuer
AgendaBefore meetingList of items to discuss
MinutesAfter meetingRecord of what happened (past tense)

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Notice is pre-information of a meeting.
  • First agenda item: confirmation of previous minutes.
  • Last agenda item: any other matter with the permission of the chair.
  • Minutes are written in the past tense by the secretary.
  • Item numbering such as 5.1 means meeting 5, item 1.
  • Capitals in e-mail are like shouting.
  • Agenda comes from agendum.
  • Revision is the best antidote before sending a paper.
  • Absentees are listed in minutes.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

A meeting is documented through a notice (issued days ahead with date, time, venue and agenda), an agenda (numbered items beginning with confirmation of previous minutes and ending with any other matter) and minutes (a signed, past-tense, sequential record). E-mails need brevity, courtesy and care with cc, capitals and attachments. Research papers require a specific title, literature review, outline, statement of purpose, revision and references.

  1. Any other matter
  2. Confirmation of the minutes of the previous meeting
  3. Vote of thanks
  4. Chairman’s speech
Answer: B
Why: It is always the first item.

  1. Future tense
  2. Past tense
  3. Present tense
  4. Imperative

  1. Avoid capitals
  2. Check attachments
  3. Send to everyone through cc
  4. Keep it brief
Answer: A, B, D
Why: Limit cc.
Q4 T/F A notice should be issued only a day before a routine meeting.
Answer: False
Why: Give 4–5 days unless urgent.

Lecture 27: Business Letters

Week 6 · Course book PDF pages 429–449 (printed pages 428–448)

Learning objectives

  • Explain the functions of business letters and how they differ from personal letters.
  • List the ten parts of a business letter and the rules for each.
  • State the correct salutation, closing and signature conventions.
  • Recall Lord Chesterfield’s advice on politeness.

Important definitions

Business letter

A formal written communication between an organisation and a customer, firm or individual for business purposes: direct, neutral in tone, simple in language, kept as record.

Letterhead

The printed heading with organisation name, logo, address, telephone, fax and website.

Inside address

The address of the recipient, written accurately with title and full name.

Subject line

A clear one-line statement of what the letter is about.

Complimentary close

The polite closing phrase such as ‘Yours faithfully’ or ‘Yours sincerely’.

Identification marks

Initials of the dictator and the typist, e.g. BM/LKI.

Enclosures

Attached documents; the number of pages is stated in brackets.

Core concepts

Why business letters still matter

  • Maintain rapport with customers, clients and shareholders; speed up business.
  • Serve as record for future reference and quasi-MOU (memorandum of understanding) of the dealings.
  • Promote products, get feedback, expand business beyond a region.
  • The writer represents the organisation; reveals personality and ability; employers want written skills.
  • Horace Walpole's lady: “I write to you because I have nothing to do; I finish my letter because I have nothing to say.”

Business versus personal letters

  • Tone: business letters are neutral, formal, direct, cordial; no sarcasm or emotion.
  • Content: only business; personal letters (Charles Lamb, Thomas Gray, Carlyle, Robert Lynd, A. G. Gardiner, Lord Chesterfield) can talk about weather and family.
  • Language: simple; no sesquipedalian (long) words or long sentences.
  • Time is a commodity; come to the point at once.

Ten parts of a business letter

  1. Letterhead – follow your organisation’s style (centre, left or right).
  2. Date line – month first (March 10, 2017) or numeric; follow organisation practice.
  3. Reference – both ‘Our ref.’ and ‘Your ref.’.
  4. Inside address – full, accurate; use Shri/Dr/Mr/Ms; if name unknown write designation.
  5. Subject line
  6. Salutation – ‘Dear Sir/Madam’ (do not drop ‘Dear’); comma optional in full block.
  7. Body – usually 3 paragraphs: opening (direct), discussion/details, closing.
  8. Complimentary close – Yours faithfully/sincerely (no apostrophe in ‘Yours’).
  9. Signature line – typed name in brackets; leave two lines gap for signature; ‘for’ when writing on behalf.
  10. Enclosures (and identification marks) – state number of pages; e.g. cheque, receipt, order copy.

Closing lines

  • Avoid participial endings such as “Waiting to see you” or “Hoping to get your response”.
  • Use complete sentences: “I look forward to receiving your answer soon.”, “I hope to hear from you soon.”
  • Yours truly / yours cordially are less common; a student may write ‘yours obediently’. Yours faithfully (unknown name) or yours sincerely are safest.

Politeness

Lord Chesterfield: politeness is as much concerned in answering letters within a reasonable time as in returning a bow immediately. Letters are an exchange of goodwill and build relationships.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Ten parts of a business letter
No.PartKey point
1LetterheadName, logo, address, phone, fax, website
2DateMonth first or numeric per organisation
3ReferenceOur and your reference
4Inside addressAccurate name and address
5Subject lineClear topic
6SalutationDear Sir/Madam
7BodyThree paragraphs
8Complimentary closeYours faithfully/sincerely
9Signature lineName typed, space for signature
10EnclosuresList, with number of pages

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Politeness in letters — Lord Chesterfield

Answer letters within a reasonable time.

Examples and applications

Example

Subject lines: “Regarding replacement of solar panel batteries”; “Transfer of my savings account”; “Regarding the status of my article”.

Example

Inside address: Shri Rajkumar Bhatt, 25 Winter Mayor Cottage, Summer Hills, Shimla, Himachal Pradesh.

Example

Identification marks BM/LKI.

Comparison tables

Business versus personal letter
PointBusinessPersonal
ToneFormal, neutral, cordialInformal, emotional
ContentOnly businessFamily, weather, feelings
FormatFixed ten partsFree
Record valueHighLow

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • A business letter has at least ten parts.
  • Use ‘Dear Sir/Madam’.
  • Yours faithfully / sincerely; no apostrophe after ‘Yours’.
  • Reference includes ‘our reference’ and ‘your reference’.
  • Body usually has three paragraphs.
  • Identification marks: dictator’s and typist’s initials.
  • Enclosures with number of pages.
  • Lord Chesterfield on prompt reply.
  • Follow the organisation’s letterhead and date style.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W6 Q2p.22Closing of business lettersFill-in-the-blank
W6 Q4p.22Tone of business lettersFill-in-the-blank

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Business letters keep organisations and customers in touch, serve as records and reveal the writer’s skill. They are formal, neutral and direct, unlike personal letters. The standard structure has ten parts – letterhead, date, reference, inside address, subject, salutation, body, complimentary close, signature and enclosures – and requires accuracy, courtesy and prompt replies.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ A business letter has at least how many parts?
  1. 5
  2. 8
  3. 10
  4. 15
Answer: C
Why: Ten parts.
Q2 MCQ The safest complimentary close for a letter to an unnamed person is:
  1. Yours faithfully
  2. Love
  3. Cheers
  4. Bye
Answer: A

Q3 MSQ Which belong to the structure of a business letter?
  1. Subject line
  2. Enclosures

Why: There is no poem.
Q4 T/F A business letter may include weather and family news.
Answer: False
Why: It deals only with business.
Q5 Fill Both ‘our’ and ‘your’ ______ are written in a business letter.

Q6 Short How should a business letter be closed?

Lecture 28: Business Letters: Format & Style

Week 6 · Course book PDF pages 450–466 (printed pages 449–465)

Learning objectives

  • Describe the four letter formats: full block, modified block, semi-block (indented), simplified.
  • State the punctuation rules (open punctuation) and placement of date, close and signature for each.
  • Apply style rules: reader-friendly, you-approach, thank-you opening, positive language, active/passive voice, I versus we.
  • Rewrite negative letter sentences positively.

Important definitions

Full block format

All parts (date, address, salutation, subject, body, close, signature, enclosures) are aligned to the left margin; open punctuation; the most common format in organisations.

Modified block format

Address, subject, salutation and body paragraphs on the left; date, complimentary close and signature between the centre and the right margin.

Semi-block (indented) format

Like modified block but paragraphs are indented.

Simplified format

Used when the recipient’s name or gender is unknown: no salutation, no complimentary close; subject line and attention line; date and writer's name aligned on the right.

Open punctuation

No punctuation in the inside address, salutation and complimentary close; punctuation only in the body.

Attention line

A line (in capitals) directing the letter to a particular person or post, e.g. ATTENTION: Finance Officer.

Core concepts

Four formats

  1. Full block – everything on the left; no commas after date or address; double space between salutation and subject; typed name in brackets with gap for signature; designation below.
  2. Modified block – date, close and signature between right margin and centre; body blocked; sometimes a colon after ‘Dear Sir’.
  3. Semi-block – same as modified, but paragraphs indented; date in the middle or right; inside address at left.
  4. Simplified – no salutation or close; use subject and attention line; writer's name on right; like a memo; easy to type.

Always follow the practice of your organisation.

Salutation when gender is unknown

Write ‘Dear’ plus the first name (Dear Rahil, Dear Stella, Dear Stephanie).

Opening and closing

  • Reply letters begin with thanks (“Thank you for your letter dated …” / mention reference no.).
  • Come straight to the point: “Please find enclosed…”.
  • Last line should be cordial, reinforcing goodwill (you-approach); mention enclosures.

Style of business letters

  • Reader-friendly, you-approach – imagine receiving your own letter.
  • Courtesy – thank-you note connects with earlier correspondence.
  • Consideration – short, to the point; do not write in anger; do not blame the reader; avoid many negatives.
  • Be specific – mention model, colour, design in orders; specific letters get quicker replies.
  • Simple and precise words, natural tone.
  • I versus we: use ‘we’ when representing the organisation; ‘I’ for personal informal tone.
  • Voice: active for good news; passive for indirect or defensive messages.

Positive rewriting

  • “If you do not pay on time, we will lose you as a customer” → “We appreciate timely payment to continue a relationship with our customers.”
  • “Your refrigerator’s warranty has expired; service on payment” → “We welcome you as a valued customer; for goods out of warranty, service is available on payment.”
  • “We are glad we can now send your Xerox machine” → “Please find your new Xerox machine; we look forward to receiving orders from our valued customers.”
  • “Your application cannot be entertained; try next year” → “May we request you to submit a detailed resume with full educational details?”
  • “We are not responsible for damages in transportation” → “Though we take care in quality control, things can go wrong in transit; we are always ready to serve you.”

Types, classifications and characteristics

Letter formats compared
FeatureFull blockModified blockSemi-blockSimplified
DateLeftRight/centre-rightRight/middleRight
ParagraphsBlockedBlockedIndentedBlocked
SalutationYesYes (colon optional)YesNone
CloseLeftRight/centre-rightRight/centre-rightNone
Subject lineYesYesYesYes (plus attention line)

Examples and applications

Example

“Dear Rahil” when the gender is unknown.

Example

Attention: Finance Officer / Registrar.

Example

Subject: Regarding excursion (simplified format letter to the Regional Tourist Officer).

Comparison tables

Negative versus positive phrasing
NegativePositive
Your warranty has expiredService is available on payment for out-of-warranty goods
We will lose you as a customerWe appreciate timely payment
Your application cannot be entertainedWe request a detailed resume

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Four formats: full block, modified block, semi-block, simplified.
  • Full block: all on the left margin.
  • Open punctuation: none in address, salutation and close.
  • Semi-block: indented paragraphs.
  • Simplified: no salutation or close; attention line.
  • When gender unknown: Dear + first name.
  • Begin replies with thanks and reference.
  • Do not blame the reader.
  • Use ‘we’ for the organisation.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W6 Q1p.22Simplified letter formatFill-in-the-blank

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Business letters are laid out in four formats – full block (all left), modified block (date, close and signature right of centre), semi-block (indented paragraphs) and simplified (no salutation or close, with subject and attention line). Whatever the format, style must be reader-friendly: thank, be specific and brief, avoid blame and negatives, use ‘we’ for the organisation and keep a positive, cordial tone.

Lecture 29: Types of Business Letters Part I

Week 6 · Course book PDF pages 467–485 (printed pages 466–484)

Learning objectives

  • List the types of business letters.
  • Write a sales letter using the AIDA technique.
  • Draft letters of enquiry, responses to enquiries, order letters, credit letters and refusal letters.
  • Apply Sir Ernest Gowers’ advice on courtesy.

Important definitions

Sales letter

A letter written like an advertisement to promote a product and convert the reader into a customer.

AIDA

Attention, Interest, Desire, Action: the pattern for sales letters.

Letter of enquiry

A letter seeking information about a product or service; main idea first, polite command ending in a period.

Order letter

A letter placing an order; specific, direct, lists items vertically and states the payment procedure.

Credit letter

A request to purchase on credit, supported by valid credit references.

Refusal letter

A letter declining a request without breaking goodwill; gives reasons and keeps hope.

Core concepts

Types of business letters

Sales letters, letters of enquiry, placing orders, letters of credit, collection letters, refusal letters, claim letters, adjustment letters and letters of recognition. All business letters are goodwill letters.

Sir Ernest Gowers’ rule

  • If he is rude, be specially courteous.
  • If he is muddle-headed, be specially lucid.
  • If he is pig-headed, be patient.
  • If he is helpful, be appreciative.
  • If he convicts you of a mistake, acknowledge it (with gratitude).

Planning any letter

Three As: Analysis, Anticipation, Adaptation – determine your purpose, anticipate the reader's reaction, adapt and organise carefully; proofread.

Sales letter and AIDA

  • Introduction (Attention): striking statement, significant point, quotation, question, special appeal, central selling point.
  • Body (Interest and Desire): trial offers, guarantees, free samples, brochures.
  • Close (Action): positive call to action, e.g. “Send your order today and be the first in town to own our new television.”
  • Avoid exaggeration (ethical limit) and negatives (“Do not miss…”).

Letter of enquiry and its reply

  • Enquiry: main idea first; polite command ending in a period; appreciation at the end (“I appreciate this information, which will enable me to decide on 45 ACs.”); subject line should be clear.
  • Reply: good news first; avoid dull openings (“Lying before me is your letter…”); begin “Please find the information you requested…”; answer all queries sequentially; use lists or bullets; enclose brochures/coupons to promote other products.
  • Mixed messages: give good news first, place bad news before the end, close positively (e.g. accept the talk but propose the last week of March because of a conference in Sweden).

Order, credit and refusal letters

  • Order letter: first line “Please send me 45 Hitachi window ACs”; be specific; list items vertically; mention payment procedure (cheque, banking); cordial conclusion.
  • Credit letter: give sufficient valid credit references and offer additional information.
  • Refusal of credit: thank the applicant; explain (company is new); welcome cash purchases; promise future credit arrangements; keep the customer.

Types, classifications and characteristics

AIDA in a sales letter
LetterStageTechnique
AAttentionStriking opening, quotation, question
IInterestExplain features and benefits
DDesireSamples, guarantees, trial offers
AActionPrompt the reader to order

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Courtesy toward correspondents — Sir Ernest Gowers

Be courteous to the rude, lucid to the muddle-headed, patient to the pig-headed, appreciative of the helpful and gracious when proved wrong.

AIDA process — Advertising model

Attention → Interest → Desire → Action.

Examples and applications

Example

Insurance agent writes to promote another policy.

Example

Enquiry about 25 geysers, 20 room heaters or 45 ACs.

Example

Accepting a talk invitation while proposing the third week of March.

Example

Credit refusal: company is new; pay cash now.

Comparison tables

Good news versus bad news placement
PositionContent
BeginningGood news
Middle / before endBad news, softened
EndPositive, cordial close

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • AIDA = Attention, Interest, Desire, Action.
  • Three As of planning: analysis, anticipation, adaptation.
  • Gowers: if he is rude, be specially courteous.
  • Enquiry: main idea first; polite command ends with a period.
  • Reply: good news first, avoid dull openings.
  • Order letter: specific, vertical list, payment procedure.
  • Credit letter needs valid credit references.
  • Refusal letters must retain goodwill.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W6 Q2p.22Closing of business lettersFill-in-the-blank
W6 Q3p.22Subject line in business lettersFill-in-the-blank
W6 Q7p.23Refusal lettersMCQ
W6 Q10p.23Parts of a business letter (sales letter)MCQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Business letters include sales, enquiry, reply, order, credit, refusal, collection, claim, adjustment and recognition letters. Sales letters follow AIDA and avoid exaggeration and negatives. Enquiries state the main idea first; replies lead with good news and answer all queries; order letters are specific and state payment; credit requests need references; refusals thank, explain and keep hope. Sir Ernest Gowers’ advice guides tone.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ In AIDA, ‘D’ stands for:
  1. Decision
  2. Desire
  3. Direction
  4. Discount
Answer: B
Why: Attention, Interest, Desire, Action.
Q2 MCQ Where should good news be placed in a reply?
  1. End
  2. Middle
  3. Beginning
  4. Nowhere
Answer: C
Why: Announce good news first.
Q3 MSQ Which are ways to attract attention in a sales letter?
  1. Striking statement
  2. Quotation
  3. Question
  4. Threat
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Avoid threats and negatives.
Q4 T/F A refusal letter should close all ties with the customer.
Answer: False
Why: It should preserve goodwill.
Q5 Fill Gowers advises: if he is rude, be specially ______.
Answer: courteous
Why: Keep courtesy.
Q6 Short What should an order letter contain?
Answer: A specific order in the first line, itemised list, and the payment procedure with a cordial close.
Why: No roundabout expressions.

Revision checklist

  • I can expand AIDA.
  • I know Gowers’ advice.
  • I can write enquiry and reply letters.
  • I know order and credit letter rules.
  • I can write a polite refusal.

Lecture 30: Types of Business Letters Part II

Week 6 · Course book PDF pages 486–503 (printed pages 485–502)

Learning objectives

  • Write a collection letter series with a gradually firmer tone.
  • Draft a claim (complaint) letter: state the problem, explain, state the adjustment wanted, close cordially.
  • Draft an adjustment letter: good news first, active voice, explanation, empathy, appreciation.
  • Write a recommendation letter honestly and quote the closing remark on simplicity.

Important definitions

Collection letter

A letter, or series of letters, written to recover money owed; tone moves from gentle to firm.

Claim / complaint letter

A letter written to correct a wrong and obtain replacement, repair or compensation.

Adjustment letter

The organisation’s reply to a claim; conveys the remedy while restoring goodwill.

Recommendation letter

A letter nominating and justifying a person for promotion, awards or recognition, written honestly and confidentially.

Empathetic language

Language that places oneself in the customer’s position (“You are right to be disappointed…”).

Core concepts

Collection letters

  • A series (often four) because one letter may be ignored.
  • 1st: gentle reminder; 2nd: a bit harsh; 3rd: harsher and indirect; final: strong, may be intimidating but still ends cordially.
  • Sample final letter: “Despite three reminders we have not received the outstanding amount for 20 photocopiers… You are morally obliged to pay… further delay may force us to hand the matter to legal agencies… Please send the cheque and inform us by telephone by 5 p.m. tomorrow.” (deadline set)
  • Credit and collection go hand in hand; keep the customer if possible.

Claim letters

  • Receiving complaints is good: an opportunity for dialogue, relationship and product improvement and feedback.
  • Open with the problem or the action wanted (“Please send your service engineer to rectify the technical problems in 20 geysers…”).
  • Explain what went wrong without alleging; appeal to the company’s fair reputation (“we have always enjoyed your prompt service”).
  • State the inconvenience and the adjustment wanted (replacement or refund).
  • Do not blame or shout; include copies of bills, receipts and documents; suggest remedy.
  • Close cordially: “We realise mistakes in ordering and shipping sometimes occur; because we have enjoyed your prompt service, we hope you will send the ACs by …”.
  • Example: two employees with confirmed reservations were refused rooms – “would you clarify your room reservation policy for late arrivals?”

Adjustment letters

  • Complaints are more valuable than surveys.
  • Open with the good news (action taken) in active voice: “We have sent the new photocopier to your address” – not “has been sent”.
  • Explain why things went wrong; give additional information; emphasise the action wanted from the customer.
  • Address the claimant by name.
  • Avoid negative words (trouble, fault, regret, misunderstanding); do not blame the customer or staff; avoid unrealistic promises (“this will never happen again”).
  • Use sensitive, empathetic language: “We are really sorry; you are right to be disappointed with the Lumia cell phone” (Gowers: if he convicts you of a mistake, admit it).
  • Close with appreciation: “Thanks for writing. Your satisfaction is important to us… our goal is to earn your confidence.”
  • Send appreciation notes when a wrong is righted.

Recommendation letters

  • Purpose: nominate and justify the merits for promotion, awards or recognition.
  • Be honest, confidential, not biased; do not exaggerate; avoid vague or ambiguous words.
  • Mention if you are writing at someone's suggestion.
  • Evaluate communication, organisational, leadership, team and behavioural skills and dynamism.
  • Do not include negatives without evidence; keep a copy for your record.
  • Closing quotation about letters: “The art of art, the glory of expression and the sunshine of the light of letters is simplicity” (attributed to a famous poet; the transcript gives no name).

Types, classifications and characteristics

Sequence of collection letters
LetterTone
FirstGentle, courteous reminder
SecondA little firmer
ThirdHarsher, indirect
FinalStrong, deadline, mentions legal action; ends cordially
Claim, adjustment and recommendation letters

ClaimState the problem / action wantedNo blame; attach documents; request remedy
AdjustmentGood news in active voiceEmpathy; address by name; no negatives; thank
RecommendationState relationship and recommendationHonest, specific, confidential

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Admit mistakes — Sir Ernest Gowers (cited again)

If he convicts you of a mistake, admit it.

Examples and applications

Example

Example

Example

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Collection letters form a series with increasing firmness.
  • Open claim letters with the problem or action wanted.
  • Do not blame; include documents.
  • Adjustment letters open with good news in active voice.
  • Address the claimant by name.
  • Avoid trouble, fault, regret, misunderstanding and unrealistic promises.
  • Complaints are opportunities.
  • Keep a copy of recommendation letters.
  • Closing quote: simplicity is the glory of expression.

Summary

Collection letters grow firmer across a series but keep goodwill. Claim letters state the problem and desired remedy politely with documents; adjustment letters open with the good news in active voice, explain, show empathy, avoid blame and negative words and thank the customer. Recommendation letters must be honest, specific and confidential. Simplicity and cordiality underlie all letters.

Lecture 31: Report Writing

Week 7 · Course book PDF pages 504–517 (printed pages 503–516)

Learning objectives

  • State the origin and definition of a report.
  • List the attributes of a report: formal, factual, specific purpose and audience, organised, unified, illustrated, bound.
  • Explain the objectives of reports.
  • Differentiate report writing from literary writing.

Important definitions

Report

A formal communication written for a specific purpose and audience; it describes the procedures followed for collecting and analysing data and gives conclusions and, if required, recommendations.

Etymology of report

From Latin reportare = re (back) + portare (to carry): to carry information to a person who is not present. (The transcript glosses ‘re’ as ‘to send’.)

Unity and coherence

Every sentence and paragraph links to the topic and to one another, giving a smooth flow of thought.

Cross-fertilisation of ideas

Exchange of ideas across people and departments through reports.

Core concepts

Why reports matter

  • Decisions (promotion, product launch, new policy) rest on reports; a committee studies data and concludes.
  • Doctor’s medical tests are an everyday analogy of procedure, data, interpretation and conclusion.
  • A report is the “bread and butter” of an organisation; it can make or mar the fortune of both organisation and individual.

Attributes of a report

  1. Formal piece of writing.
  2. Factual – data-based, objective, no wrong data.
  3. Written for a specific audience (language adapted; computer professional versus layman).
  4. Written for a specific purpose; only when required (need-based).
  5. Systematic and well organised (beginning, discussion, end).
  6. Unity and coherence.
  7. Uses illustrations (charts, graphs, pie charts, tables).
  8. Bound properly with a cover page and title; sequence of parts.

Objectives of a report

  • Update progress of the organisation.
  • Evaluate performance (e.g. five-year data and trends).
  • Guide the organisation to a corrective path when things go wrong (committee, data, conclusions).
  • Spread information internally and externally to stakeholders and shareholders.
  • Cross-fertilise ideas and reveal gaps in reasoning.
  • Record findings and recommend action.
  • Create awareness and self-awareness (compare yourself with a promoted colleague).

Professional’s only tangible product

A report is “a professional’s only tangible product”: it often is the only contact between an engineer/professional and management, so it shows reasoning, scientific attitude, grasp, intelligence and initiative; clarity is the hallmark.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Attributes of a report
AttributeMeaning
FormalOfficial style and structure
FactualBased on accurate data
Audience-specificLanguage fits reader
Purpose-specificWritten on demand for a reason
OrganisedFixed structure and sequence
Unified and coherentSmooth flow of ideas
IllustratedCharts, tables, graphs
BoundCover page, title, order

Examples and applications

Example

Medical test reports leading to diagnosis.

Example

Committee investigating a fire loss.

Example

Comparing yourself with a promoted colleague.

Comparison tables

Report writing versus literary writing
PointReportLiterary
InitiativeWritten on demandOwn initiative
AudienceSpecificGeneral, not fixed
StructureFixedNo fixed structure
ApproachObjectiveSubjective
LanguagePlain, matter-of-factFigurative, ornamental
Aim of readerInformationPleasure
FormalityFormalSpontaneous

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Report derives from Latin reportare (re + portare).
  • A report is a formal communication for a specific purpose.
  • Reports are need-based, not self-initiated.
  • A report is a professional’s only tangible product.
  • Reports contain data, procedure, conclusions and recommendations.
  • Illustrations include charts, graphs, pie charts and tables.
  • Objectives: update, evaluate, guide, spread information, cross-fertilise ideas, record and recommend, create awareness.
  • Clarity is the hallmark of business communication.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W7 Q5p.25Technical vs literary writingTrue/False
W7 Q15p.27Technical reports vs literary writingMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

A report (from Latin reportare) is a formal, factual, systematic communication prepared on demand for a specific audience and purpose. It describes procedure and data, draws conclusions and may recommend action. Reports update, evaluate, correct, inform and create awareness. Unlike literary writing, reports are objective, structured, plain and audience-specific.

  1. Reportare
  2. Reportum
  3. Reporter
  4. Rapere
Answer: A

  2. Plain and factual
  3. Highly figurative
  4. Exaggerated

  1. Formal
  2. Factual
  3. Spontaneous
  4. Well organised
Answer: A, B, D
Why: Spontaneity belongs to literary writing.
Q4 T/F A report is written whenever the writer feels like it.
Answer: False
Why: It is written on demand.
Q5 Fill A report is a professional’s only ______ product.
Answer: tangible
Why: It shows his abilities.
Q6 Short State two objectives of a report.

Revision checklist

  • I know the etymology and definition.
  • I can list the attributes.
  • I can list the objectives.
  • I can differentiate report from literary writing.
  • I recall ‘tangible product’.

Lecture 32: Types of Reports

Week 7 · Course book PDF pages 518–528 (printed pages 517–527)

Learning objectives

  • Classify reports by length, form, nature, frequency and purpose.
  • Differentiate short (informal) and technical (formal) reports.
  • Differentiate informative and analytical (interpretive) reports.
  • Explain routine, management and special reports.
  • Recall the bathing-suit quotation by Sherring and Christian.

Important definitions

Short report

An informal, brief report (one page to about 10 pages) with an authorisation, a problem, a conclusion and, only if asked, a recommendation; includes letter reports and memo reports.

Technical report

A formal, documented report with prefatory material, deep and systematic discussion, data analysis, illustrations and back matter.

Informative report

A report that presents information as it is, without analysis or recommendation (e.g. inspection, lab, attendance reports).

Analytical (interpretive) report

A report that interprets, analyses and evaluates data, reaches conclusions and may recommend action.

Routine report

A report written regularly at fixed intervals on a fixed proforma, dealing with regular matters (finance, production, sales, inventory).

Management report

A non-technical, short report for top managers, with restricted language, journalistic tone and more illustrations.

Special report

A report written during a critical juncture or aberration, with no standard format and time pressure.

ACR

Annual Confidential Report: a routine report evaluating an employee’s performance.

Appendix

Back matter where supporting material that would block the flow of the report is placed.

Core concepts

Ways of classifying reports

  • By length: short and long.
  • By form: written and oral.
  • By subject / nature: management and technical.
  • By frequency: routine and special.
  • By purpose / function: informative and analytical (interpretive).

Short reports

  • Informal; e.g. a school picnic report; one page to 5–6 or 8–10 pages.
  • Needs a letter of authorisation and a statement of the problem; then conclusion.
  • Recommendation only if asked: conclusions rest on data, recommendations bring in your own thoughts.
  • Letter reports (salutation, complimentary close, informal tone) and memo reports (no salutation or close) are short reports.
  • Be concise, plain, direct; ask ‘why am I writing this?’; review before sending; move blocking material to the appendix.

Technical reports

  • Proper documentation: beginning, prefatory material, discussion, analysis, illustrations, supporting material.
  • Very detailed, logical, systematic discussion to analyse a problem and provide a solution.
  • Data must be factual and its sources authentic: wrong data leads to wrong decisions that harm the institution.
  • Data is collected, analysed, interpreted and sampled to reach a conclusion (and recommendation, if required).
  • Analysis is divided into sections and subsections (e.g. pollution, garbage disposal) and must lead logically to the conclusion.
  • Supporting material (sources, methods) goes to the back matter.
  • Divided into informative and analytical.

Informative reports

  • Simply convey information as it is, not as it should be; no analysis, usually shorter.
  • Often a fixed proforma with ticks; little language use; the writer does not recommend action.
  • Examples: market survey, laboratory report, inventory, inspection, traffic observation.
  • Also: periodic (weekly attendance, monthly inspection), readership surveys, employee booklets, prospectus, website information, committee reports, policy statements, sales, credit and production reports.

Analytical (interpretive) reports

  • Interpret, analyse and evaluate data; investigate a problem (e.g. a product with deficiencies) and may recommend action.
  • Writer must be unbiased and keep personal bias out.
  • Elaborate and descriptive: may run 30–35 pages.
  • Example: a Pay Commission examines situations, recommends, and government decides.
  • More objective and scientific; analysis must serve the common good.
  • Busy readers read the illustrations (tables, charts, graphs), so use relevant visuals.

Routine reports

  • Regular, at fixed intervals (fortnight, month, year); fixed proforma; little language.
  • Deal with finance, production, sales, performance, inventory; keep internal people and outside agencies informed.
  • Examples: annual reports, project progress proforma (every six months), lab report, stock verification, inspection reports (e.g. car makers withdrawing or adding features), ACR (annual confidential report).

Management reports

  • Non-technical, short, for top people; more illustrations, little explanation.
  • Language restricted, tone at times journalistic.
  • Managers read the conclusion and may refer to the technical report for detail.
  • Writer must know the readers’ background, age, taste and preferences.

Special reports

  • Written only in critical hours or after a mishap; low frequency; time limit.
  • No standard format; writer relies on caution and felicity of language.
  • Writer must be extra vigilant, diligent, intelligent and careful because action will follow.

Key quotation

Sherring and Christian: “A report is like a bathing suit; it covers everything that has to be covered, but nothing more.” (No unnecessary or irrelevant material.)

Types, classifications and characteristics

Classification of reports
BasisTypes

Examples of informative and routine reports

Theories, principles, models and contributors

A report covers everything that has to be covered, but nothing more.

Examples and applications

Example

School picnic report (short report).

Example

Report on pollution or garbage disposal (technical).

Example

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Reports are classified by length, form, subject, frequency and function.
  • Letter and memo reports are short reports.
  • Do not recommend unless asked; conclusion is fine.
  • Technical report needs authentic data and sources.
  • Technical reports are informative or analytical.
  • Analytical reports can run 30–35 pages.
  • Routine reports follow fixed proformas at fixed intervals.
  • ACR = Annual Confidential Report.
  • Management reports are non-technical, short, illustrated.
  • Special reports have no standard format and are rare.
  • Bathing-suit quote by Sherring and Christian.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W7 Q7p.26Types of reportsMCQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Reports vary by length (short or long), form (written or oral), subject (management or technical), frequency (routine or special) and function (informative or analytical). Short reports are informal; technical reports are formal and documented and must rest on authentic data. Informative reports present facts; analytical reports interpret and may recommend. Routine reports are periodic and proforma-based, management reports are short and non-technical, and special reports arise in critical situations. A report should cover everything necessary, but nothing more.

Lecture 33: Strategies for Report Writing Part I

Week 7 · Course book PDF pages 529–545 (printed pages 528–544)

Learning objectives

  • List the steps in preparing to write a report: purpose and scope, audience, data collection, organising material, outline.
  • Explain terms of reference (TOR) and why knowing the reader matters.
  • Distinguish primary and secondary sources of data.
  • Evaluate personal observation: process, precautions, limitations.
  • State the advantages and disadvantages of telephonic interviews.

Important definitions

Terms of reference (TOR)

Instructions from the person assigning the report stating the problem, scope, readers and what is to be done.

Scope

The extent of the problem the report covers and who will read it.

Primary source

First-hand data from personal observation and investigation.

Secondary source

Existing data used to verify or supplement: library, Internet, internal records.

Personal observation

Seeing with a purpose; the word is explained in the lecture as ob + servation.

Controlled observation

Observation where the subject knows or is prepared in advance.

Uncontrolled observation

Observation where the subject is unaware of being observed.

Primary reader

The boss or person who assigned the report.

Secondary reader

Other readers, including those outside the organisation with relevant expertise.

Telephonic interview

Collecting data by planned telephone questioning.

Core concepts

Steps before writing a report

  1. Identify purpose and scope.
  2. Determine the audience.
  3. Choose the right method of data collection.
  4. Organise the material.
  5. Make an outline.

Purpose, scope and TOR

  • Ask: why am I writing, and who gave me the task?
  • The assigner supplies the terms of reference: problem, scope, readers, what to cover.
  • Example: Mess Secretary asked by the Chief Warden to report on mess functioning (timing, food quality, vegetarian meals, sweets).

Knowing the audience

  • Readers: superior officers, colleagues, counterparts, subordinates, people of other organisations, shareholders, customers, the public. (In the example: Chief Warden, Dean Students’ Welfare, Deputy Director, Director.)
  • Know their background, your relationship with them and their attitude (favourable or hostile); this guides language and presentation.
  • Primary reader = boss; secondary readers = others, including outsiders with expertise.
  • Do not be influenced by the assigner’s expectations: work on the data to stay unbiased.

Sources of data

  • Primary: personal observation and investigation (verified later).
  • Secondary: library, Internet, internal records.
  • Ways of collection: personal observation, telephonic interviews, personal interviews, questionnaires.
  • Use specific words in search engines; avoid straying to irrelevant sites; verify authenticity with other sources without wasting time.

Personal observation: how to do it

  • Fix beforehand what data you want (a habit, trend, quality).
  • Decide between controlled and uncontrolled observation; people change behaviour when they know they are watched.
  • Do not display pen and paper; note afterwards from memory when observing people (open note-taking is fine for traffic counts).
  • Ignore sudden irrelevant changes (posture, gesture); stay focused.
  • Avoid prejudice and see with the ‘mind’s eye’; first impressions (e.g. a tired-looking speaker) may be wrong.
  • Covert recording that is later disclosed creates problems; be specific, not general.

Limitations of observation

  • Cannot be the only method; gives first-hand but not necessarily representative data.
  • Can report behaviour but not interpret it.
  • Biased by the observer; one day may not represent others (e.g. rainy Sunday traffic).
  • Slow, costly, and subjects may be unavailable.

Telephonic interviews

Advantages: accessible anywhere, saves time and travel, wide geographic reach, low refusal rate.

Disadvantages: needs meticulous planning and specific questions; casual talk differs from data-collecting talk; detailed data is hard to get; no sight of non-verbal cues (video calls help only partly); cannot judge age or nationality or if the respondent hides information; limited time, so follow-up is hard; respondents may give wrong information or send someone else.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Sources of data
Source typeExamples
PrimaryPersonal observation, investigation
SecondaryLibrary, Internet, internal records
Methods of data collection (Part I)
MethodKey point
Personal observationFirst-hand; cannot interpret; observer bias
Telephonic interviewWide reach; limited depth
Personal interviewDiscussed next lecture
QuestionnaireDiscussed next lecture

Examples and applications

Example

Mess Secretary’s report for the Chief Warden.

Example

Observing Sunday traffic flow.

Example

Misjudging a tired-looking speaker.

Example

Searching for air pollution data.

PointPrimarySecondary
NatureFirst-handExisting material
MethodsObservation, investigationLibrary, Internet, records
RoleStarting dataVerification and support
Controlled versus uncontrolled observation
PointControlledUncontrolled
Subject aware?Yes, in advanceNo
RiskSubject prepares or hides thingsLess altered behaviour

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Preparation: purpose and scope, audience, data method, organisation, outline.
  • TOR = terms of reference.
  • Know the attitude of readers.
  • Primary sources: observation and investigation.
  • Secondary sources: library, Internet, internal records.
  • Observation reports behaviour but cannot interpret it.
  • Do not recommend or take sides under the boss’s influence.
  • Telephonic interviews: low refusal, wide reach, limited detail.

QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W7 Q1p.25Primary and secondary sourcesTrue/False

W7 Q3p.25Interview as a data-collection methodTrue/False

W7 Q10p.26Personal observationMCQ
W7 Q11p.26Preparatory steps for report writingMSQ
W7 Q12p.27Observation: advantages and disadvantagesMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Preparing a report starts with the purpose and scope defined in the terms of reference, then analysis of the audience and its attitude, selection of data-collection methods, organisation of material and an outline. Data come from primary sources (personal observation, investigation) and secondary sources (library, Internet, internal records). Observation gives first-hand data but is biased, slow and cannot interpret behaviour; telephonic interviews reach many people cheaply but lack depth and non-verbal cues.

Practice questions

  1. Terms of Reference
  2. Time of Report
  3. Type of Research
  4. Tone of Reader

  1. Personal observation
  2. Investigation
  3. Library
  4. Eyewitness view

  1. No non-verbal cues
  2. Limited detail
  3. Wide reach
  4. Respondent may mislead
Answer: A, B, D

Q4 T/F Observation can both report and interpret behaviour reliably.
Answer: False
Why: It reports but cannot interpret.
Q5 Fill The boss who assigns the report is the ______ reader.

Lecture 34: Strategies for Report Writing Part II

Week 7 · Course book PDF pages 546–560 (printed pages 545–559)

Learning objectives

  • Define a personal interview and list its advantages and precautions.
  • Explain questionnaires, their advantages and disadvantages.
  • List the types of questions: closed, rating, open-ended, multiple choice.
  • Apply guidelines for framing a questionnaire and covering letter.
  • Recall Freud’s and Ruskin’s quotations.

Important definitions

Personal interview

A process of seeking information directly through a face-to-face conversation with a purpose.

Questionnaire

A set of standardised questions sent to many respondents to collect data without travel.

Closed question

A question with a fixed answer such as yes/no (e.g. ‘Do you smoke?’).

Rating question

A question asking the respondent to rate on a scale such as 1 to 10.

Open-ended question

A question allowing respondents to express their own opinion in their own words.

Multiple-choice question

A question with (typically four) offered answers.

Leading question

A question that suggests the expected answer or is embarrassing or tricky.

Covering letter

A letter accompanying a questionnaire explaining its purpose and assuring confidentiality.

Representative sample

A group of respondents whose answers reflect the whole population.

Core concepts

Why other methods are needed

Observation and telephonic interviews have limits and their data may not be representative, so data should be verified through personal interviews and questionnaires.

Personal interview: advantages

  • Face-to-face communication is better because non-words (dress, bearing, gestures, postures, silence, pauses) are available.
  • Gathers attitudes and feelings; faces reveal emotions.
  • Learns from people with intimate knowledge of the problem; verifies and supplements observation, questionnaire or telephone data.
  • The interviewer has control and can bring the interviewee back to the point.
  • Questions can be interpreted and ambiguities cleared; replies are more representative (people may lie on the phone but less so in person).
  • Gives first-hand impressions and immediate feedback.

Freud: “He who has eyes to see and ears to hear can convince himself that no mortal can keep a secret. If his lips are silent, he chatters with his fingertips; betrayal oozes out of him at every pore.”

Personal interview: tips and disadvantages

  • Costly and needs travel.
  • Seek an appointment in advance, state the purpose; be courteous and tactful.
  • Standardise questions; limit time (busy professionals).
  • Jot notes if allowed; recording is possible.
  • Do not argue; give the interviewee room; speak less, listen carefully; take a subordinate role.
  • Avoid embarrassing questions that affect the interviewee’s image; frame questions subtly.

Questionnaire: advantages

  • Saves travel, time and money; reaches people in different locations with one set of questions.
  • Convenient for respondents, who can look up information at leisure.
  • No interviewer bias; respondents are fearless and may stay anonymous.
  • Standardised questions; follow-up questions can follow closed ones.
  • Return postage can be prepaid.

Questionnaire: disadvantages

  • Low response; people decide on likes and dislikes, so the sample may not be representative.
  • Uncertainty; questions left unanswered or answered inadequately or meaninglessly.
  • Reluctance on confidential or complex matters.
  • No control over the situation.
  • Cost per mailing is low but cost per return is high.

Framing a questionnaire

  • Keep it short, specific and attractive; too long means unanswered.
  • Two parts: personal information first, then the main questions.
  • Use simple wording; arrange logically with coherence.
  • Place tricky questions in the middle, not at the beginning or the end.
  • Avoid leading and embarrassing questions (e.g. about alcohol brand, newspaper read).
  • Be free of grammatical errors.
  • Include a covering letter stating purpose and assuring confidentiality.

Ask direct questions: ‘Do you smoke?’ rather than ‘What is your reaction to smoking?’.

Closing quotation

John Ruskin: “To be able to ask a question clearly is two-thirds of the way to getting it answered.”

After collection: analyse, organise, outline, then write.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Types of questions in a questionnaire
TypeDescriptionExample / use

RatingScale such as 1–10Rate the service
Open-endedOwn opinion, space providedReveals attitudes and trends
Multiple choiceChoose from four optionsFixed alternatives
Methods of data collection

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Freud on body language — Sigmund Freud

No one can keep a secret; the body betrays emotions.

Ruskin on questions — John Ruskin

A clearly asked question is two-thirds answered.

Examples and applications

Smoking research: ‘Do you smoke?’

Example

Tricky or leading: ‘Do you drink alcohol?’, ‘Which brand?’

Example

Pre-paid return postage to improve response.

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Personal interview = conversation with a purpose.
  • Non-verbal cues are available in personal interviews.
  • Do not argue in an interview; listen more.
  • Seek appointments in advance.
  • Questionnaires save time and travel and allow anonymity.
  • Types: closed, rating, open-ended, multiple choice.

Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W7 Q4p.25Personal interviewTrue/False
W7 Q14p.27QuestionnairesMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Personal interviews give reliable, verifiable, first-hand data with non-verbal cues and interviewer control but are costly; the interviewer should plan, listen, avoid arguments and embarrassing questions. Questionnaires reach many respondents cheaply and anonymously but risk low and unrepresentative responses. A good questionnaire is short, simple, logical, error-free, uses closed, rating, open-ended and multiple-choice questions, avoids leading questions, places tricky ones in the middle and carries a covering letter. Ruskin: a clearly asked question is two-thirds answered.

Answer: B
Why: Face-to-face reveals non-words.

  2. Middle
  3. Last

  2. Rating

Answer: A, B, C

Lecture 35: Evaluation and Organization of Data

Week 7 · Course book PDF pages 561–575 (printed pages 560–574)

Learning objectives

  • Explain why collected data must be evaluated and organised.
  • Describe probability and non-probability sampling and their types.
  • Apply note-making techniques.
  • State the principles of an outline: indenting, numbering, coordination, subordination, parallel construction, logical ordering.
  • Prepare a decimal-numbered outline.

Important definitions

Sampling

Selecting a representative part of the data so that conclusions can be drawn for the whole.

Probability sampling

Sampling in which every item has a known chance of selection: random, systematic or stratified.

Random sampling

Selecting items purely by chance so that every item has an equal chance; the ‘purest’ way.

Systematic sampling

Choosing records by a fixed system, e.g. every ninth record.

Stratified sampling

Dividing the data into strata (e.g. men/women, students/teachers, children/adults) and sampling from each.

Non-probability sampling

Sampling by convenience or judgment of the analyst.

Outline

A rough, tentative, short structure of the whole report; a distribution table showing which information goes to which section.

Coordination

Main head and sub-heads of the same level are of equal importance and belong to the same subject.

Subordination

Sub-heads are less important than, and visibly lower than, the main head (indent and typeface).

Parallel grammatical construction

Headings of the same level use the same grammatical form (all nouns, all phrases).

Note cards

Cards with sections and sub-sections used to sort jumbled data.

Core concepts

From data to report

Collected data is huge and haphazard. Steps: evaluate (validate, sample) → make notes → organise → outline → write.

Sampling methods

  • Probability: random, systematic (e.g. every ninth record), stratified (groups by criteria).
  • Non-probability: convenience, judgment.
  • Aim: representative data so the report can lead to a solution.

Making notes

  • Notes later create the outline; report writing is a systematic process.
  • Place quoted words in quotation marks and give credit.
  • Add a small summary; use abbreviations (shorthand is abandoned now).
  • Use note cards with sections and sub-sections.
  • Write clearly so you can read your own notes.
  • Record observations, facts and trends under suitable categories.

Principles of outline

  1. Indenting – sub-topics shifted to the right, main topics at left.
  2. Numbering – Roman, alphabetical or decimal (preferred: 1, 2, 3.1, 3.2.1); do not go beyond the third level.
  3. Coordination – no new matter unrelated to the head.
  4. Subordination – sub-heads visibly less important (bold head, normal sub-heads).
  5. Parallel grammatical construction – all nouns/phrases/adjectives, not a mix.
  6. Logical ordering – a sequence such as introduction, history, discussion, conclusion, recommendation, bibliography; stick to the pattern chosen.
  7. Phrasing – use phrases, not full sentences.

Sample outlines

Advertising in India: 1 Introduction; 2 History; 3 Types → 3.1 Print, 3.2 Electronic → 3.2.1 (sub-type).

Modes of presentation: reading from manuscript, impromptu, memorisation (coordinated, no new matter).

Benefits of advertising: economic, political, moral, religious, cultural (all adjectives; not ‘politics’ or ‘moralise’).

Advantages of nationalisation: mobilisation of national resources, promotion of agricultural production… (nouns, not ‘to promote’).

Role of the outline

  • A tailor’s analogy: cut cloth into pieces, then stitch into one whole.
  • Outline is a tentative guide that reminds you what is missing or misplaced; the writer may change it.
  • “Once the outline is ready, half the job is done.”
  • Next: structure (anatomy) of the report.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Sampling methods
CategoryTypes
ProbabilityRandom, systematic, stratified
Non-probabilityConvenience, judgment
Principles of outline
PrincipleMeaning
IndentingSub-topics to the right
NumberingDecimal preferred; max three levels
CoordinationSame-level items belong together
SubordinationLower items less prominent
ParallelismSame grammatical form
Logical orderingSystematic sequence

Examples and applications

Example

Outline for ‘Advertising in India’ with decimal numbering 3.2.1.

Example

Parallel list: economic, political, moral, religious, cultural.

Example

Stratified groups: men/women, student/teacher, child/adult.

Example

Tailor cutting and stitching cloth.

Comparison tables

PointProbabilityNon-probability
BasisChance or systemConvenience or judgment
TypesRandom, systematic, stratifiedConvenience, judgment
RepresentativenessHigherDepends on analyst

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Evaluate data by sampling.
  • Probability sampling: random, systematic, stratified.
  • Systematic example: every ninth record.
  • Non-probability: convenience, judgment.
  • Quote with quotation marks.
  • Decimal numbering preferred.
  • Do not go beyond three levels.
  • Parallel grammatical construction in headings.
  • Outline uses phrases, not sentences.
  • Outline is a tentative guide: half the job done.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W7 Q6p.25Sampling methodsMCQ
W7 Q11p.26Preparatory steps for report writingMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Collected data must be evaluated through sampling (probability: random, systematic, stratified; non-probability: convenience, judgment), noted carefully with quotation marks, abbreviations and note cards, and organised into an outline. The outline follows the principles of indenting, numbering (decimal, up to three levels), coordination, subordination, parallel grammatical construction and logical ordering, uses phrases rather than sentences and serves as a tentative guide; a good outline means half the report is done.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ Which is a probability sampling method?
  1. Convenience
  2. Judgment
  3. Stratified
  4. Casual
Answer: C
Why: Random, systematic and stratified.
Q2 MCQ The preferred numbering system for outlines is:
  1. Roman
  2. Alphabetical
  3. Decimal
  4. Mixed
Answer: C
Why: It avoids confusion.
Q3 MSQ Principles of outline include:
  1. Coordination
  2. Subordination
  3. Parallelism
  4. Rhyme
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Rhyme is irrelevant.
Q4 T/F An outline should be written in full sentences.
Answer: False
Why: Phrases are used.
Q5 Fill Divisions in an outline should not go beyond the ______ level.
Answer: third
Why: Else it becomes sketchy.
Q6 Short What is parallel grammatical construction? Give an example.
Answer: Using the same grammatical form for headings of one level, e.g. economic, political, moral, religious, cultural (all adjectives).
Why: Avoid mixing ‘politics’ or ‘moralise’.

Revision checklist

  • I know sampling types.
  • I can make notes properly.
  • I can list outline principles.
  • I can draft a decimal outline.
  • I know parallelism.

Lecture 36: Structure of Reports: Part-I

Week 8 · Course book PDF pages 576–592 (printed pages 575–591)

Learning objectives

  • State the three structural divisions of a technical report.
  • List the items of the prefatory material (front matter) in order.
  • Describe the cover page, title page, frontispiece, copyright notice and forwarding letter.
  • Differentiate preface and foreword; write an acknowledgement.
  • Prepare a table of contents and list of illustrations; differentiate abstract, summary and executive summary.

Important definitions

Prefatory material (front matter)

Items preceding the main text of a report: cover, frontispiece, title page, copyright notice, forwarding letter, preface, acknowledgement, table of contents, list of illustrations, abstract.

Cover page

Outer page on thick paper bearing ‘Report on’, title, report number, writer, ‘prepared for’, organisation and month/year.

Title page

Page after the cover with the title (and subtitle), prepared for/by, ‘approved by’ and organisation and date.

Frontispiece

A thin protective page, or illustration, facing the title page, giving a window display of the report to create curiosity; used in bound reports.

Copyright notice

A statement on the back of the title page such as ‘© Amit Jain 2017. All rights reserved. No part of this report may be reproduced…without written permission’.

Forwarding letter / letter of transmittal

A letter by the controlling officer forwarding the report through proper channel; includes authorisation, subject, purpose, scope, limitations and acknowledgement.

Preface

The writer’s own first page introducing the subject, work done, significance and organisation; kept short.

Foreword

A recommendation written by an expert or celebrity, not the author.

Acknowledgement

A separate page thanking those who helped, in simple polite language, in 2–3 paragraphs.

Table of contents

Division of the report into parts with page numbers.

List of illustrations

Separate list of tables, charts, graphs with numbers and pages.

Abstract

A very short condensed statement of the focus of the report, with key words.

Summary

A longer account (up to 1.5–2 pages) of the entire report.

Executive summary

The whole report in a nutshell for top managers: purpose, objectives, methodology, findings, recommendations.

Core concepts

Three parts of a technical report

Prefatory material (front matter) → Main body (text) → Back matter. Like a letter’s introduction, middle and end. Check the TOR for what the report needs; short reports need not follow the full structure. Analogy: a palatial building built brick by brick on a foundation.

Items of front matter (order)

  1. Cover
  2. Frontispiece
  3. Title page
  4. Copyright notice
  5. Forwarding letter
  6. Preface
  7. Acknowledgement
  8. Table of contents
  9. List of illustrations
  10. Abstract (or summary)

Cover page

  • Thick paper, usually white (coloured allowed); many organisations have a proforma.
  • Contents: ‘Report on’, title (key words; capitals or initial capitals except prepositions), report number (right), writer’s name (employee or enrollment number), ‘Prepared for’ (person), organisation, month and year.
  • Number and date help later researchers.

Title page

  • Gives title and subtitle (e.g. ‘Quality Health Management in Hospitals: A Case Study’).
  • Prepared for / prepared by; ‘Approved by’ at lower left with the controlling officer’s name.
  • Studies: descriptive (demand of two-wheelers), correlational, causal (effect of pollution on mankind) help in choosing titles.

Frontispiece, copyright, forwarding letter

  • Frontispiece: only bound, thick reports; protects drawings and charts; now often dropped.
  • Copyright notice: on the back of the title page with year and ‘All rights reserved’.
  • Forwarding letter: in business-letter format; certifies the report, states authorisation and scope; not mandatory.

Preface versus foreword; acknowledgement

  • Preface: by the author; foreword: by an expert as recommendation.
  • Keep preface short; put thanks on a separate page.
  • Acknowledgement: 2–3 paragraphs, categorise helpers, vary phrases (“I am thankful to…”, “I shall be failing in my duty if…”), avoid jargon and metaphor.

Table of contents and illustrations

  • Differs from outline by page numbers; title in the centre/top.
  • Order: Preface, Acknowledgement, Abstract (in italics, Roman numerals), then chapters from Introduction with Arabic numbering, then appendix and bibliography.
  • Sub-divisions are not essential.
  • List of illustrations gives number, title and page of tables and charts (graphs, pie charts) for readers who scan visuals.

Abstract, summary, executive summary

  • Abstract: very short, key words, main focus; as at conferences.
  • Summary: longer, covers the report; up to 1.5–2 pages.
  • Executive summary: whole report in a nutshell: reason for writing, objectives, findings, methodology, recommendations; topic sentences in paragraphs.
  • Language: neutral, scientific, detached, no flowery words or jargon; it prepares the reader for the report.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Structure of a technical report
PartAlternative nameContents
1Prefatory material / front matterCover to abstract
2Main body / textIntroduction to recommendation
3Back matterAppendix, bibliography, glossary, index
Front matter items
ItemKey point
CoverReport on, title, number, writer, date
FrontispieceThin protective page; optional
Title pageSubtitle, approved by
CopyrightAll rights reserved
Forwarding letterThrough channel; not mandatory
PrefaceAuthor’s note; short
AcknowledgementThanks; simple language
Table of contentsPage numbers
List of illustrationsTables and charts
AbstractBrief, key words

Examples and applications

Example

Cover: ‘Quality Management in Service Industries’ with report number and month/year.

Example

Title page with subtitle ‘A Case Study’.

Example

Copyright: Amit Jain © 2017 – All rights reserved.

Example

Forwarding letter to Nilesh Mathur.
