Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Report has three parts: front matter, main body, back matter.
  • Front matter items in order: cover, frontispiece, title page, copyright, forwarding letter, preface, acknowledgement, contents, illustrations, abstract.
  • ‘Approved by’ goes at lower left of the title page.
  • Copyright notice on the back of the title page.
  • Forwarding letter is also called letter of transmittal.
  • Preface by author; foreword by expert.
  • Table of contents has page numbers; outline does not.
  • Preface, acknowledgement and abstract use Roman numerals/italics.
  • Abstract is shorter than summary.
  • Executive summary may go up to 1.5–2 pages.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W8 Q2p.28Introduction of a reportTrue/False
W8 Q6p.28Executive summaryMCQ
W8 Q13p.30Introduction of a reportMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

A technical report has front matter, main body and back matter. The front matter contains the cover, frontispiece, title page, copyright notice, forwarding letter, preface, acknowledgement, table of contents, list of illustrations and abstract or summary. The cover carries the report number, title, writer, recipient, organisation and date; the title page adds the subtitle and ‘approved by’. The preface is by the author, the foreword by an expert. The table of contents carries page numbers. Abstracts and summaries use neutral, concise language.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ The three parts of a technical report are:
  1. Front matter, body, back matter
  2. Title, text, index
  3. Cover, letter, bibliography
  4. Preface, summary, appendix
Answer: A
Why: Prefatory material, main elements and back matter.
Q2 MCQ A foreword is written by:
  1. The author
  2. An expert
  3. The printer
  4. The publisher
Answer: B
Why: It recommends the work.
Q3 MCQ The ‘approved by’ line appears on the:
  1. Cover top
  2. Title page, lower left
  3. Index
  4. Appendix
Answer: B
Why: Controlling officer’s approval.
Q4 MSQ Which belong to the front matter?
  1. Abstract
  2. Acknowledgement
  3. Bibliography
  4. Copyright notice
Answer: A, B, D
Why: Bibliography is back matter.
Q5 T/F A table of contents contains page numbers while an outline does not.
Answer: True
Why: That is the difference.
Q6 Fill Another name for a forwarding letter is a letter of ______.
Answer: transmittal
Why: It sends the report through channel.
Q7 Short Differentiate abstract and executive summary.
Answer: An abstract is a very brief statement of the report’s focus with key words; an executive summary is a fuller digest including objectives, methodology, findings and recommendations.
Why: Executive summary suits busy managers.

Revision checklist

  • I can name the three parts.
  • I can list front matter in order.
  • I know cover and title page contents.
  • I can differentiate preface and foreword.
  • I know abstract vs summary.

Lecture 37: Structure of Reports: Part-II

Week 8 · Course book PDF pages 593–608 (printed pages 592–607)

Learning objectives

  • List the four parts of the main body: introduction, discussion, conclusion, recommendation.
  • Describe the contents of an introduction.
  • Explain how to write discussion, conclusions and recommendations.
  • List the items of back matter: appendix, list of references, bibliography, glossary, index.
  • Differentiate bibliography and list of references; APA and MLA styles.

Important definitions

Introduction

First part of the main body: background, problem, authorisation, scope, limitations, sources and methods, definitions of terms and report preview.

Discussion

The part where data are analysed, interpreted and evaluated to lead logically to the conclusion.

Conclusion

Logical inferences and judgments based only on the analysis, with no new information.

Recommendation

Feasible action suggested on the basis of the conclusions, written only when asked.

Back matter

Supporting material after the main text: appendices, references, bibliography, glossary, index.

Appendix

Supporting material (questionnaire, interview sheets, charts) placed at the end; labelled Appendix A, B, C.

List of references

Only the works cited in the text; mandatory.

Bibliography

Complete alphabetical list of works consulted, including those not quoted, for further reading.

Glossary

Alphabetical list of technical terms with meanings.

Index

Alphabetical list of topics and sub-topics with page numbers, for very long reports.

APA style

Citation style common in sciences; year follows the author’s name.

MLA style

Citation style common in humanities/social sciences; year given near the end.

Core concepts

Four parts of the main body

  1. Introduction
  2. Discussion
  3. Conclusion
  4. Recommendation (if asked)

Use decimal numbering; Introduction is 1, Discussion 2 and so on.

Contents of the introduction

  • Historical background and statement of the problem (about the organisation, sector, or issue).
  • Authorisation and terms of reference; hypothesis or concept of the problem.
  • Scope – who or what is affected.
  • Limitations of the findings and methods.
  • Sources and methods of data collection.
  • Definitions of terms for non-expert readers (e.g. the boss).
  • Report preview – division of the report into sections.

Discussion

  • ‘The entire drama takes place’: analysis, interpretation and evaluation of data.
  • Divide into sections and sub-sections with decimal numbers.
  • Present findings in tables, graphs and charts that are in sync with the text; useful for busy readers.
  • Example: absenteeism – the data may show personal problems, unsuitable unit or poor communication with bosses, not mere insincerity.
  • Raise questions and answer them with data.

Conclusion

  • Based entirely on the analysis; no new information.
  • Brief (1–2 pages even for a 30-page report); may be numbered.
  • Busy readers read only conclusions and illustrations.

Recommendation

  • Give only when asked.
  • Must be feasible, not aggressive or ‘the only solution’, no personality; maintain equilibrium between truth and feasibility.
  • Infeasible or invented recommendations make the report fail.

Back matter items

  • Appendices (A, B, C) – questionnaires, interview sheets, charts that would block the flow; only interested readers consult them.
  • List of references – works cited; bibliography – all works consulted; glossary; index.
  • Few references can be footnotes on the same page; many go to the end.

Documentation styles

  • Order of entry: author (surname first in bibliography; full name in footnote), year (APA: after author), title (italic), edition, place, publisher, year (MLA: at end), pages.
  • Types: one, two, three authors, corporate author, e-mail, newspaper article, essay in an edited book, abstract.
  • Example MLA: Gibaldi, Joseph. MLA Handbook for Writers of Research Papers. New York: Prentice Hall, year.
  • Entries are alphabetical; follow your organisation’s style (italics or underline).

Glossary and index

  • Glossary: alphabetical technical terms; essential when many terms (more than 10–20).
  • Index: alphabetical; main topics and sub-topics; only for extremely long reports.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Main body of a report
PartFunction
IntroductionBackground, problem, scope, limitations, methods, definitions, preview
DiscussionAnalysis and interpretation of data
ConclusionInferences based on analysis
RecommendationFeasible action; only if asked
Back matter
ItemContent
AppendixSupporting material
List of referencesWorks cited
BibliographyWorks consulted
GlossaryTechnical terms
IndexTopics with pages

Examples and applications

Example

Absenteeism report: causes found through data.

Example

Unemployment report in a technology or automobile sector.

Example

MLA entry for Joseph Gibaldi.

Example

Appendices A, B and C for questionnaires.

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Main body: introduction, discussion, conclusion, recommendation.
  • Introduction gives background, authorisation, scope, limitations, methods, definitions and preview.
  • Conclusion contains no new information.
  • Recommend only when asked and keep it feasible.
  • Appendices hold supporting material labelled A, B, C.
  • List of references = cited works; bibliography = consulted works.
  • APA: year after author; MLA: year at end.
  • Bibliography is alphabetical, surname first.
  • Glossary and index are alphabetical.
  • Index only for extremely long reports.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W8 Q8p.29Bibliography vs referencesMCQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

The main body has introduction (background, authorisation, scope, limitations, methods, definitions, preview), discussion (analysis and interpretation), conclusion (based on analysis, no new data) and recommendation (only if asked and feasible). The back matter holds appendices, list of references, bibliography, glossary and index. A list of references contains only cited works; a bibliography lists all consulted works. APA and MLA differ in the position of the year.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ No new information should appear in the:
  1. Introduction
  2. Discussion
  3. Conclusion
  4. Appendix
Answer: C
Why: Conclusions rest on analysis.
Q2 MCQ Which gives the full list of consulted works?
  1. Glossary
  2. Index
  3. Bibliography
  4. Appendix
Answer: C
Why: References contain only cited works.
Q3 MSQ The introduction may contain:
  1. Scope
  2. Limitations
  3. Definitions of terms
  4. Final recommendation
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Recommendation is a separate part.
Q4 T/F Recommendations should always be added to a report.
Answer: False
Why: Only if asked.
Q5 Fill In APA style the ______ follows the author’s name.
Answer: year
Why: In MLA it comes near the end.
Q6 Short What is placed in appendices?
Answer: Supporting material such as questionnaires, interview sheets and charts that would interrupt the flow of the text.
Why: Labelled Appendix A, B, C.

Revision checklist

  • I know the four parts of the main body.
  • I can list introduction contents.
  • I know conclusion and recommendation rules.
  • I can list back matter items.
  • I can differentiate bibliography and references.

Lecture 38: Report Style: Part I

Week 8 · Course book PDF pages 609–630 (printed pages 608–629)

Learning objectives

  • Explain why report language must be familiar, specific and simple.
  • Identify and correct clichés, jargon, foreign words, redundancy, circumlocution and unfamiliar abbreviations.
  • Replace difficult words and phrasal verbs with simple ones.
  • Correct misplaced modifiers and use specific rather than vague words.
  • Recall Nathaniel Hawthorne’s remark on words.

Important definitions

Cliché

A word or phrase once fresh but now overused and stale (e.g. ‘threadbare’, ‘uphill task’, ‘writing on the wall’, ‘leave no stone unturned’).

Jargon

Technical language of a profession that non-specialist readers cannot follow.

Redundancy

Using unnecessary words that repeat meaning (basic fundamentals, repeat again, final result, great majority).

Circumlocution

A roundabout, wordy way of saying something simple.

Foreign words and phrases

Words from other languages (e.g. subpoena, avant-garde, tête-à-tête) that readers may not know.

Modifier

A word (adjective/adverb) that describes another; piled-up or misplaced modifiers confuse.

Phrasal verb / idiom

Verb plus particle (make out, make up) with changed meaning; avoid in reports.

Synonym

A word with a near meaning, used to replace a difficult word.

Abbreviation

Shortened form; use only if familiar to readers (HIV is fine; FIG, ATR, CVR may not be).

Core concepts

Why style matters

  • A report is written to bring action; action requires understanding.
  • Readers have different backgrounds (an engineer and a doctor use different terminologies).
  • Technical language is needed for technical subjects, but use words that are at least familiar.
  • Report language is not only sentences but also words and paragraphs: choose words, phrases, sentences and paragraphs carefully.
  • Hawthorne: “Words so innocent and powerless as they are, as standing in a dictionary, how potent for good and evil they become in the hands of one who knows how to combine them.”

Be specific and familiar

  • Avoid unusual words used for an ‘air of intellectuality’.
  • Be specific: ‘the majority were divided’ → ‘70 per cent were divided’.
  • Revise and replace difficult words with simple synonyms.

Difficult word replacements

  • ramifications → results; corroborate → verify / prove
  • veracity → truth; validation → proof
  • apogee → orbit (as given); camaraderie → friendship; dismember → cut; impasse → deadlock/difficulty
  • obtuse → dull; synergy → unity/coordination
  • antiquated → old; annihilation → destruction; conflagration → fire
  • cognizant → aware/know; pedagogue → teacher

Examples: “The ramifications of the experiment should be corroborated” → “The result of the lab experiment should be verified.” “The veracity of the scientist’s claim requires validation” → “The truth of the claim requires proof.”

Modifiers and phrasal verbs

  • Avoid double modifiers: “all praises and credits”, “new and novel structure” – use one word.
  • Avoid phrasal verbs: “could not make out” → “could not understand”.
  • Misplaced modifier: “Speaking before the large audience, my knees began to knock” → “Speaking before the large audience, I felt my knees knocking.”

Clichés

  • “The report discusses threadbare the problem…” → “…in detail”.
  • “uphill task” → “challenge”; “writing on the wall” → “prediction”.
  • “leave no stone unturned” → “work hard / try my best”; “burning question” → “common issue”; “day in and day out”; “repeat again”.

Foreign words, jargon, redundancy, circumlocution

  • Foreign: subpoena (court summons), avant-garde → pioneer, tête-à-tête → personal interview.
  • Jargon: “the spectrum of infection with the human immunodeficiency virus continues to evolve…” → “we are learning more about the range of infection in people with HIV”; “pedagogical pursuits” → “teaching”.
  • Redundancy: basic fundamentals, repeat again, final result, a great majority.
  • Circumlocution: “We are cognizant that you are experiencing difficulties during this period of reorganisation” → “We know…”.

Abbreviations and slang

  • Use only abbreviations the reader knows; explain unfamiliar ones (FIG = Faculty Initiation Grant; CVR = cockpit voice recorder; ATR).
  • Avoid campus slang such as ‘insti’, ‘poly class’, ‘audie’ (auditorium), ‘enthu’.
  • Take the medium path: maximum familiar words.

Wrong word choice

Confusing sensitive with sensible leads to awkward writing; the doctor cannot cure what he cannot diagnose, and a solution needs understanding. Next lecture: sentence structure, readability, active/passive.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Faults of word choice in reports
FaultMeaningExample → fix
ClichéOverused expressionthreadbare → in detail
JargonTechnical terminologypedagogue → teacher
Foreign wordNon-English wordavant-garde → pioneer
RedundancyRepeated meaningbasic fundamentals → fundamentals
CircumlocutionRoundabout expressioncognizant → know
Unfamiliar abbreviationUnknown short formspell out FIG
Phrasal verbMulti-word idiommake out → understand

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Power of words — Nathaniel Hawthorne

Words are innocent and powerless in a dictionary but become potent for good and evil in the hands of one who combines them.

Examples and applications

Example

70 per cent instead of ‘majority’.

Example

Corroborate → verify.

Example

Writing on the wall → prediction.

Example

HIV instead of human immunodeficiency virus.

Example

‘Audie’ mistaken for an Audi car.

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Report language must be simple, specific and familiar.
  • Cliché = overused expression.
  • Jargon = technical language unknown to the reader.
  • Redundancy = unnecessary repetition.
  • Circumlocution = roundabout expression.
  • Use one modifier, not two.
  • Avoid phrasal verbs and idioms.
  • Avoid unfamiliar abbreviations and slang.
  • Hawthorne’s quotation on words.
  • Be specific with numbers instead of ‘majority’.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Report language should be simple, specific and familiar so that readers of different backgrounds can act on it. Avoid unusual words, clichés, jargon, foreign phrases, redundancy, circumlocution, phrasal verbs, double modifiers, misplaced modifiers and unfamiliar abbreviations. Replace difficult terms with synonyms, give exact figures and revise. As Hawthorne notes, words become powerful in the hands of one who knows how to combine them.

Lecture 39: Report Style: Part II

Week 8 · Course book PDF pages 631–650 (printed pages 630–649)

Learning objectives

  • Use active and passive voice appropriately in reports.
  • Apply sentence-style techniques: subject-verb closeness, emphatic word order, parallel construction, splitting breathless sentences, avoiding noun clusters.
  • Build unified, coherent, ordered paragraphs and use transitions.
  • Compute the Gunning fog index and interpret it.
  • Recall George Orwell’s ‘windowpane’ principle.

Important definitions

Active voice

The subject performs the action; direct, clear, but can sound like an allegation.

Passive voice

The subject receives the action (verb in third form); neutral, polite, impersonal, used to avoid blame.

Emphatic word order

Arranging words so the important item is at the beginning or end of the sentence.

Parallel construction

Using the same grammatical pattern for parts of a sentence.

Breathless sentence

A long sentence with no pause; split it into two or more.

Noun cluster (noun string)

A long chain of nouns (e.g. ‘current group member meeting dates scheduling package format’) that hides meaning; replace with verbs and prepositions.

Transitions (signposts)

Linking words and phrases that guide readers from one idea to the next.

Readability

The ease with which a text can be read and understood.

Fog index

Robert Gunning’s formula: 0.4 × (average sentence length + percentage of difficult words); a score below 12 means readable.

Topic sentence

The main sentence of a paragraph to which others are related.

Core concepts

Variety as a style principle

  • Repeating the same pattern (‘I am thankful to…’ repeatedly) is monotonous; vary voice, sentence length and order.
  • Mix short and long sentences; give paragraphs for ‘eye relief’.
  • Examples of variety in acknowledgement: ‘I am indebted to…’, ‘I acknowledge…’.

Active versus passive voice

  • Active: direct and easier (“We do not advise the training division to concentrate its time, money and resources on a new programme”).
  • Passive: “Concentration… is not advisable from our point of view.”
  • Use passive to neutralise blame: “Since your workers did not pack the tape recorders, they got damaged” (allegation) → “Since the tape recorders were not packed properly…”.
  • Commands (‘Shut the door’) can be softened: “You are requested to close the door.”
  • “You are responsible” → “I think it is your responsibility.”
  • A complaint is written in active voice; the reply is often in passive.

Sentence style

  • Put the subject first and the verb just after it; don’t let the verb get lost at the end.
  • Place important matter in the subject; use emphatic word order.
  • Revise: “Some aspects of desktop publishing… have powerful control…” → “Today even a low-cost word processor can deliver…”.
  • Link clauses: “Parts are manufactured in Mexico, which has lower wage rates than the United States.”
  • Word order example: “He, as a management representative, is aware that he should uphold the authority to fire workers who disobey orders.”
  • Put the positive first: “We feel that by using this system we are missing some patients and therefore losing revenue.”

Parallelism and splitting

  • Parallel: “This book is good as a college text and even better as a means of enlarging their knowledge.”
  • Split breathless sentences: “We are enclosing next month’s event calendar. If interested, kindly inform the concerned organiser.”
  • Complex scientific sentences (adrenergic–cholinergic antagonism) become readable when split and using pronouns.

Noun clusters

Kick the noun habit, create the verb habit: “Please revise the schedule package format of group members’ meetings” instead of a string of nouns. Value-orientation example: bring the verb closer (“The concept of value orientation in this sense is thus…”).

Paragraph structure

  • Three mantras: unity, coherence, order.
  • Begin with a topic sentence; other sentences coordinate or subordinate to it using connecting words, repetition and pronouns.
  • Develop paragraphs by comparison and contrast, cause and effect, classification, problem–solution.
  • Avoid very long paragraphs; paragraph breaks give relief.

Transitions

  • Addition: moreover, furthermore, besides, finally.
  • Cause / result: hence, therefore, thus, consequently.
  • Contrast: however, nonetheless, on the other hand, while.
  • Time: earlier, now, later, after a while, meanwhile.
  • Repetition / summary: in short, that is, thus.

Readability and fog index

  • Gunning (1950s) fog index = 0.4 × (average sentence length + percentage of difficult words).
  • Result below 12 = readable.
  • Orwell: style should be unobtrusive – like a windowpane through which information is clearly seen.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Transitions by function
FunctionExamples
Additionmoreover, furthermore, besides, finally
Cause/effecthence, therefore, thus, consequently
Contrast/comparisonhowever, nonetheless, on the other hand
Timeearlier, now, later, meanwhile
Repetition/summaryin short, that is
Paragraph development methods
MethodUse
Comparison and contrastShow similarity/difference
Cause and effectExplain reasons and results

Problem–solutionState problem then remedy

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Fog index — Robert Gunning (1950s)

Index = 0.4 × (average sentence length + % of difficult words); below 12 is readable.

Unobtrusive style — George Orwell

Style should be like a windowpane: invisible, letting information be clearly seen.

Examples and applications

Example

Tape recorders damaged because not packed (passive to avoid blame).

Example

‘Please revise the schedule package format of group members’ meetings.’

Example

Mexico wage-rate sentence.

Example

Splitting the event-calendar sentence.

Comparison tables

PointActivePassive
Verb formVerb follows subjectBe + past participle

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Vary voice, sentence length and word order.
  • Passive neutralises allegations.
  • Subject then verb, close together.
  • Positive points come first in comparisons.
  • Split breathless sentences.
  • Replace noun clusters with verbs.
  • Paragraph = unity, coherence, order with topic sentence.
  • Transitions are signposts.
  • Fog index below 12 = readable.
  • Gunning proposed the fog index; Orwell compared style to a windowpane.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W8 Q4p.28Report style: active voiceTrue/False
W8 Q12p.30Sentence style in reportsMSQ
W8 Q14p.30Transitions and coherenceMSQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Good report style mixes active and passive voice (passive neutralises blame), keeps subject and verb close, uses emphatic word order, parallel construction and a mix of short and long sentences, and splits breathless sentences. Noun clusters should be replaced by verbs. Paragraphs need unity, coherence, order and a topic sentence, linked by transitions. Readability can be measured by Gunning’s fog index (0.4 × (average sentence length + % difficult words)); a score below 12 is readable. Style should be unobtrusive, like a windowpane (Orwell).

  2. 12
  3. 20
  4. 30
Answer: B
Why: Below 12.

  1. Active
  2. Passive
  3. Imperative
  4. Subjunctive
Answer: B
Why: Passive neutralises allegation.
Q3 MSQ Which are transitions of contrast?
  1. However
  2. Nonetheless
  3. Therefore
  4. On the other hand
Answer: A, B, D

Q4 T/F A long string of nouns makes a sentence clearer.
Answer: False

Q5 Fill A sentence that cannot be read in one breath is called a ______ sentence.

Lecture 40: Group Communication

Week 8 · Course book PDF pages 651–668 (printed pages 650–667)

Learning objectives

  • Define a group and a team and differentiate them.
  • List the tasks of groups and types of groups.
  • State the advantages and disadvantages of group work, including groupthink and hidden agendas.
  • Explain group dynamics: cooperation, loyalty, cohesiveness.
  • Describe Tuckman’s stages of group development and the three group roles.

Important definitions

Group

A collection of people who work, interact and cooperate to achieve a common goal in a specified time; identity of members is taken individually and they share information and resources.

Team

A group of people with a common goal, within a limited time, having collective accountability; slogan: ‘one for all and all for one’.

Formal group

A group formally constituted by an organisation.

Informal group

A group formed through personal relations; often works better in problem solving.

Virtual group

A group communicating online (WhatsApp, Facebook).

Groupthink

A poor group decision resulting from majority pressure that ignores individual dissent.

Hidden agenda

A concealed personal aim of a member that harms group interaction.

Group dynamics

Forces that keep a group working: cooperation, loyalty, cohesiveness.

Cohesiveness

Staying together and committed to the goal despite disagreements.

Consensus

General agreement reached by the group.

Core concepts

Why groups

  • Humans form groups; modern tasks (events, reports, products, presentations) cannot be done alone or in short time.
  • Example: a college cultural festival (‘Cognizance’) is a group effort; teams for music, lectures etc.
  • This lecture itself is a team product (recording, editing, approving, posting).

Group versus team

  • Group: common goal within a period; members taken individually.
  • Team: a subdivision with its own specific goal and collective accountability; shared leadership; joint accountability for performance.
  • Both share a common goal, cooperation, identification of skills, leadership and accountability.

Tasks of groups and teams

  1. Solve problems.
  2. Investigate an opportunity (new product, policy).
  3. Write reports.
  4. Give oral or group presentations (experts present their parts).
  5. Devise, evolve and produce new products (e.g. incubation centres).

Types of groups

  • Formal, informal (often more effective) and virtual groups.
  • Informal groups help problem solving: members help each other beyond formal roles.

Advantages of groups

  • More information and resources.
  • Diversity of views and experience.
  • Better acceptance of decisions and consensus after weighing pros and cons.
  • Better performance.
  • Sense of belonging; broadened knowledge.

Disadvantages of groups

  • Rigid ideas and ego clashes cause wasted, frustrating time.
  • ‘Know-it-all’ delusion; unacceptance of consensus.
  • Groups within groups; individual opinions ignored.
  • Groupthink and poor decisions.
  • Hidden agendas.
  • Difficulty coordinating diverse backgrounds; a ‘messiah’/leader is needed.

Group dynamics

  • Cooperation – communication clears roadblocks.
  • Loyalty – willingness to make small sacrifices.
  • Cohesiveness – staying together despite differences (utensils make noise).

Tuckman’s stages

  1. Forming – introductions, rapport building; members are new.
  2. Storming – clash of ideas; beneficial for new ideas.
  3. Norming – peace; members mellow, accept views, filter.
  4. Performing – energetic, effective work.
  5. Adjourning – group dissolves after the task (added later by Tuckman).

Bruce Tuckman, psychologist; initially four stages, adjourning added later.

Group roles

  • Self-oriented roles – members pursuing self-interest, wanting control or withdrawing.
  • Maintenance roles – encouraging, harmonising, compromising to keep the group together.
  • Task-facilitating roles – initiating, giving and seeking information, setting procedures.

Leader and Drucker

A leader coordinates and secures consensus. Peter Drucker: “When the ship goes down, you do not call a meeting. The captain gives an order or everybody drowns.” (crisis needs decisive leadership). Members must also consider ethics and personal responsibility. Next lecture: leadership.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Tuckman’s stages of group development
StageCharacteristic
FormingIntroductions, uncertainty, building rapport
StormingConflict of ideas; clashes
NormingAcceptance, norms, harmony
PerformingProductive teamwork
AdjourningTask completed; group disbands
Group roles

MaintenanceEncourage, harmonise, compromise
Task-facilitatingInitiate, give/seek information, set procedure

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Stages of group development — Bruce Tuckman

Forming, storming, norming, performing and (later) adjourning.

Crisis leadership — Peter Drucker

When the ship goes down, you do not call a meeting; the captain gives an order.

Examples and applications

Example

College cultural festival (Cognizance) with music and lecture teams.

Example

Incubation centre developing a new product.

Example

Task force that is adjourned after completing its work.

Example

WhatsApp virtual groups.

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip

  • Team = collective accountability.
  • Tasks: solve problems, investigate opportunities, write reports, present, develop products.
  • Types of groups: formal, informal, virtual.
  • Group dynamics: cooperation, loyalty, cohesiveness.
  • Roles: self-oriented, maintenance, task-facilitating.
  • Groupthink leads to poor decisions.
  • Drucker: the captain gives an order.
  • Informal groups can work better than formal ones.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

A group is a collection of people cooperating for a common goal in a set time; a team is a subdivision with collective accountability. Groups solve problems, investigate opportunities, write reports, give presentations and develop products. They may be formal, informal or virtual. Advantages are more information, diversity, acceptance, performance and belonging; disadvantages include ego clashes, ignoring individuals, groupthink and hidden agendas. Group dynamics: cooperation, loyalty, cohesiveness. Tuckman’s stages: forming, storming, norming, performing, adjourning. Roles are self-oriented, maintenance and task-facilitating.

Week 9: Leadership, Discussion, Meetings and Ethics

Lecture 41: Leadership Skills

Week 9 · Course book PDF pages 669–683 (printed pages 668–682)

Learning objectives

  • Identify traits and characteristics of a leader.
  • Describe leadership styles: autocratic, democratic, laissez-faire, transactional, transformational.
  • Explain initiating structure and consideration, and Fiedler’s contingency model.
  • Describe charismatic and visionary leadership.
  • List cultural differences in leadership and the elements of trust.

Important definitions

Leader

A person from among the group who initiates action, guides members and helps the group reach its goal; not necessarily a manager.

Autocratic leader

Takes decisions on his own ideas, with little interest in members’ problems.

Democratic leader

Considers members’ interests, takes suggestions and arrives at a solution acceptable to all.

Laissez-faire leader

Gives members full autonomy and does not intervene.

Transactional leader

Guides followers towards established goals by clarifying roles and task requirements.

Transformational leader

Inspires followers to transcend self-interest and brings profound change.

Initiating structure

Leader behaviour that defines tasks, work relationships, goals, standards and deadlines.

Consideration

Leader behaviour that builds mutual trust, respects ideas and feelings, and cares for members’ welfare.

LPC (least preferred co-worker)

Scale in Fiedler’s model measuring whether a leader is task-oriented or relationship-oriented.

Charismatic leader

Leader with heroic, extraordinary qualities, vision and commitment, an agent of radical change.

Visionary leadership

Leadership offering a clear, compelling image of an innovative future that taps people’s energy and emotions.

Core concepts

Who is a leader

  • A leader is ‘among us’, not from heaven; example of a passenger who organised a solution after a flight was diverted to Bhopal instead of Jabalpur.
  • Distinguish a manager from a leader.
  • Traits: intelligence, charisma, decisiveness, enthusiasm, strength, bravery, integrity, self-confidence.
  • Characteristics shown: initiative, desire to lead, honesty, integrity, subject knowledge, concern for others.

Styles of leadership

  • Autocratic – not considered good; ignores problems of members.
  • Democratic – involves everyone, weighs pros and cons, acceptable solution.
  • Laissez-faire – autonomy; no interference.
  • Transactional – assigns tasks and roles.
  • Transformational – goes beyond role, sees a new tomorrow.

Dimensions of leader behaviour

  • Initiating structure: assigns tasks, expects standards and deadlines.
  • Consideration: trust, respect, concern, well-being.
  • Leaders high on both bring better products and solutions; too much consideration may cause absenteeism.

Fiedler’s model

  • Fred Fiedler (spelt Fielder in the transcript): effectiveness depends on the match between the leader’s style (measured by LPC) and the degree of control and influence the situation gives.
  • Leadership style is fixed: either task-oriented or relationship-oriented.
  • A relationship-oriented leader may neglect the task and cost the organisation.

Charismatic leadership

  • Followers attribute heroic qualities; leaders show commitment, vision, confidence; agents of radical change (politics, religion, business).
  • Rise above petty self-interest; articulate the vision; participative; pioneers of change; aware of environmental concerns.

Visionary leadership

  • Clear and compelling imagery of an innovative future; draws on traditions and connects to actions people can take.
  • Taps energy and emotions, creating enthusiasm like for sports.
  • Visionary leader explains the vision, has no hidden agenda, expresses it through behaviour and action, extends it to different contexts.

Transactional versus transformational

  • Transactional: roles and task requirements.
  • Transformational: inspires followers to transcend self-interest, instils courage, profound effect.

Cultural differences in leadership

  • Koreans: paternalistic, mentors.
  • Arabs: strict; kindness seen as weakness.
  • Japanese: humble, speak little.
  • Scandinavians and Dutch: do not praise individuals in public.
  • Americans: talk of responsibility, rationality, value orientation and hedonism.

Trust: the hallmark of an effective leader

  • Integrity (honesty, words match action)
  • Competence (subject knowledge)
  • Consistency
  • Loyalty (to organisation and coworkers; represents their case assertively)
  • Openness / candidness

The leader is the sailor guiding the ship; members are passengers.

Closing quotation

Warren E. Bennis: “Leadership is the capacity to translate vision into reality.” Are leaders born or made? Both – they emerge from among us and are made by their qualities (to be seen in group discussion).

Types, classifications and characteristics

Leadership styles
StyleFeature
AutocraticOwn decisions, rigid
DemocraticParticipative
Laissez-faireHands-off autonomy
TransactionalRoles, tasks, rewards
TransformationalInspires beyond self-interest
CharismaticHeroic, vision, radical change
Elements of trust
ElementMeaning
IntegrityHonesty; word = action
CompetenceKnowledge of the subject
ConsistencySame action for same problem
LoyaltySacrifice and defending co-workers
OpennessCandid, clear explanation

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Contingency model — Fred Fiedler (as ‘Fielder’)

Effectiveness depends on matching the leader’s style (task or relationship oriented, measured by LPC) with situational control.

Leadership definition — Warren E. Bennis

Leadership is the capacity to translate vision into reality.

Examples and applications

Example

Passenger organising alternative transport after the Bhopal landing.

Example

Korean paternalistic leaders; Arab strictness; Japanese humility.

Example

Scandinavian leaders avoid public praise.

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • A leader emerges from the group.
  • Five styles: autocratic, democratic, laissez-faire, transactional, transformational.
  • Initiating structure and consideration are two dimensions.
  • Fiedler: leadership style fixed; task or relationship oriented.
  • Charismatic leaders have vision and conviction.
  • Transformational leader inspires beyond self-interest.
  • Trust elements: integrity, competence, consistency, loyalty, openness.
  • Bennis: capacity to translate vision into reality.
  • Koreans paternalistic; Arabs strict; Japanese humble.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

A leader emerges from the group and shows initiative, integrity, knowledge and concern. Styles are autocratic, democratic, laissez-faire, transactional and transformational. Leader behaviour has two dimensions, initiating structure and consideration, and Fiedler’s model links effectiveness to the fit between a fixed task or relationship style and the situation. Charismatic and visionary leaders inspire change; leadership differs across cultures. Trust, built on integrity, competence, consistency, loyalty and openness, is the hallmark. Bennis: leadership is the capacity to translate vision into reality.

Practice questions

  2. Democratic
  3. Laissez-faire
  4. Transactional

  2. Competence
  3. Consistency

Answer: True

Answer: public
Why: It is considered impolite.
Q6 Short Differentiate transactional and transformational leaders.
Answer: A transactional leader guides followers to established goals by defining roles and tasks; a transformational leader inspires them to rise above self-interest and brings profound change.
Why: Transformational is one step ahead.

Lecture 42: Group Discussion: Part I

Week 9 · Course book PDF pages 684–702 (printed pages 683–701)

Learning objectives

  • Define group discussion (GD) and explain its importance in selection.
  • Differentiate debate and GD.
  • Describe GD set-up: seating, size, time, topic announcement.
  • Differentiate topic GD and case GD.
  • List language, qualities evaluated, individual and group responsibilities, and leadership types in a GD.

Important definitions

Discussion

An exchange of knowledge and experience among people.

Group discussion (GD)

A formal, face-to-face, unstructured, cooperative discussion among participants who exchange views on a topic; a dialogic learning process.

Debate

A formal, structured contest with participants speaking for or against a motion.

Motion

The topic of a debate, supported by the ‘for’ speakers and opposed by the ‘against’ speakers.

Topic GD

A GD on an announced topic, often unstructured and announced on the spot.

Case GD

A GD based on a printed real-life or imaginary case where the group is expected to reach a solution.

Dialogic

In the form of a dialogue among many people.

Bulldozer

A dominating, selfish participant who tries to overpower the group.

Inert participant

A member who does not participate and needs encouragement.

Core concepts

Why GD matters

  • Organisations use GD as a criterion to shortlist/eliminate candidates when applicants are many and vacancies few, often followed by another test.
  • It also helps in crises as a means to find remedies; it has an inbuilt mechanism of correction.
  • Evaluates traits: awareness, reasoning, behaviour, initiative, motivation, assertiveness, listening, leadership, teamwork.

Debate versus GD

  • Both are formal and face-to-face.
  • Debate: structured, fixed sides (for/against), topic given in advance (a week or ten days), competitive, emotional, flowery language.
  • GD: unstructured, flexible (a participant may change stance), topic often announced on the spot, cooperative atmosphere, facts and simple language.

Set-up of a GD

  • Seating circular or semi-circular so all see each other.
  • Usually 7–10 participants.
  • Time: 15, 20 or 25 minutes (decided by organisers).
  • Topic announced; a few minutes to think; keywords ignite thoughts.
  • No rule on who speaks first or in what order; no designated leader.

Topic GD versus case GD

  • Topic GD: clear topic announced.
  • Case GD: detailed printed case (real or imaginary); tests analytical, decisive and reasoning skills; aim is a solution; suggestions of ways may be offered.
  • Flexibility is the hallmark of both.

Language and phrases

  • Simple, concise, appropriate, persuasive and polite; avoid flowery, pompous, obsolete or sesquipedalian words.
  • Starting: “Today we have gathered to discuss…”.
  • Strong view: “I strongly feel…”, “I am convinced…”, “I am pretty sure…”.
  • Neutral: “According to me…”.
  • Making a point: “May I make a point about…?”, “I think it is important to consider…”.
  • Agreeing: “I fully agree with what Mohan says.”
  • Disagreeing politely: “That may be your personal view; my view is…”.
  • Returning to the topic: “Could we stick to the topic?”, “I fear we are deviating…”.

Qualities evaluated

  • Awareness, reasoning with facts, behaviour (group dynamics matter more than content), initiative, motivation (encourage the quiet), assertiveness (not aggression), listening, leadership and team skills.

Responsibilities

  • Individual: speak, behave well, listen, show interest.
  • Group: commit to group goals, keep a conducive and amicable atmosphere, resolve conflicts, ensure full participation, keep cohesion; differences should be ideological, not personal.

Leadership in GD

  • No designated leader; anyone can lead at any time; tested in conflict or chaos.
  • Driver – initiates.
  • Amiable – speaks little, neutral; not acceptable.
  • Democrat – best; restores order and shares time.
  • Bulldozer – dominating, selfish; can be cut to size by exposing weak logic.

Body language

Must never be negative; accept opposition graciously through eyes, face and posture; show commitment. “A group swims and sinks together.”

Types, classifications and characteristics

Types of GD
TypeFeatures
Topic GDTopic announced; unstructured
Case GDPrinted case; aim at solution
Leadership types in GD
TypeBehaviourAcceptable?
DriverStarts discussionYes
AmiableSilent, neutralNo
DemocratOrderly, shares timeBest
BulldozerDominatesNo

Examples and applications

Example

Job fair using GD to shortlist.

Example

College debates for and against a motion.

Example

Phrases like ‘Could we stick to the topic?’.

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • GD is formal, face-to-face, unstructured and cooperative.
  • Debate is structured with motion for/against.
  • GD seating: circular or semi-circular.
  • 7–10 participants; 15–25 minutes.
  • Two types: topic GD and case GD.
  • No designated leader in GD.
  • Leader types: driver, amiable, democrat, bulldozer.
  • Group dynamics matter more than content.
  • Body language must be positive.
  • Participants have individual and group responsibilities.

W9 Q7p.32Role of the leader in discussionMCQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Group discussion is a formal, unstructured, cooperative, dialogic exchange used to shortlist candidates. Unlike a structured, competitive debate with fixed sides, GD allows flexible stances and uses simple, factual, polite language. Held in a circle or semi-circle with 7–10 participants for 15–25 minutes, it may be a topic GD or case GD. Evaluators look for awareness, reasoning, behaviour, initiative, motivation, assertiveness, listening and leadership; participants have individual and group responsibilities. Leaders may be drivers, amiable, democrats or bulldozers.

Lecture 43: Group Discussion: Part II

Week 9 · Course book PDF pages 703–718 (printed pages 702–717)

Learning objectives

  • Explain the role of an emergent leader and the LEADER acronym used in the lecture.
  • Describe what panelists observe in a GD.
  • List the dos for preparing and participating in a GD.
  • List the don’ts in a GD.
  • Recognise types of GD topics and the need for decorum.

Important definitions

Emergent leader

A leader who is not designated but emerges from the group by taking initiative to bring the discussion back to the main issue.

Panelist

The evaluator who observes participants’ speech, behaviour and non-verbal conduct, usually noting points.

Inert (silent) participant

A member who does not participate; he becomes a liability to the group.

High / low participator

Members who speak often or rarely; panelists note changes in participation.

Chairman role

Giving judgment; participants must not assume it.

Bulldozer

A dominating member who creates chaos; can be countered by exposing flaws in his logic.

Decorum

Proper behaviour in a discussion, free from gender, cultural or ego bias.

Core concepts

Role of leader in GD

  • Saves the group from being sidetracked (like a boat in the middle of a river).
  • Resolves conflicts, summarises, encourages silent members, keeps participation going.
  • Leader types: specific, implied, emergent, designated – in GD the leader is emergent.
  • Influence by constructive participation and rational thoughts; build support among supporters and logically weaken opponents’ view.

LEADER acronym (lecture’s analysis)

  • Liberal – not rigid; ensures everyone participates.
  • Eager and enthusiastic.
  • Assertive – without arrogance; influence by constructive ideas.
  • Democrat – everyone gets a chance; nobody’s sentiment is hurt.
  • Erudite – subject knowledge.
  • Rational – no discrimination.

Also: good communication ability. (The lecture lists these traits in running speech; the letter mapping follows the speaker’s explanation.)

What panelists observe

  • Who started, who speaks more or less, who became silent, who keeps the ball rolling.
  • Reactions to others: gesture, posture, pitch and loudness of voice.
  • Whether anyone is hurt (religion, ethics) in controversial topics.
  • Shifts in participation: support, opposition or attempts to subdue.
  • Consistent involvement – not one speech followed by ‘golden silence’.

How to prepare / dos

  1. Seat yourself comfortably (non-verbal behaviour).
  2. Listen to the topic carefully; keywords ignite thoughts.
  3. Organise thoughts before speaking; speak complete, grammatical sentences with follow-up points.
  4. Speak at the earliest opportunity without snatching; be patient.
  5. Politely intervene if someone speaks too long: “May I share my piece of mind…”.
  6. Identify supporters and opponents; augment or counter.
  7. Keep track of time; share time fairly.
  8. Maintain eye contact while speaking and listening.
  9. Persuade silent members politely.
  10. Use a proper pitch, intonation and articulation; speak at a pace others follow.
  11. Define the problem when sidetracked; look at both sides of the coin.

Don’ts in a GD

  • Do not be in a hurry to start; never remain silent.
  • Do not dominate; influence through constructive, rational, factual views.
  • Never take the role of a chairman or an extreme, judgmental stance.
  • Do not argue with opponents or whisper with supporters; no value judgments.
  • Do not look at the panelist to impress; do not use slang to shout at silent members.
  • Do not move excessively forward or show physical weight.
  • Do not put all ideas in one go or speak too fast.
  • Do not digress; avoid gender, cultural or ego issues.

GD topics

  • Factual (e.g. brain drain should be stopped; human cloning should be banned), abstract, fictional, controversial (e.g. no reservation in jobs; transfer of religious places; ‘money makes happy, but too much money makes us unhappy’).
  • Take a solid stand based on logic; discuss, do not quarrel; consensus is desirable but not always possible.

Types, classifications and characteristics

GD topics
TypeExample
FactualBrain drain should be stopped
Factual/policyHuman cloning should be banned
Abstract / fictionalImaginative or conceptual topic
ControversialNo reservation in jobs; religious places
Dos and don’ts summary
DoDon’t
Listen to the topicDominate
Organise thoughtsAct as chairman
Maintain eye contactWhisper or argue
Share timeSpeak too fast or all at once
Encourage silent membersLook at the panelist

Examples and applications

Example

Boat in the middle of a river without a leader.

Example

Crowded bus: creating your own space.

Example

Polite phrase: ‘Mr Suraj may be right in a particular situation, but every situation has its own requirements.’

Comparison tables

Influence versus dominate
PointInfluenceDominate
MethodConstructive, rational, factual ideasLoudness, weight, interruption
ResultEmerges as leaderSeen as a bulldozer

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • The GD leader is emergent.
  • Silent members are a liability to the group.
  • Panelists observe both verbal and non-verbal behaviour.
  • Listen to the topic carefully; keywords matter.
  • Organise thoughts; use complete sentences.
  • Never take the role of a chairman.
  • Don’t whisper or argue; maintain eye contact.
  • Influence, don’t dominate.
  • Topics: factual, abstract, fictional, controversial.
  • Consensus not always necessary; exchange of views is.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W8 Q1p.28Group discussion etiquetteTrue/False
W9 Q9p.32Group discussion do's and don'tsMCQ
W9 Q10p.32Evaluation criteria in group discussionMCQ
W9 Q14p.33Group discussion conclusionTrue/False

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

In a GD the leader emerges from the group; he is communicative, liberal, eager, democratic, assertive, erudite and rational, saves the group from conflict, summarises and encourages silent members. Panelists observe participation, tone, gestures and fairness. Prepare by sitting comfortably, listening to the topic, organising thoughts, speaking early but politely, sharing time, maintaining eye contact and encouraging others. Avoid dominating, acting as chairman, extreme stands, arguments, whispering, looking at the panelist, over-speed and digressions. Topics may be factual, abstract, fictional or controversial; a solid, logical stand and decorum are essential.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ The kind of leader in a GD is:
  1. Designated
  2. Hereditary
  3. Emergent
  4. Elected
Answer: C
Why: A leader emerges on his own initiative.
Q2 MCQ Which role should a participant NOT take?
  1. Encourager
  2. Chairman
  3. Supporter
  4. Listener
Answer: B
Why: Participants discuss; they do not judge.
Q3 MSQ Which are don’ts in a GD?
  1. Whispering with supporters
  2. Looking at the panelist
  3. Maintaining eye contact
  4. Speaking too fast
Answer: A, B, D
Why: Eye contact is recommended.
Q4 T/F Staying silent in a GD is safe because it avoids mistakes.
Answer: False
Why: Silence makes you a liability.
Q5 Fill Instead of dominating, a participant should ______ the group.
Answer: influence
Why: Through constructive ideas.
Q6 Short How should a silent member be handled?
Answer: Politely encourage and persuade him to speak, without shouting or using slang.
Why: The group is judged collectively.

Revision checklist

  • I know the emergent leader’s traits.
  • I know what panelists observe.
  • I can list GD dos.
  • I can list GD don’ts.
  • I know GD topic types.

Lecture 44: Meeting Management

Week 9 · Course book PDF pages 719–731 (printed pages 718–730)

Learning objectives

  • Define a meeting and list its functions.
  • Identify reasons why meetings fail.
  • Plan a meeting by answering why, who, when, where and what.
  • Conduct a meeting: start on time, follow the agenda, allow participation, record minutes.
  • State participant etiquette and the leader’s closing duties.

Important definitions

Meeting

An organised gathering of people for a specific purpose, called to share information, train, solve problems, plan, receive/demonstrate products and make decisions.

Agenda

A list of items to be discussed, circulated in advance to give members first-hand information.

Minutes

The official record of what was discussed and decided; unpleasant incidents are not recorded.

Venue

The place where the meeting is held.

Fiasco

A meaningless or failed meeting with no outcome.

Follow-up action

Assigning responsibilities and deadlines after the meeting.

Core concepts

Functions of a meeting

  1. Information sharing in the unit.
  2. Training of new members and for new practices.
  3. Problem solving and crisis management, using diverse resources of the group.
  4. Planning and taking stock of work in progress.
  5. Receiving / demonstrating a new product (e.g. e-rickshaw) to find lapses.
  6. Decision making – the overall function of every meeting.

Why meetings fail

  • Poor planning – notice of 2–3 hours, no agenda or unprepared members.
  • Poor leadership.
  • Too many routine meetings; half-hearted, disinterested participation.
  • Meetings at very short notice (except emergencies).

Questions before calling a meeting

  • Why – is a meeting really necessary or is a discussion enough?
  • Who – only people whose presence matters.
  • When – check calendars and convenience of key people (by e-mail); avoid evenings after 7 pm; first half preferred by some.
  • Where – accessible common place; change venues for a series but keep cost low.
  • What – agenda circulated beforehand.

Arrangements

  • Check the venue well in advance: capacity (6–7 to 20 people), electricity, coolers or AC, gadgets, microphones for large groups.
  • Pad and pen at each seat; refreshments if possible.
  • Agenda well prepared and systematic.

Conducting the meeting

  • Start on time even if some are late (do not scold latecomers; may say ‘we started without you’).
  • Follow the agenda; keep the discussion from deviating.
  • Allow full participation; democratic control; encourage quiet members politely (“Well, Mr X, what is your opinion?”).
  • Appoint a secretary to record minutes; use official, positive language; do not record unpleasant incidents.
  • Be task-centred; finish on time.

Etiquette for participants

  • Wait for your turn to speak.
  • Switch off cell phones; no satirical language.
  • If the meeting overruns because of a domineering person, ask politely: “May I know if the meeting is over?”
  • Be an active participant, not a mute spectator.

Closing the meeting

  • Summarise the outcomes; identify specifics.
  • Assign responsibilities and set deadlines; decide follow-up.
  • Thank the leader/participants; announce that minutes will be circulated.
  • Leader resolves conflict neutrally in polite, persuasive language.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Functions of meetings
FunctionPurpose
Information sharingCirculate new information
TrainingInduct new employees; introduce practices
Problem solvingFind solutions in crisis
PlanningPlan and review progress
Product demonstrationTest and announce new products
Decision makingReach a decision
Planning checklist
QuestionConsideration
Why?Clear aim
Who?Essential people only
When?Check calendars; daytime
Where?Accessible, adequate venue
What?Agenda circulated

Examples and applications

Example

E-rickshaw as a new product announced internally before launch.

Example

Meeting of 6–7 versus 20 people needing different rooms.

Example

Latecomer remark: ‘We started without you’.

Comparison tables

OutcomeDecisions and deadlinesNo decision

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Meeting = organised gathering for a purpose.
  • Overall function of every meeting: decision making.
  • Plan with why, who, when, where, what.
  • Agenda is circulated in advance.
  • Start on time; follow agenda.
  • Minutes should not record unpleasant incidents.
  • Switch off cell phones.
  • Assign responsibilities and deadlines at the end.
  • Avoid meetings after 7 pm.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W9 Q13p.33Meeting management: agendaTrue/False

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Meetings are organised gatherings to share information, train, solve problems, plan, demonstrate products and above all make decisions. They fail through poor planning and leadership. Before calling a meeting ask why, who, when, where and what; check calendars, venue and facilities and circulate the agenda. During the meeting start on time, follow the agenda, encourage participation, record official minutes; participants should follow etiquette. Close by summarising, assigning responsibilities and deadlines and promising to circulate minutes.

  2. Attendance
  3. Unpleasant incidents
  4. Action points

  2. Who
  3. Where
  4. Whom to blame
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Also when and what.
Q4 T/F A meeting should wait until all latecomers arrive.
Answer: False
Why: Start on time.
Q5 Fill The list of items to be discussed is called the ______.
Answer: agenda
Why: Circulated beforehand.
Q6 Short What should a leader do when concluding a meeting?
Answer: Summarise outcomes, assign responsibilities, set deadlines, decide follow-up and announce circulation of minutes.
Why: Ensures action.

Revision checklist

  • I know why meetings fail.
  • I can plan using wh-questions.
  • I know conduct and etiquette.
  • I know closing steps.

Lecture 45: Adaptability and Work Ethics

Week 9 · Course book PDF pages 732–749 (printed pages 731–748)

Learning objectives

  • Define adaptability and explain why employees must adapt.
  • Define ethics and work ethics.
  • Explain mutual obligations of employer and employee, including Kant’s view that employees are ends, not merely means.
  • List employee rights: freedom/non-discrimination, privacy, due process, participation, health and safety, fair wages, speech.
  • Identify unethical practices and ways to be ethical.

Important definitions

Adaptability

The ability of an individual or organisation to change itself or its responses to new circumstances, situations or environments; shows the ability to learn.

Ethics

A sense of righteousness that tells whether actions are right or wrong.

Work ethic

A set of values based on the moral virtues of hard work and diligence, belief in the moral benefit of work and its ability to enhance character (as quoted from Wikipedia).

Code of conduct

Written rules and regulations of an organisation; supported by unwritten personal codes.

Moral hazard

Risk that parties in an employer–employee relationship behave unethically.

Plagiarism

Reproducing another person’s words or ideas as one’s own.

Pilfering

Stealing small things slowly and steadily from the organisation.

Misquoting / misrepresenting data

Reporting what was not said or distorting data to gain advantage.

Probation period

Initial period to judge an employee’s capability.

Core concepts

Why adaptability

  • Organisations change practices: paperless offices, electronic pay slips, online tax returns.
  • Resisting change makes people rigid, isolated and a barrier to communication; adaptable people learn and improve themselves and the organisation’s fitness.
  • Change is the law of nature; follow the organisational flow of communication.
  • Computer literacy spread because people adapted.

Skills needed to adapt

  • Physical fitness (a sound mind in a sound body).
  • Mental and psychological preparedness.
  • Willingness to work in a multicultural setting.
  • Learn the organisation’s culture and code of conduct; remember that you are its ambassador.

Personal ethics at work

  • Commitment, reliability, loyalty to organisation over friends and relatives.
  • Keep confidential information secret (finance, banking passwords).
  • Regular attendance for pay received.
  • Honouring the appointment letter, contract and probation; leaving for a small perk soon after joining is an ethical issue.

Employer–employee relationship

  • Based on trust and legal obligations; rights come with duties.
  • Organisation provides pay, facilities, environment and self-actualisation.
  • The employer has authority to monitor and control, and may invest in the employee.
  • Immanuel Kant: employees should be treated as ends, not only means; occupation gives awareness of one’s powers and happiness; ‘man feels contented after heavy work’.

Employee rights

  • Right to freedom / non-discrimination – on gender, colour, faith, age, disability.
  • Right to privacy – personal data and habits, except where safety demands (e.g. testing a pilot for alcohol); work–life balance (no odd-hour duties for women).
  • Right to due process – equal criteria for promotion and fair treatment in layoffs with adequate time and compensation.
  • Right to participation and association – consulted in decisions such as closing a unit.
  • Right to health and safe working conditions – well-being measures, helmets, yoga, swimming.
  • Right to fair wages – same pay for same work.
  • Right to freedom of conscience and speech – but not anti-organisational.

Unethical practices to avoid

  • Plagiarism.
  • Misusing office resources (phone, property); pilfering.
  • Selective misquoting.
  • Taking credit for others’ work.
  • Misrepresenting data; distorting visuals; exaggeration.
  • Criticising the organisation or colleagues publicly.
  • Conflict of loyalties: personal ethics versus organisational code (e.g. evaluating a loss-making unit that will cause redundancies).

How to be ethical

  • Follow written and unwritten codes of conduct.
  • Be honest, transparent, sincere, hardworking, an ambassador of the organisation.
  • Love your work; speak well of the organisation.
  • Protect confidential information.
  • Respect all faiths and beliefs in a multicultural workplace.
  • Balance rights and duties.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Employee rights
RightMeaning
Freedom / non-discriminationEqual opportunity regardless of gender, colour, faith, age, disability
PrivacyPersonal data protected except for safety
Due processEqual criteria for promotion and termination
ParticipationInvolved in decisions
Health and safetySafe conditions and well-being
Fair wagesEqual pay for equal work
SpeechExpress views without harming organisation
Unethical practices
PracticeDescription
PlagiarismUsing others’ work as own
Misuse of resourcesUsing office property/phone personally
MisquotingSelective or false reporting
Credit stealingTaking credit for others’ work
Data misrepresentationDistorting facts

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Employees as ends — Immanuel Kant

Persons must never be treated merely as means but also as ends; work brings awareness of one’s powers and contentment.

Examples and applications

Paperless office and electronic pay slips.

Example

  • Ethics = sense of right and wrong.
  • Work ethic is based on hard work and diligence.
  • Kant: treat employees as ends, not only means.
  • Rights come with duties.
  • Loyalty to organisation outweighs loyalty to friends in confidentiality.
  • Rights listed: freedom, privacy, due process, participation, health, fair wages, speech.
  • Unethical: plagiarism, misuse, misquoting, credit stealing.
  • Employees are ambassadors of the organisation.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Adaptability is the ability to change with new circumstances; it helps employees learn and strengthens the organisation. Ethics is the sense of right and wrong; work ethics rests on hard work, diligence, reliability and loyalty. Employer–employee relations are bound by trust and law; Kant argues employees are ends, not merely means. Employees have rights – freedom from discrimination, privacy, due process, participation, safe conditions, fair wages and speech – and duties. Avoid plagiarism, misuse of resources, misquoting, credit stealing and data misrepresentation; follow the code of conduct and act as the organisation’s ambassador.

Lecture 46: Advanced Speaking Skills

Week 10 · Course book PDF pages 750–767 (printed pages 749–766)

Learning objectives

  • Explain why speaking is a skill and how it differs from writing.
  • List the situations where speaking is required.
  • State the advantages of speaking over writing.
  • Describe the four components of effective speaking: voice, vocabulary, audience, style.
  • Recall the quotations by Shakespeare and Benjamin Franklin.

Important definitions

LSRW

Listening, Speaking, Reading, Writing: the four language skills; this lecture covers ‘S’.

Speaking skill

The ability to perform tasks (convince, inform, report, entertain) by spoken words coupled with non-verbal communication.

Conversation

An art of informal two-way speaking, e.g. breaking the ice with a co-passenger.

Spontaneity

Natural, unplanned quality of speech that keeps the audience engaged.

Style (in speaking)

The speaker’s inner voice: animation, enthusiasm and audibility.

Audibility

Being heard clearly even by the last row.

Core concepts

Why speaking is a skill

  • Speaking is natural, but in organisations tasks are communication-based; a professional must master all LSRW skills.
  • Individually you must convince, prove, satisfy, report, inform and entertain (as employee, manager or CEO).
  • A silent person in a group is seen as uninterested or without content.
  • Verbal communication is coupled with non-verbal cues.

Situations requiring speaking

  • Conversation, meetings, negotiations, group discussions, debates.
  • Speeches and presentations; giving or taking interviews.
  • Oral presentations; talks with several people for an MOU.

Advantages of speaking over writing

  • Face to face: immediate feedback and reading the listener’s reaction.
  • Allows clarification, dialogue and persuasion.
  • Builds credibility and rapport; more energising than reading.
  • Emotion conveyed through voice (commentary on cricket versus silent TV).
  • Clearer, more lucid, explanatory and spontaneous; reliable because of expressions.
  • Saves time; quick response; flexible tone and tenor (anger, sadness, joy, favour, instruction).
  • Voice can be modulated (also can be manipulated, as actors do).

Four components of effective speaking

  1. Voice – God’s gift but the way of speaking can be trained (singers train pitch); aim at clarity; avoid too fast (may signal fear) or too slow.
  2. Vocabulary – enriched over years; must use right words in the right place; words are context-based (e.g. ‘recycle’ for a computer person versus a pharmacist); use familiar words.
  3. Audience – know age, background, taste, gender and education; if unknown, imagine a mixed gathering; size affects enthusiasm.
  4. Style – enthusiasm and animation; audibility to the last row (check microphone); a vibrant start and varied tone, never monotone.

Quotations

  • “Half the world is full of people who have nothing to say and keep saying on; the other half has too much to say yet cannot say it.”
  • Shakespeare: men of few words are the best men.
  • Benjamin Franklin: “Remember not only to say the right thing in the right place, but far more difficult still, to leave unsaid the wrong thing at the tempting moment.”
  • Every speaker wins or loses in the beginning.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Components of effective speaking
ComponentKey idea
VoiceTrainable; clarity; modulation
VocabularyRight word, right place; familiar words
AudienceKnow and adapt
StyleEnthusiasm, audibility, variety
Speaking situations
SituationExample
InformalConversation
GroupMeeting, GD, debate, negotiation
Formal soloSpeech, presentation
InterviewGiving or taking

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Right word, right time — Benjamin Franklin

It is more difficult to leave unsaid the wrong thing at the tempting moment than to say the right thing in the right place.

Men of few words — William Shakespeare (as cited)

Men of few words are the best men.

Examples and applications

Example

Cricket commentary versus a silent television.

Example

Breaking the ice with a co-passenger on a train.

Example

Word ‘recycle’ in computing versus pharmacy.

Example

Speaking to a half-empty versus full class.

Comparison tables

Speaking versus writing
PointSpeakingWriting
TimeQuickSlower, can be delayed
FeedbackImmediateDelayed
Non-verbal cuesPresentAbsent
EmotionConveyed by voiceLimited
PermanenceFleeting (unless recorded)Permanent record

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • LSRW: this lecture is on speaking.
  • Four components: voice, vocabulary, audience, style.
  • Voice can be trained; the quality itself is a gift.
  • Use familiar words.
  • Know your audience; imagine a mixed audience if unknown.
  • Speakers win or lose at the start.
  • Audibility to the last row is essential.
  • Speaking can be modulated and even manipulated.
  • Franklin quotation on leaving unsaid the wrong thing.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType

W10 Q2p.34Importance of advanced speaking skillsMCQ
W10 Q3p.34Components of effective speakingMCQ
W10 Q11p.36Advanced speaking skillsTrue/False

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Speaking is a skill that complements writing: it is fast, face to face, emotive and spontaneous, builds rapport and credibility, and gives immediate feedback. It is needed in conversation, meetings, negotiations, GDs, debates, speeches, presentations and interviews. Effective speaking rests on four components – voice (trainable), vocabulary (familiar, context-appropriate), audience (know and adapt) and style (enthusiasm, audibility, variety). Franklin: leaving unsaid the wrong thing at the tempting moment is harder than saying the right thing.

Lecture 47: Oral Presentations, Speeches, and Debates

Week 10 · Course book PDF pages 768–783 (printed pages 767–782)

Learning objectives

  • Define oral presentation, speech and debate and differentiate them.
  • List occasions for oral presentations.
  • Explain the role of visual aids and the question–answer session.
  • Describe the purposes and features of speeches.
  • State how to prepare for and deliver a debate, including rebuttal and courtesy (Roosevelt).

Important definitions

Oral presentation

A planned, factual, professional talk to a small, defined audience to inform or persuade, usually with visual aids and a question–answer session leading to a decision.

Speech

Formal face-to-face oral communication to an audience for a purpose, relying on voice, vocabulary, gesture and posture, usually without visual aids.

Debate

A formal contest of arguments on a motion, with speakers for or against, requiring research, logic and rebuttal.

Visual aids

Charts, graphs, tables, film clips and live demonstrations used in presentations.

Briefing

A short presentation to update a group (press, vendors, senior executives).

Rebuttal

The time to counter the opponent’s arguments.

Orientation programme

A presentation introducing new members to an organisation or course.

Core concepts

Purposes of speaking

All speaking situations aim to inform, persuade or entertain.

Oral presentation

  • Given to a small defined group; more challenging because they listen to every word.
  • Factual and not too emotional; informative or persuasive.
  • Uses visual aids – charts, graphs, tables, film clips, live demo – to satisfy curiosity.
  • Leads to a decision (e.g. a paperless office, biometric attendance).
  • Question–answer session: anticipate questions and prepare answers; convince the audience.

Occasions for presentations

  • Budget presentation for a department/unit.
  • Briefing: the press, vendors, senior executives (progress updates).
  • Training and orientation of new employees, students, teachers.
  • Recruitment tips by placement officers.
  • Launching a new product and competing with others.

Speech

  • Occasions: degree ceremonies, Bhavan day, farewell, impromptu announcements; may inform, persuade or entertain.
  • Fear comes from unfamiliar crowds and lack of preparation.
  • Uses voice, vocabulary, style, gesture and posture.
  • Difference from presentation: speeches generally have no visual aids and the audience is usually larger.

Debate: value and skills

  • Builds argumentative ability and critical thinking (analysis, evaluation, conceptualisation).
  • Two sides: for or against the motion; opinion must be backed by logic and data.
  • Enriches speaking and arguing skills; topics may be literal, psychological, quotations, etc.

Preparing for a debate

  1. Research the topic (not only Google) and collect data.
  2. Draft the speech with conviction; use arguments supported by facts.
  3. Rehearse several times with a guide.
  4. Listen carefully to the opposition and prepare to counter.
  5. Use note cards that are legible.
  6. Use rebuttal time well – it can change the result.

On the day of the debate

  • Reach the venue well in time; be mentally, physically and psychologically ready.
  • Treat opponents with courtesy; greet panelists.
  • Use appropriate non-verbal behaviour; polite, harmless language even when contradicting.
  • Roosevelt: “Courtesy is as much a mark of a gentleman as courage.”

Types, classifications and characteristics

Occasions for oral presentations
OccasionAudience
Budget presentationColleagues, management
Press briefingMedia
Vendor briefingVendors
Executive briefingSenior executives
Training/orientationNew recruits, students, teachers
Product launchTeam, customers
Debate preparation
StepDetail
ResearchCollect facts and data
DraftLogical arguments
RehearsePractise delivery
ListenNote opposing points
RebuttalCounter effectively

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Courtesy and courage — Theodore Roosevelt (as cited)

Courtesy is as much a mark of a gentleman as courage.

Examples and applications

Example

Biometric attendance replacing registers.

Example

Budget presentation to colleagues.

Example

Impromptu announcement of a student’s name on Bhavan day.

Example

Rebuttal turning the result.

Comparison tables

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Speaking aims to inform, persuade or entertain.
  • Oral presentations suit small defined groups and use visual aids.
  • Prepare for the Q&A by anticipating questions.
  • Briefings: press, vendors, senior executives.
  • Speeches normally lack visual aids.
  • Debate develops argumentative ability and critical thinking.
  • Rebuttal time can decide the winner.
  • Reach the debate venue early.
  • Roosevelt: courtesy is as much a mark of a gentleman as courage.

W10 Q5p.34DebateMCQ
W10 Q6p.35SpeechesMCQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Speaking serves to inform, persuade or entertain. An oral presentation is a planned, factual talk to a small defined audience, using visual aids and ending with a question–answer session that leads to decisions; occasions include budgets, briefings, training and product launches. A speech is a formal oral address to a larger audience for an occasion, usually without visual aids. A debate develops argumentative ability and critical thinking and requires research, rehearsal, careful listening, effective rebuttal and courtesy.
