Lecture 48: Combating Nervousness

Week 10 · Course book PDF pages 784–799 (printed pages 783–798)

Learning objectives

  • Define nervousness and describe its physical and psychological symptoms.
  • Explain why nervousness is natural, universal and useful.
  • Recall the views of William James, Sartre, Dale Carnegie and Shakespeare.
  • List causes of nervousness and their remedies.
  • Apply stress busters during a talk.

Important definitions

Nervousness

A psychological and physiological state of anxiety/fear about performance, with cognitive, emotional and physical components.

Arousal

The keyed-up state caused by adrenaline surge in the body.

Adrenaline surge

Release of adrenaline that speeds the heartbeat and breathing and pumps extra energy.

Fight or flight

Psychologists’ term for the response to threat; the speaker should ‘fight’ nervousness.

Stress busters

Techniques such as deep breathing, pausing and speaking slowly to control nerves.

Rapport

Friendly connection built with the audience.

Core concepts

Symptoms

  • Butterflies and a sinking feeling in the stomach.
  • Dry throat, trembling hands, quivering voice.
  • Rapid heartbeat and fast speech; forgetting lines.
  • Feeling is mostly internal; the audience rarely notices.

Why people feel nervous

  • Fear of not performing well; concern about image before strangers.
  • Unfamiliar audience, unfamiliar or difficult topic, poor preparation, inexperience.
  • Survey cited: 80 per cent of people prefer death to public speaking.
  • Common to speakers, musicians, singers, cricketers, swimmers.

Nervousness is natural and useful

  • Natural and universal – even celebrities and experienced speakers feel it.
  • You are more conscious because you want to perform better.
  • William James: survival devices prepare us for an emergency – muscles tense for agility, heartbeat and breathing quicken, glands sharpen senses and give emergency energy.
  • It is energising and improves the talk; fears pass after the opening.
  • Analogy: a new Test batsman’s nerves vanish after the first ball; crossing a bridge carefully.
  • Sartre: people have a choice whether to be cowards.
  • Dale Carnegie: “I am OK, you are OK.” The audience will not attack you; worst case, the talk is not appreciated and you learn.
  • Shakespeare: “Cowards die many times before their death; the valiant taste of death but once.”

Remedies before the talk

  • Prepare well in advance – the best antidote or panacea.
  • Know the audience (age, education) and use examples from their field.
  • Begin with familiar topics; research thoroughly.
  • Practise before friends in small groups and take feedback.
  • Record yourself on a cell phone (audio/video) to review body language, pauses and accent.
  • Check the venue – acoustics, microphone, seating.
  • Treat the audience as friends; see their smiles and whispers as compliments.

Stress busters during the talk

  • Face the crowd: stand, pause and build rapport before the first word.
  • Take a deep breath; start with a pause, not a verbal firework.
  • Speak slowly and distinctly; experienced speakers keep a steady pace (slow and steady wins the race).
  • If you forget, review the last point; the next thought will come.
  • Concentrate on the topic, not on shaking hands; a dais hides trembling.
  • Tell yourself: ‘bear it, not fear it’.
  • Fear is in the mind; practise; learn from failures and imitate good speakers.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Symptoms of nervousness
TypeSymptoms
PhysicalDry throat, trembling, fast heartbeat, butterflies
VoiceQuivering, fast speech
CognitiveForgetting, worry about image
EmotionalAnxiety, arousal
Causes and remedies
CauseRemedy
Poor preparationPrepare well in advance
Unknown audienceResearch audience; treat as friends
Difficult topicStart with familiar topics
InexperiencePractise in small groups; record yourself
Venue worriesCheck venue in advance
ForgettingReview and pause

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Survival devices — William James

Nervousness is part of survival: the body prepares for emergency with energy.

I am OK, you are OK — Dale Carnegie (as cited)

Tell yourself you and the audience are fine.

Choice to be coward — Jean-Paul Sartre

People choose whether they are cowards.

Examples and applications

Example

New Test batsman facing bouncers.

Example

Crossing a bridge cautiously.

Example

Recording a talk on a cell phone.

Example

80% prefer death to public speaking.

Comparison tables

Experienced versus inexperienced speaker
PointExperiencedInexperienced
PaceSlow and steadyFast
StartPause, then speakRushes
NervesControls themOverwhelmed

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Nervousness is natural and universal.
  • It is energising because of adrenaline.
  • 80 per cent prefer death to public speaking (survey cited).
  • William James: survival devices.
  • Preparation is the best antidote.
  • Begin with familiar topics.
  • Record yourself on a cell phone.
  • Pause and breathe before starting.
  • Check the venue in advance.
  • Shakespeare: cowards die many times.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W10 Q7p.35Combating nervousnessMCQ
W10 Q8p.35Causes of nervousnessMCQ
W10 Q13p.36Anxiety and nervousnessTrue/False

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Nervousness is a natural, universal psychological and physiological response – dry throat, trembling, quivering voice, rapid heartbeat – caused by concern for one’s image and unfamiliarity. It is useful because adrenaline gives extra energy (William James). Control it through thorough preparation, knowing the audience, starting with familiar topics, rehearsing and recording yourself, checking the venue, treating the audience as friends, pausing and breathing before speaking, speaking slowly, and reviewing if you forget. Fear is in the mind: bear it, not fear it.

Q5 Fill The keyed-up state caused by adrenaline is called ______.

Lecture 49: Patterns and Methods of Presentation

Week 10 · Course book PDF pages 800–813 (printed pages 799–812)

Learning objectives

  • List the 5 Ps of presentations and the three stages of a presentation.
  • Describe the five patterns: chronological, causal, problem–solution, spatial, topical.
  • Describe the four methods of delivery: manuscript, memorised, impromptu, extemporaneous.
  • Evaluate the advantages and limitations of each method.
  • Recall the Shakespeare quotations used.

Important definitions

5 Ps

Preparation, Planning, Practice, Pattern, Performance.

Chronological pattern

Arranges material in time sequence, e.g. history of civilisation.

Causal pattern

Presents causes first and then effects (cause–effect relationship); informative.

Problem–solution pattern

Presents a problem (e.g. deforestation, pollution, global warming) and then offers solutions; persuasive.

Spatial pattern

Arranged by space or direction, e.g. design of a new product.

Topical pattern

Divides information into topics and sub-topics; easiest when others do not fit.

Reading from manuscript

Delivering a written text word for word.

Memorised method

Delivering a fully memorised text.

Impromptu method

Unprepared speech given on the spur of the moment.

Extemporaneous method

Flexible, prepared with notes/outline/slides, delivered conversationally.

Core concepts

5 Ps and stages

  • 5 Ps: preparation, planning, practice, pattern, performance.
  • Three stages: pre-presentation, during presentation, post-presentation.
  • Preparation is the best antidote to nervousness (fight or flight).

Five patterns of presentation

  1. Chronological – time sequence, historical events.
  2. Causal – causes then effects (e.g. causes and effects of nervousness).
  3. Problem–solution – problem then solution; persuasive.
  4. Spatial – space or direction, product design.
  5. Topical – topics and sub-topics; easiest.

Choose according to topic and information.

Reading from manuscript

  • Advantages: accuracy, suitable for technical or data-heavy subjects, carefully drafted words.
  • Limitations: no eye contact or rapport; no control over crowd; hard to be spontaneous; strain on eyes; little vocal variety (modulation, pauses); time limit pressure (“Damocles’ sword”); needs a practised reader; audience loses interest.
  • Not advisable for beginners.

Memorised method

  • Looks fresh and allows eye contact.
  • Problems: cannot memorise pauses, shifts in voice or transitions; speed and time control (may finish early); sounds like a robot; forgetting a part halts the talk; hard to prepare and sustain.

Impromptu method

  • No preparation: on the spot (name announced suddenly).
  • Challenging but rewarding; relies on past reading, experience and observations.
  • Be brief and to the point; take a moment, pick a keyword; summarise when ideas run out; resist the temptation to add; silence is better than saying nothing.
  • Be mentally prepared always.
  • Shakespeare: ‘It is best to speak few than to speak more’; ‘There is nothing either good or bad, but thinking makes it so.’

Extemporaneous method

  • Prepared flexibly: prepare more material than needed; use notes, slides and visual aids; outline ready so you can skip.
  • Conversational and interactive; gauge the audience’s mood and cut or squeeze points.
  • Needs expertise.
  • A speech may sound conversational but is not a conversation.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Patterns of presentation
PatternBasisExample
ChronologicalTimeHistory of civilisation
CausalCause → effectNervousness
Problem–solutionProblem → remedyDeforestation, pollution
SpatialSpace / directionProduct design
TopicalTopics and sub-topicsGeneral information
Methods of delivery
MethodStrengthWeakness
ManuscriptAccurateNo rapport; monotone
MemorisedFresh, eye contactForgetting; robotic
ImpromptuSpontaneous, rewardingNo preparation
ExtemporaneousFlexible, interactiveNeeds expertise and outline

Examples and applications

Example

Presentation on deforestation, soil erosion or global warming (problem–solution).

Example

Design of a new product (spatial).

Example

Name suddenly announced at a function (impromptu).

Comparison tables

Causal versus problem–solution
PointCausalProblem–solution
ContentCauses and effectsProblem and remedies
NatureInformativePersuasive
Impromptu versus extemporaneous
PointImpromptuExtemporaneous
PreparationNonePrepared with notes/outline
FlexibilityOn the spur of the momentPlanned flexibility
MaterialPast knowledgeMore material than needed

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • 5 Ps: preparation, planning, practice, pattern, performance.
  • Three stages: pre, during, post-presentation.
  • Five patterns: chronological, causal, problem–solution, spatial, topical.
  • Four methods: manuscript, memorised, impromptu, extemporaneous.
  • Manuscript reading limits eye contact.
  • Memorised talks fail when a part is forgotten.
  • Impromptu: be brief.
  • Extemporaneous: prepare more than needed; use outline.
  • Topical is the easiest pattern.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W10 Q14p.36Methods of presentationTrue/False

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Presentations are built on the 5 Ps and go through pre-, during and post-presentation stages. They may follow a chronological, causal, problem–solution, spatial or topical pattern. Delivery methods are reading from manuscript (accurate but lifeless), memorised (fresh but risky), impromptu (unprepared, brief, relies on experience) and extemporaneous (flexible, conversational, with outline and notes). Beginners should avoid reading and memorising; speaking extemporaneously with an outline is most effective.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ Which pattern arranges content by time sequence?
  1. Spatial
  2. Topical
  3. Chronological
  4. Causal
Answer: C
Why: Time and events.
Q2 MCQ Which method uses an outline and prepares more material than required?
  1. Manuscript
  2. Memorised
  3. Impromptu
  4. Extemporaneous
Answer: D
Why: Flexible method.
Q3 MSQ Limitations of reading from a manuscript include:
  1. No rapport
  2. Eye strain
  3. Little vocal variety
  4. Perfect spontaneity
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Spontaneity is lost.
Q4 T/F An impromptu speech should be long to impress the audience.
Answer: False
Why: Be brief and to the point.
Q5 Fill The pattern that divides information into topics and sub-topics is ______.
Answer: topical
Why: Easiest to use.
Q6 Short Why is the memorised method risky?
Answer: Forgetting one phrase can halt the talk; pauses and transitions are hard to memorise; speed is hard to control and delivery sounds robotic.
Why: Not advisable for novices.

Revision checklist

  • I know the 5 Ps.
  • I can name five patterns.
  • I can name four methods.
  • I know the limits of manuscript and memorised.
  • I know how to handle impromptu.

Lecture 50: Oral Presentation: Planning & Preparation

Week 10 · Course book PDF pages 814–831 (printed pages 813–830)

Learning objectives

  • Plan an oral presentation: purpose, topic, audience analysis, research, specific purpose statement.
  • Prepare content that is audience-specific and written to be spoken.
  • Structure the presentation into introduction, body and conclusion with time proportions.
  • Plan visual aids, repetition, signposts and anticipated questions.
  • Choose topics through an inventory and brainstorming.

Important definitions

Purpose statement

A specific statement of what the talk aims to do: add knowledge, persuade, inform of trends.

Audience analysis

Studying the age, background, education, faith, gender and interests of the listeners.

Audience-specific content

Content designed to benefit the particular audience.

Verbatim

Word for word, as in a full manuscript.

Inventory of topics

A personal record of topics and new information kept for future talks.

Signposts / transfer stations

Transitions and previews that guide the audience through the talk.

Call for action

A closing appeal in problem–solution talks urging the audience to act.

Core concepts

Planning sequence

  1. Know the purpose: why, whom and how.
  2. Decide the topic (given by host or chosen by you).
  3. Analyse the audience.
  4. Research and gather information.
  5. Formulate a specific purpose statement.
  6. Prepare content and visual aids.
  7. Divide into beginning, middle, end.
  8. Plan for questions and practise.

Choosing the topic

  • Pick a topic familiar to you (reduces nervousness) but add something new for the audience.
  • Keep an inventory of topics of interest from school/college days; update it continuously (library, internet).
  • Brainstorm about the audience’s tastes, habits, interests, beliefs.
  • Mix the new and the old: familiarity increases receptivity.

Audience analysis

  • Many good presentations fail because the speaker did not understand the audience.
  • Consider age, background, education, faith, gender, rural/urban, profession; mixed gatherings are common.
  • Make the talk beneficial to them.

Preparing the content

  • Good content matters more than a good voice.
  • Write for the ear: compose word by word (manuscript method) but keep it spoken.
  • Short sentences that speak; suitable, simple words; avoid jargon, clichés, circumlocution, idioms and phrases for second-language audiences.
  • Use repetition and summarising (e.g. Martin Luther King’s ‘I have a dream’).
  • Insert transitions and signposts; plan places for pauses and shifts.

Visual aids

  • Use for technical subjects; pictures aid understanding.
  • Decide in advance where each aid will appear and mark it in the outline.
  • Make aids relevant, specific and accessible; avoid merely catchy ones.

Three parts: beginning, middle, end

  • Introduction – capture attention (quote, anecdote, story, e.g. Nehru’s ‘Tryst with destiny’); state purpose; preview main points; build credibility and rapport. About 2 minutes of a 20-minute talk; do not make it long.
  • Body – the heart of the matter; limit to two or three points, each explained and supported; simple, logical, stepwise.
  • Conclusion – inference from the body; restate and recapitulate so the audience carries something home; call for action in problem–solution talks; signal the end (‘to conclude…’); avoid an abrupt or limp ending.

Questions and notes

  • Encourage questions; anticipate them and prepare answers.
  • Prepare more material than required but be ready to skip.
  • Note cards with legible keywords; leave space in the script to look up at the audience.
  • Use quotations and references for support; create animation through word choice.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Structure of a presentation
PartRoleGuidance
IntroductionCapture attention; previewAbout 10% of time; quote, anecdote, story
BodyDevelop 2–3 pointsLogical, supported, simple
ConclusionRecapitulate; call for actionNatural signal to end; invite questions
Planning checklist
StepQuestion to ask
PurposeWhy am I speaking and to whom?
TopicIs it familiar and useful to them?
AudienceAge, background, education?
ContentAre sentences short and spoken?
AidsWhere will each be used?
QuestionsWhat will they ask?

Examples and applications

Example

Martin Luther King’s repetition ‘I have a dream’.

Example

Nehru’s ‘Tryst with destiny’ opening.

Example

Presentation on modern cell phones adding special new information.

Example

20-minute talk with a 2-minute introduction.

Comparison tables

Good versus poor introduction
PointGoodPoor
OpeningQuote, anecdote, storyLong, dull background
LengthAbout 10% of timeExcessively long
EffectCaptures attention, rapportLoses audience

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Planning starts with purpose, topic and audience.
  • Speak on a familiar topic and add new information.
  • Maintain an inventory of topics.
  • Write short sentences meant to be spoken.
  • Repetition helps (Martin Luther King).
  • Decide positions of visual aids in the outline.
  • Three parts: beginning, body, end.
  • Introduction ≈ 2 minutes in a 20-minute talk.
  • Restrict body to two or three points.
  • Conclusion restates and may call for action.

QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType

W10 Q10p.35Selecting a topicMCQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Plan an oral presentation by fixing the purpose, selecting a familiar yet fresh topic (using an inventory and brainstorming), analysing the audience and researching. Draft audience-specific content in short, simple, spoken sentences with repetition, signposts and planned visual aids. Structure it into a brief attention-grabbing introduction, a body of two or three well-supported points and a conclusion that recapitulates and, if needed, calls for action. Anticipate questions, prepare notes and extra material, and then practise.

  1. 2 minutes
  2. 10 minutes
  3. 15 minutes
  4. 5 minutes after the body
Answer: A
Why: Do not make it long.

  1. Two or three
  2. Ten
  3. Fifteen
  4. As many as possible
Answer: A

Q3 MSQ Good ways to open a presentation:
  1. Quotation
  2. Anecdote
  3. Story
  4. Apology for being unprepared
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Capture attention.
Q4 T/F A speech written to be spoken should use long, complex sentences.
Answer: False
Why: Use short sentences that speak.
Q5 Fill Keeping an ______ of topics helps in impromptu situations.
Answer: inventory

Q6 Short What should the conclusion contain?
Answer: An inference from the body, a restatement or recapitulation of main points, a call for action if relevant, and a natural signal that the talk is ending.

Lecture 51: Making Effective Presentations

Week 11 · Course book PDF pages 832–851 (printed pages 831–850)

Learning objectives

  • List the features of an effective presentation: beginning, voice, body language, humour, examples, ending.
  • Explain the role of practice and rehearsal, including timing and self-recording.
  • Describe delivery factors: expressiveness, rate, volume, pitch, articulation, appearance.
  • Use visual aids, PPT slides and connectives effectively.
  • State tips for the delivery day and recall Freud and Confucius.

Important definitions

Effective presentation

A presentation that captivates at the start, is clear in voice and body language, uses humour and familiar examples, integrates aids and ends emphatically.

Rehearsal (practice)

Repeated, timed practice of the presentation, ideally recorded and reviewed.

Expressiveness

The quality of voice and body in conveying the intended meaning.

Rate

Speed of speaking in words per minute.

Pitch movement / intonation

Rise and fall of voice to give emphasis and emotion.

Connectives (signposts)

Transitions, internal previews and internal summaries that show the audience where the speaker is.

Lectern

A stand for notes that may also hide nervousness.

Desensitisation

Telling oneself positive things and visualising success to reduce anxiety.

Core concepts

Effective beginning

  • Capture attention: a question, story, anecdote, episode or startling fact; avoid an abrupt start.
  • Example: “There is one thing common to all of us…and that is nervousness.”
  • Memorise the first lines and any quotation fully, with the name of the author, to avoid embarrassment.

Voice and body language

  • Voice: clear and articulate; speak slowly enough that words are not eaten; voice can be trained.
  • Body language: eye contact with all sections of the audience; stand firmly; use space naturally; gestures that match the message; avoid too frequent movement and fidgeting.
  • Freud: “He who has eyes to see and ears to listen can convince himself that no mortal can keep a secret…betrayal oozes out of him at every pore.”

Humour and examples

  • Humour relieves technical subjects (e.g. dentist joke) and sustains attention.
  • Use familiar examples suited to the audience (sports, music, movies; for yoga, a mix).

Ending

End emphatically with a restatement and signposts (‘before I close…’); summarise main points; do not end abruptly or limply.

Practice and rehearsal

  • Confidence cannot be gained in a day; rehearse repeatedly.
  • Prepare a final outline; do not read word for word.
  • Time yourself with a clock; cut points if too long; record on a cell phone to review body language, eye contact and voice.
  • Be your own critic; give small presentations to friends for honest feedback; use a mirror.

PPT slides

  • Use slides as an outline: only essential points, a limited number per slide; no clutter or reading from slides (same as reading from manuscript).
  • Animation without ‘music of thought’ is useless.

Delivery factors

  • Expressiveness of voice and body.
  • Rate: moderate – the lecture cites 120–130 words per minute versus 180 as too fast.
  • Volume: audible; adjust to hall, crowd and acoustics; keep a little distance from the microphone.
  • Pitch and intonation: vary emphasis.
  • Articulation: clear syllables.
  • Appearance: formal, presentable dress, haircut, bearing; avoid distracting items (earrings, cell phones).

Visual aids and connectives

  • Integrate aids at the relevant point; interpret them; do not stand between the aid and the audience (OHP failure).
  • Use pointers properly.
  • In speech we use connectives instead of punctuation: transitions, internal previews, internal summaries; signposts such as ‘finally’, ‘last but not the least’, ‘before I close’.

Tips

  • Prepare thoroughly, practise repeatedly, time yourself.
  • Use a lectern if needed; microphone etiquette (no shouting).
  • Stress busters: desensitise, visualise success, begin with a pause, speak the first sentence from memory, keep eye contact, slow down.
  • Move naturally; ignore smiles and whispers or treat them as appreciation.
  • Do not read, do not memorise everything, no long introduction, no ‘verbal bullets’, avoid frowning.
  • Keep writing and speaking when using a blackboard.
  • Stand with one foot ahead of the other; avoid fidgeting.
  • Give handouts after; stay for questions; if you cannot answer, offer to discuss later; end with summary and appreciation.
  • Confucius: “A superior man is modest in his speech but exceeds in his actions.”

Types, classifications and characteristics

Features of effective delivery
FeatureGuidance
BeginningQuestion, story, anecdote, startling fact
VoiceClear, moderate rate, audible, varied pitch
Body languageEye contact, natural gestures, firm stance
Humour/examplesFamiliar, relevant
AidsIntegrated, interpreted
EndingEmphatic recapitulation
Connectives
TypeRole
TransitionsMove to the next idea
Internal previewsAnnounce what comes next
Internal summariesRecap points so far

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Body betrays the speaker — Sigmund Freud

The body speaks even when the lips are silent.

Modest speech, strong action — Confucius

A superior man is modest in his speech but exceeds in his actions.

Examples and applications

Example

Speaker forgetting half of his opening quotation.

Example

Dentist humour.

Example

OHP presenter standing between the transparency and the audience.

Example

Self-recording on a cell phone.

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Start with a question, story, anecdote or startling fact.
  • Memorise opening and closing lines.
  • Rate cited: 120–130 words per minute.
  • Do not read slides.
  • Time yourself and record your practice.
  • Dress formally; check appearance.
  • Do not stand between aid and audience.
  • Use connectives and signposts.
  • Freud: the body speaks.
  • Confucius: modest in speech, exceed in action.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W10 Q4p.34Effective oral presentationMCQ
W10 Q7p.35Combating nervousnessMCQ
W11 Q1p.37Delivery of presentationsMCQ
W11 Q2p.37Evaluating presentationsMCQ
W11 Q11p.39Evaluating presentationsTrue/False

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

An effective presentation opens with a captivating hook, uses a clear voice at a moderate rate, appropriate volume and varied pitch, supportive body language and eye contact, humour and familiar examples, well-integrated aids and signposts, and an emphatic ending. Success depends on rehearsal: outline, timing, recording and feedback. Slides should be an outline, not a script. On the day, begin with a pause, stay natural, dress formally, handle questions gracefully and end with a summary. Freud reminds that the body speaks; Confucius that a superior man is modest in speech.

  2. A startling fact or question
  3. A long apology
  4. Reading the title slowly

  1. Extemporaneous speaking
  2. Reading from a manuscript
  3. Impromptu speaking
  4. Debating

  1. Timing with a clock
  2. Recording on a cell phone
  3. Using a mirror
  4. Never rehearsing

Q5 Fill Speakers should begin with a ______ rather than rush.

Q6 Short What are connectives in a talk?
Answer: Transitions, internal previews and internal summaries that signal movement from one idea to the next.
Why: Replace punctuation in speech.

  • I recall Freud and Confucius.

Lecture 52: Speeches for Various Occasions

Week 11 · Course book PDF pages 852–868 (printed pages 851–867)

Learning objectives

  • Explain why professionals need to give occasion speeches.
  • Differentiate speeches from presentations.
  • Stress the importance of the beginning and closing, with the Antony and Frost illustrations.
  • Describe the types: welcome, introductory, felicitation, commemorative, farewell, vote of thanks.
  • State the do’s of each type.

Important definitions

Speech

A formal, subjective, emotion-soaked oral address for a specific occasion, without visual aids, depending on voice and language.

Welcome speech

A speech welcoming the chief guest and the audience, with accurate facts about the guest.

Introductory speech

A speech introducing a guest or speaker to the audience, praising achievements without embarrassment or exaggeration.

Felicitation speech

A speech honouring and celebrating a person’s achievements or award.

Commemorative speech

A speech honouring the memory of a person who is no more, in a sorrowful tone.

Farewell speech

A leave-taking speech, in a subdued tone, by the organisation or by the departing person.

Vote of thanks

Closing speech thanking everyone in order of seniority with reasons, briefly and warmly.

Core concepts

Why speeches

  • We maintain social and professional relationships; occasions arise at work: welcoming guests, retirements, honours, tributes, farewells.
  • Notice may be only a day or two, so always be ready.

Speech versus presentation

  • Presentation: technical, complex, objective, with visual aids.
  • Speech: emotional, sentimental, subjective, no visual aids; the speaker is the visual.
  • Language: may be ornate or figurative but must stay simple and understandable; style varies by occasion.

Power of opening and closing

  • The beginning should generate intimacy; the closing leaves a lasting impression (Longfellow: great is the art of beginning, but greater the art of ending).
  • Opening illustration: in Shakespeare’s Julius Caesar Antony wins over a hostile crowd by a courteous, subtle opening that asks for their attention and gradually reverses their opinion of Caesar as a traitor; ‘half the battle is won in the beginning’.
  • Closing illustration: a convocation address ending with a famous poem’s lines about promises to keep and miles to go (Robert Frost) reminds graduates that life is a greater examination.

Welcome speech

  • Gather facts about the guest; get the name and achievements exactly right.
  • Welcome the chair, dignitaries, the guest and the audience; say why the guest is important and how the audience will benefit.
  • Example: welcoming a renowned Indian English poet-novelist to a conference of English teachers.

Introductory speech

  • Start with salutations and an engaging note; give achievements in short sentences.
  • Do not speak too long; no embarrassing or untrue claims.
  • Example: introducing Mukesh Ambani – joined Reliance in 1981, chairman in 2002, awards for telecom; end by inviting applause.

Felicitation speech

  • Honour achievements; talk about the laurel, award or recognition and its impact.
  • Example: felicitating a nominee to the World Bank for work benefiting farmers; use auspicious, respectful language.

Commemorative speech

  • Honour the memory of a departed person; talk of loss and contribution.
  • Tone is sorrowful; words figurative and suited to the occasion.
  • Example: Pandit Nehru’s address on the death of Mahatma Gandhi – light gone out, ‘Bapu’, ‘Father of the Nation’.

Farewell speech

  • Subdued tone; talk of the person’s association and contribution; mention that he will be missed.
  • If you are the one leaving: control emotions, thank organisers, be polite, brief and humble; do not exaggerate your contribution.
  • No criticism and no excessive praise.

Vote of thanks

  • Acknowledge everyone, in order of seniority, with reasons; include supporting staff.
  • Brief but warm; change the language from the report acknowledgement; a touch of humour is welcome.
  • Example: ‘I am thankful to Mr X for his valuable contribution…’.

Closing quotation

Buddha: the wise fashion speech with thought, sifting it as grain is sifted through a sieve – speak only filtered, valuable thoughts. Work hopefully and prepare for every speech situation.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Types of occasion speeches
TypePurposeTone / key point
WelcomeWelcome guest and audienceRespectful; accurate facts
IntroductoryIntroduce the speakerConcise; no exaggeration
FelicitationHonour achievementsCelebratory
CommemorativeRemember the departedSorrowful, subdued
FarewellBid goodbyeWarm, humble, brief
Vote of thanksThank allOrdered, brief, warm

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Art of ending — Henry Wadsworth Longfellow (as cited)

Great is the art of beginning, but greater the art of ending.

Speech as sifted thought — Buddha (as cited)

The wise shape speech with thought, sifting it like grain through a sieve.

Examples and applications

Example

Antony’s funeral speech from Julius Caesar (opening).

Example

Convocation address closing with lines from Robert Frost.

Example

Nehru’s speech on Gandhi’s death.

Example

Welcome to Prof. Shiv Kumar; introduction of Mukesh Ambani; felicitation on World Bank nomination.

Comparison tables

Speech versus presentation
PointSpeechPresentation
NatureEmotional, subjectiveTechnical, objective
Visual aidsNoneYes
LanguageOrnate/figurative but simplePlain, factual
PurposeOccasion (honour, thanks, welcome)Inform/persuade
Welcome versus introductory speech
PointWelcomeIntroductory
AddresseeGuest and audienceAudience about the speaker
ContentGratitude and welcomeAchievements and background

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Six speech types: welcome, introductory, felicitation, commemorative, farewell, vote of thanks.
  • Speeches have no visual aids.
  • Beginning generates intimacy; closing leaves a lasting impression.
  • Welcome speech welcomes both guest and audience.
  • Do not exaggerate in introductions or farewells.
  • Commemorative speech uses a sorrowful tone.
  • Vote of thanks follows seniority and gives reasons.
  • Longfellow: art of ending.
  • Buddha: speech sifted like grain.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W11 Q3p.37Vote of thanksMCQ
W11 Q4p.37Welcome speech vs vote of thanksMCQ
W11 Q12p.39Opening and closing a speechTrue/False

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Professionals must be ready to give speeches on occasions. Unlike presentations, speeches are emotional, subjective and without visual aids, so language, style, and especially the opening and closing matter, as illustrated by Antony’s speech and a convocation ending. The six types are welcome, introductory, felicitation, commemorative, farewell and vote of thanks, each with its own tone and conventions: accurate facts, respect, no exaggeration, humility, brevity and warmth. Speak only sifted, valuable thoughts.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ Which speech honours the memory of a departed person?
  1. Welcome
  2. Felicitation
  3. Commemorative
  4. Vote of thanks
Answer: C
Why: Delivered in a sorrowful tone.
Q2 MCQ Visual aids are normally used in:
  1. Speeches
  2. Oral presentations
  3. Farewell speeches
  4. Vote of thanks
Answer: B
Why: Speeches rely on the speaker.
Q3 MSQ A vote of thanks should be:
  1. In order of seniority
  2. Brief
  3. With reasons for thanks
  4. Critical
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Never critical.
Q4 T/F A farewell speech should criticise the departing person.
Answer: False
Why: No criticism or exaggeration.
Q5 Fill A speech’s beginning should generate ______.
Answer: intimacy
Why: It connects with the crowd.
Q6 Short What should be done in a welcome speech?
Answer: Welcome the chief guest and the audience, state the guest’s identity and importance accurately, and explain how the audience will benefit.
Why: Gather accurate facts.

Revision checklist

  • I can differentiate speech and presentation.
  • I can list six types.
  • I know each type’s tone.
  • I know how to open and close.
  • I recall Longfellow and Buddha.

Lecture 53: Interviews

Week 11 · Course book PDF pages 869–884 (printed pages 868–883)

Learning objectives

  • Define an interview and explain the origin of the word.
  • List the types of interview: informative, persuasive, exit, evaluation, counselling, conflict resolution, disciplinary, termination, job.
  • State general skills needed by an interviewee.
  • Describe the stages of the recruitment process.
  • List attributes recruiters look for.

Important definitions

Interview

A face-to-face, mostly oral, systematic communication in which an interviewer tests the traits and qualities of an interviewee for a purpose; from French ‘entrevue’ (a sight between), glossed in the lecture as ‘to see the other person’.

Interviewer / interviewee

The person taking the interview / the person being interviewed.

Information-gathering interview

An interview to collect data, e.g. by phone or Skype when time is short.

Persuasive interview

An interview to convince a person, e.g. to close a deal or to correct performance.

Exit interview

An interview with a departing employee to learn problems and get valuable suggestions.

Evaluation interview

A periodic progress review between a senior and a subordinate.

Counselling interview

An interview to motivate and boost the morale of employees.

Conflict-resolution interview

An interview to resolve conflict between departments or persons.

Disciplinary interview

A fact-finding interview by a disciplinary committee after a mistake.

Termination interview

An interview before ending an employee’s service (may involve a ‘golden handshake’).

Stress interview

An interview that tests the candidate in a stressful situation with challenging questions.

Screening

First stage where applications and CVs are sifted.

Core concepts

Nature of an interview

  • Occurs in jobs, admissions, hospitals, hostels and organisations throughout life.
  • Orality combined with non-verbal skills.
  • Pauline V. Young: an interview is a systematic method by which a person enters imaginatively into the inner life of a comparative stranger.
  • Nervousness arises because interviewers are strangers.

Types of interviews

  1. Informative (data collection; telephonic, Skype).
  2. Persuasive (convincing in deals or to improve performance).
  3. Exit – leaving employees give honest, constructive inputs; avoid bad-mouthing.
  4. Evaluation – progress reviews.
  5. Counselling – morale boosting.
  6. Conflict resolution between departments.
  7. Disciplinary – fact finding.
  8. Termination – final discussion; golden handshake.
  9. Job interview (selection).

Skills for the interviewee

  • Confidence – built by understanding the purpose and gathering information.
  • Listen carefully to the whole question before answering.
  • Anticipate questions; use intelligence for tricky ones (like Yorkers, googlies and bouncers in cricket).
  • Use formal language; no informal expressions.
  • Know your strengths and weaknesses; present weaknesses so they become merits; avoid over- or underestimating yourself.
  • Understand the interviewer’s needs and expectations.
  • Behave well: every person present is observing.

Importance of preparation

John D. Singleton: “Very often the difference between the person hired and the person rejected is not who is the better candidate, but who is better prepared for the interview.” Years of learning are judged in half an hour; careers can be made or lost there.

Stages of recruitment

  1. Screening of applications and CVs (saleable résumé is essential).
  2. Competency tests to eliminate candidates.
  3. Psychological tests.
  4. Group discussion / case.
  5. Negotiation.
  6. Stress interview.
  7. Medical test (last).

What recruiters look for

  • A good person, competent, an asset to the organisation; well informed and well rounded; good personality.
  • Attitude, adaptability, aptitude, behaviour, team spirit and dynamics.
  • Clarity of purpose, thought and expression; decision-making ability.
  • Originality and innovative mind; cheerful disposition.
  • Find information through newspapers (e.g. Times of India Ascent), Employment News and company websites.

Getting started

Mark Twain: the secret of getting ahead is getting started. Build confidence, revise the lectures on personality and positive thinking, prepare a good résumé to get the call (next lecture).

Types, classifications and characteristics

Types of interviews and purposes
TypePurpose
Information gatheringCollect data quickly

ExitLearn from departing employee
EvaluationReview progress
CounsellingBoost morale
Conflict resolutionSettle disputes
DisciplinaryFind facts on misconduct
TerminationEnd service
JobSelect candidates
Stages of recruitment

ScreeningSift CVs

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Interview as systematic method — Pauline V. Young

An interview is a systematic method by which a person imaginatively enters the inner life of a comparative stranger.

Preparation decides selection — John D. Singleton

The hired candidate is often the better prepared, not the better.

Getting ahead — Mark Twain

The secret of getting ahead is getting started.

Examples and applications

Example

Telephonic or Skype interview for data collection.

Example

Comparison tables

Exit versus termination interview
PointExit interviewTermination interview
InitiatorEmployee leavingEmployer ending service
PurposeFeedback and inputsFinal discussion / settlement
ToneConstructiveFormal, sensitive
Informative versus persuasive interview
PointInformativePersuasive
AimCollect informationChange opinion/action
ExampleTelephonic data collectionPersuading a client or employee

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Interview: face-to-face, mostly oral, systematic.
  • Pauline V. Young’s definition.
  • Types include exit, evaluation, counselling, conflict resolution, disciplinary and termination.
  • Develop confidence by gathering information.
  • Listen fully before answering.
  • Use formal language.
  • Stages: screening, competency test, psychological test, GD, negotiation, stress interview, medical.
  • Recruiters seek attitude, adaptability, aptitude, team spirit.
  • Singleton: preparation decides.
  • Twain: get started.
Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W11 Q5p.37Types of interviewsMCQ
W11 Q6p.38Job interview processMCQ

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

An interview is a systematic, mostly oral face-to-face method to test an interviewee’s traits; it has many forms: informative, persuasive, exit, evaluation, counselling, conflict resolution, disciplinary, termination and job interviews. Success needs confidence from information, careful listening, anticipation of questions, formal language, awareness of strengths and weaknesses and understanding the interviewer’s expectations. Recruitment proceeds through screening, tests, GD, negotiation, stress interview and medical test; recruiters seek attitude, adaptability, aptitude, team spirit and clarity. Preparation (Singleton) and getting started (Twain) matter most.

  2. Persuasive
  3. Disciplinary
  4. Evaluation
Answer: A
Why: To gain constructive inputs.

  1. Screening
  2. GD
  3. Stress interview
  4. Medical test
Answer: D
Why: Medical test comes last.
Q3 MSQ Which are types of interview?
  1. Counselling
  2. Conflict resolution
  3. Disciplinary
  4. Advertisement
Answer: A, B, C
Why: Advertisement is not an interview.
Q4 T/F Informal language is acceptable in interviews.
Answer: False
Why: Use formal language.

Why: CVs are sifted.
Q6 Short What does a counselling interview aim at?

Lecture 54: Planning and Preparation: Part-1

Week 11 · Course book PDF pages 885–902 (printed pages 884–901)

Learning objectives

  • List the preparatory steps before applying for a job.
  • Explain self-analysis: who am I, achievements, strengths, weaknesses, values, personality, background, hobbies and career goals.
  • Explain why researching the organisation and the job position matters.
  • Differentiate intuitive skills from learned skills.
  • Describe the interview file and the will to win.

Important definitions

Self-analysis

Honest examination of one’s qualifications, achievements, strengths, weaknesses, values, personality and goals before choosing a job.

USP (unique selling product/proposition)

The one skill in which a person is most confident, which becomes a personal trademark for which they are called.

Intuitive skills

Skills already present within a person, such as adaptability, assertiveness, creativity, resourcefulness and discretion, which can be further developed.

Learned skills

Skills acquired through effort, such as computer programming, marketing, interpersonal skills, speaking, organising events, time management, coordination, leadership and writing.

Interview file

An organised file of résumé, certificates, degrees, experience letters, awards, scholarships and newspaper clippings prepared before the interview.

Corporate culture

The work culture, language, values and ways of functioning of an organisation.

Panel interview

An interview in which several people judge the candidate together.

Will to win

The desire to succeed and the urge to reach one’s full potential (Confucius, as cited).

Core concepts

Preparatory steps

  1. Analyse yourself (self-analysis).
  2. Identify your skills.
  3. Research the organisation.
  4. Know the job position (roles, responsibilities, expectations).
  5. Revise subject knowledge and update skills.
  6. Draft the résumé and build the interview file.

Choice alone is not the only ingredient in getting a job; qualification, skills and fit matter. Choosing a career in line with one’s qualification avoids becoming a misfit.

Know yourself: who am I?

  • People either underestimate or overestimate themselves.
  • Ask ‘Who am I?’: think of qualifications, achievements (debates, speeches, sports, best paper award) and strengths.
  • Everyone has some quality: speaking, writing, convincing, leadership, initiative, computing (C++, Oracle).
  • Match strengths to jobs: good talkers and interviewers suit HR or placement cells.

Planning, goals and values

  • Have a plan: e.g. write books, become CEO, head the training and placement cell, general manager or regional head.
  • Be clear about goals; review past achievements (papers, patents, books, contribution to the field).
  • Prioritise the skill in which you are most confident; it becomes your USP.
  • Decide what you value: money, status, power, position, recognition as a researcher, administrative acumen.
  • Values shape the job choice: administrative service for those wanting a group; a laboratory or cabin for researchers.

Personality and behaviour

  • Do you work with a group or in isolation? A person who wants to work in isolation is not fit for today’s organisations.
  • Mental alertness, anticipation and foresight (a visionary).
  • Work style: aggressive or democratic; slow but steady; perfectionist or specialist.
  • How fast or slow you think.

Background, hobbies and goals

  • Background: schools, exposure to the outside world, accomplishments (grades, distinctions, papers).
  • Hobbies and interests may be asked, since recruiters want a well-rounded person, not merely bookish; workplace requires social relationships and responsibility.
  • Career goals must be defined.

Research the organisation

  • Visit the company website (e.g. Wipro, Infosys, L&T, Reliance, Reliance Jio): operations, growth rate, history, work culture, language of correspondence, new products.
  • Know the corporate culture, faiths and cultures of an MNC workforce and the language used.
  • Questions may come from the organisation’s recent achievements; be updated.

Know the job

  • Understand roles, responsibilities, challenges, special duties, career prospects, enhancement opportunities and promotions.
  • You may be asked which task you can do more comfortably; psychologists may create challenging situations to test your response.
  • Anticipate beforehand.

Update subject knowledge and skills

  • Revise subject knowledge; a master’s done long ago needs updating.
  • A skilled person never sits idle (e.g. a cobbler, car or bike mechanic).
  • Today’s organisations need multiple skills; a person who says he will do only one job is a ‘gone case’.

Intuitive and learned skills

  • Intuitive (inborn): adaptability, assertiveness, creativity, resourcefulness, efficiency, leadership, broad-mindedness, discretion, ability to assimilate differing opinions.
  • Learned: computer/programming skills, marketing and deal making, interpersonal skills, speaking on occasions, organising events/conferences/workshops, training, time management, coordination, administrative and leadership skills, writing for different occasions.

Interview file and will to win

  • Develop the interview file well in advance; do not be at ‘sixes and sevens’ on the day.
  • Contents: résumé that sells, certificates, degrees, diplomas in proper order, experience certificates, conference/competition/workshop certificates, awards, scholarships, fellowships, newspaper clippings for a quick glance.
  • Be confident that the recruiter is waiting for you.
  • Confucius (as cited): “The will to win, the desire to succeed, the urge to reach your full potential: these are the keys that will unlock the door to personal excellence.”
  • Winners do things with a difference; those not selected get another chance.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Will to win — Confucius (as cited)

The will to win, the desire to succeed and the urge to reach full potential unlock personal excellence.

Examples and applications

A graduate in a relevant field switching to an unrelated job becomes a misfit.

Example

Applying to Wipro, Infosys, L&T or Reliance Jio: study the website.

Someone with strong speaking and convincing ability choosing HR, placement or recruitment cell.

Cobbler and mechanic as skilled people who never sit idle.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Preparation for an interview begins long before the call. Analyse yourself honestly (qualifications, achievements, strengths, weaknesses, values, personality, background, hobbies, goals), identify and prioritise your skills to form a USP, and research both the organisation (website, growth, culture, new products) and the job (roles, responsibilities, prospects). Revise subject knowledge and update skills. Skills are intuitive (adaptability, assertiveness, creativity, resourcefulness) or learned (computer, marketing, speaking, time management, leadership, writing). Build an interview file and a saleable résumé, and keep the will to win.

Lecture 55: Planning and Preparation: Part II

Week 11 · Course book PDF pages 903–924 (printed pages 902–923)

Learning objectives

  • Differentiate résumé and CV with their etymologies.
  • State the purpose and requirements of a standard résumé.
  • List special skills and personal attributes recruiters want.
  • Describe the three formats: chronological, functional, hybrid.
  • Explain the parts of a résumé opening: heading, career objective, summary of qualification.
  • State do’s and don’ts of résumé writing.

Important definitions

Résumé

From French, ‘to summarise’: a document summarising one’s achievements, education, experience and future intentions to create interest in one’s candidature.

Curriculum vitae (CV)

From Latin, ‘the course of life’ / details of qualification: a more detailed document than a résumé; the two terms are often used interchangeably.

Career objective

A specific statement of the position sought and the skills to be used, expressing short- and long-term goals.

Chronological résumé

Lists experience in time order from the latest to the earliest; suits experienced candidates.

Functional (skill) résumé

Emphasises skills and abilities; suits entry-level candidates.

Hybrid / combinational résumé

A mix of chronological and functional formats.

Action verbs

Past-tense verbs that ‘speak’, such as accomplished, assisted, demonstrated, identified, investigated, participated, supervised, streamlined.

References (referees)

People who can verify one’s capability; supplied if asked.

Scannable résumé

A résumé on good-quality paper that can be scanned or sent as an attachment.

Core concepts

Résumé versus CV

  • Résumé (French): a summary; CV (Latin): curriculum vitae, more detailed.
  • Used interchangeably; some organisations ask for one, some for the other.
  • A résumé accompanies an application letter.

Purpose of a résumé

  • The first round of selection (elimination) is through the résumé.
  • Its purpose is to kindle interest in your candidature and win an interview call.
  • Many candidates fail by being too weighty or poorly framed.

Requirements of a standard résumé

  • Formatted (no handwritten résumé today) in a systematic manner; many organisations give a proforma, others test your own format.
  • Specific to the advertised post and its required skills.
  • Error-free in grammar and spelling.
  • Broken into units, with crisp phrases and specific words; use action verbs.
  • Positive image: ‘sell’ yourself (e.g. academically gifted, leader); relate your best features and relegate weaknesses; do not overestimate yourself.

Contents

  • Career goals/objective, education, work experience, activities, honours, rewards, awards, special skills, references.

Special skills

  • Computer proficiency (paperless office).
  • Foreign-language proficiency (multicultural workplaces).
  • Writing ability and interpersonal/human skills.
  • Problem-solving and decision-making.

Personal attributes

  • Enthusiasm, initiative, motivation, adaptability, sense of humour, friendliness; also gregarious, self-starter, manager, good speaker/writer, creative, communicative.
  • Today’s organisations are people organisations.

Education, experience and references

  • Education: school, college, university, other courses.
  • Experience: even brief or small experiences count; freshers can cite fests, group projects, reports, responsibilities, with their role clearly stated.
  • Mention gaps and job changes accurately; longer service helps; be honest if terminated (they may ask).
  • References: former or present employers, colleagues, professors; not relatives; write ‘available on request’ and give only when asked.

Types of résumé

  1. Chronological – experienced; latest to earliest; shows work style.
  2. Functional / skill – entry-level; highlights skills.
  3. Hybrid / combinational – blend of the two.

Opening of a résumé

  • Heading: exact name as on certificates (no nickname), permanent and campus address, an active phone number, a frequently checked email, and URL/web page if any.
  • Career objective: specific, not generalised. Examples: ‘To obtain a position as a software engineer allowing me to utilise my knowledge of Oracle and Linux’; ‘To obtain a post of marketing support representative where skills in administration and management, program development, public relations and writing can be used’.
  • Summary of qualification: start with the highest qualification, in order (e.g. B.Tech in computer science with specialisation in networking).

Style and length

  • Use active statements and phrases consistently, not a mix of sentences and phrases; begin with action verbs; avoid weak words like ‘help’; avoid ‘I was in charge of…’.
  • Length: 1 to 1.5 pages for entry-level candidates; even one page is fine if it shows skills.
  • Use good-quality paper; the résumé may be scanned.

Do’s and don’ts

  • Do: mention major activities, job objective, use proper headings, be honest, be specific, update regularly.
  • Don’t: write an autobiography; list every strength (keep something for the interview); mention salary; criticise a previous employer; overestimate yourself.
  • Statements in the résumé must match reality.

Types, classifications and characteristics

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Résumé is French for summary; CV is Latin.
  • Purpose: to get an interview call.
  • No handwritten résumés today.
  • Résumé must be specific, error-free, formatted.
  • Use action verbs in the past tense.
  • Three types: chronological, functional (skill), hybrid.
  • Length for entry-level: 1 to 1.5 pages.
  • Heading: real name, address, phone, email, URL.
  • Do not give references unless asked; avoid relatives.
  • Do not mention salary or criticise previous employer.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

A résumé (French: summary) or CV (Latin: course of life) is a formatted, specific, error-free document that kindles interest and secures an interview call. It presents career objective, education, experience, activities, honours, skills (computer, foreign language, writing, problem solving, decision making) and attributes (enthusiasm, initiative, motivation, adaptability, humour). Formats are chronological (experienced), functional/skill (entry-level) and hybrid. Open with heading, a specific career objective and qualifications from highest downward; use action verbs, keep to 1–1.5 pages, give references only on request, and never mention salary, criticise employers or overestimate yourself.

Lecture 56: Drafting an Effective Resume

Week 12 · Course book PDF pages 925–947 (printed pages 924–946)

Learning objectives

  • Draft each section of an effective résumé: objective statement, education, additional qualifications, experience, activities and honours, special skills, references.
  • Apply action verbs and parallelism in résumé writing.
  • Explain what an application (covering) letter is and its three-paragraph structure.
  • Use specific, active, unbiased language in the cover letter.
  • Analyse a sample entry-level résumé.

Important definitions

Objective statement

A one-to-three-line summary of area of expertise and career interest, placed just after the heading; focused and specific.

Covering letter (application letter)

A short letter, much like a business or sales letter, whose main purpose is to attract the employer to read the résumé.

Parallelism

Using the same grammatical form for items in a list, e.g. each résumé bullet beginning with a past-tense action verb.

Professional references

Referees such as professors, project in-charge, heads or employers, as opposed to personal acquaintances.

Scannable letter

A letter in which each paragraph begins with a topic sentence so that it can be skimmed or scanned.

Action phrase

A phrase beginning with a strong verb, e.g. organised a debate, conducted a workshop, represented the college team.

Core concepts

Three parts of a résumé

Like every letter, a résumé has an opening, body and close. Remember: a résumé is only a passport; leave something for the interview. Demonstrate what you can do for the company, not what the company can do for you.

Objective statement

  • One to three lines, complete and focused; tells recruiters your skills.
  • Avoid generalised statements such as ‘a position allowing me to use my knowledge in different areas’.
  • Be specific. Examples: ‘a position allowing me to apply my background in engineering and high performance computing to biological problems’; ‘a marketing position with an opportunity for eventual managerial status requiring strong customer contact skills’.

Education

  • List in reverse order: latest first (master’s, then B.Tech); a graduate starts from graduation.
  • Give grade as on the marks sheet: CGPA if the institute uses CGPA, percentage otherwise.

Additional qualifications

  • Courses that make you stand out: German or French, programming languages, effective English, business law, ethics.
  • Never mention what you do not have.
  • Write the course title, not just a code like HS 001.

Employment experience

  • Freshers: internship (e.g. two months), practice school, project in an organisation, brief positions related to the job.
  • Use action verbs; replace weak words (got, help) by assisted, demonstrated, supervised, coordinated.
  • Examples: organised a debate during Cognizance 2017; conducted a workshop; represented the college team in volleyball.
  • Mention exact dates and place of internship; everything may be questioned in the interview.

Parallelism in bullets

Begin each bullet with the same form: oversaw the planning…, assisted in training and development, created a positive…, supervised, coordinated. Do not mix full sentences with fragments.

Activities and honours

  • Volunteering, representing the institute, awards, fellowships and visits abroad carry weight.
  • Include only those you can discuss at the interview.

Special skills

  • Two foreign languages (German, French) help in MNCs; NSS/social service shows the ‘extra mile’.
  • Name software specifically (C++, Oracle); give course duration or dates.

References

  • Preferably ‘available upon request’; if compulsory give professional references (professor, project in-charge, head, employer) with full address, phone and URL.
  • Inform the referee in advance, send your résumé, tell about the company.

Application (covering) letter

  • Like a business or sales letter; purpose: attract the employer to read the résumé.
  • Short; three paragraphs, no longer than one page.
  • Begin with a header giving the addressee’s name and address.
  • Paragraph 1: get attention (AIDA-like sales technique), mention the source of the advertisement and your suitability. E.g. ‘I believe that my knowledge of public relations and proven communication and leadership skills make me a strong candidate for the position of media relations coordinator…’.
  • Paragraph 2 (body): support candidacy with experience; show, do not tell; refer to the enclosed résumé. E.g. ‘As a banking representative at … I provided quality service while promoting sale of products…’ (must have a grain of truth).
  • Paragraph 3: emphatic close creating opportunity: ‘I would welcome the opportunity to discuss these and other qualifications with you’; give a working phone number; ask for a personal interview; end with thanks.

Language of the letter

  • Active voice; past tense for past work, present for present responsibilities; balance past and present.
  • Be specific with figures: ‘I initiated a program to attract customers to Pizza Hut which resulted in a 10 percent increase’ instead of ‘I attempted to attract customers’.
  • Avoid weak words (tried, helped, attempted), gender-biased words (use chairperson), jargon, clichés, redundancy.
  • Proofread; mail with résumé in a proper envelope; address company values (‘your esteemed organisation’).

Sample résumé (Nischay Buddhiraja)

  • Objective: ‘Position in sales or marketing with opportunity for advancement and travel.’
  • Academic profile with action verbs in past tense: scored 100 percentile, completed B.Tech, obtained, secured.
  • Achievements: presented a paper on the Aadhaar card and data security at IIT Delhi and won first prize; submitted a project on Electro Volt at Apogee, BITS Pilani.
  • Extracurricular activities; no referees because the format had no space (could write ‘on request’); personal details: date of birth, father’s name, languages, nationality, hobbies.

EducationLatest first; grade as on marks sheet
Additional qualificationsReal courses, with titles
ExperienceAction verbs; dates; relevant
Activities and honoursDiscussable at interview

ReferencesProfessional; on request

Example

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Objective statement: 1 to 3 lines.
  • A résumé is a passport; leave something for the interview.
  • Education in reverse chronological order.
  • Mention CGPA or percentage as on the marks sheet.
  • Write the course name, not the code.
  • Never mention what you do not have.
  • Use parallelism with action verbs.

Quiz-related concepts (quiz PDF page references)
QuestionQuiz PDF pageConcept testedType
W12 Q1p.40Career objective in a résuméMCQ
W12 Q2p.40Action phrases in a résuméMCQ
W12 Q11p.42Résumé: referencesTrue/False

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

An effective résumé opens with a specific one-to-three-line objective, lists education from latest to earliest with grades as on the marks sheet, names real additional courses, uses parallel action-verb phrases for experience, highlights activities, honours and named special skills, and gives professional references only when needed. The covering letter, a short three-paragraph business/sales letter, attracts the reader in the first paragraph, shows rather than tells in the body, and closes emphatically with a request for an interview. Language should be active, specific, unbiased and error free.

Practice questions

Q1 MCQ The objective statement should be:
  1. Generalised
  2. One to three lines and specific
  3. A full page
  4. Omitted
Answer: B
Why: Specific and focused.
Q2 MCQ In the education section, entries are listed:
  1. Oldest first
  2. Latest first
  3. Alphabetically
  4. Randomly
Answer: B
Why: Reverse chronological.
Q3 MSQ The covering letter should be:
  1. Within one page
  2. Three paragraphs
  3. Attractive in the first paragraph
  4. A full autobiography
Answer: A, B, C
Why: It only attracts attention.
Q4 T/F Referees need not be informed before their names are given.
Answer: False
Why: Inform them and send your résumé.
Q5 Fill Use the course ______, not just the code, in the résumé.
Answer: title
Why: Recruiters cannot decode HS 001.
Q6 Short What is parallelism in a résumé?
Answer: Starting list items with the same grammatical form, e.g. past-tense action verbs such as supervised, coordinated, assisted.
Why: Makes bullets consistent.

Revision checklist

  • I can write a specific objective.
  • I can list education correctly.
  • I can use action verbs and parallelism.
  • I can structure a covering letter.
  • I can avoid weak and biased language.

Lecture 57: Facing Job Interviews: Part- I

Week 12 · Course book PDF pages 948–965 (printed pages 947–964)

Learning objectives

  • List the types of interview questions.
  • Explain how to be patient, positive and confident before and during an interview.
  • Describe the importance of appearance, personality, dress, grooming and bearing.
  • State the dress code for men and women.
  • Explain the role of voice, pauses, mannerisms and nonverbal cues.

Important definitions

Types of questions

Open, closed, probing, reflective, loaded, hypothetical and leading questions.

Tact

The skilful way of combining sentences and responding to questions without offending.

Personality test

An interview stage that judges personality traits and communication beyond subject knowledge.

Bearing

The manner of carrying oneself: facial expression, posture, enthusiasm.

Mannerisms

Habitual ways of behaving: how one looks, bends, turns, greets and responds.

Circumlocution

Roundabout questioning by interviewers that may puzzle the candidate.

Decorum

Appropriate, dignified behaviour and dress for a formal occasion.

Core concepts

Facing the call

  • An interview call brings delight and fear.
  • Success depends on how well you are prepared, not on contacts.
  • Most interviews are ‘decided in our brains’ by anticipation; tell yourself you can answer.

Types of questions

Open, closed, probing, reflective, loaded, hypothetical and leading questions (discussed in later lectures).

Patience, positivity, tact

  • Panel members may ask in quick succession; the best approach is patience.
  • Be confident and positive; be aware of likely questions; use tact and intuition; stay energetic.
  • Control your nerves; remain committed; do not show frustration by gesture or posture, since everything within is written on the face.

Conduct during questioning

  • Half of the battle is won by nonverbal behaviour.
  • Let the interviewer lead; do not answer before the question is complete.
  • Pause, stay composed, do not hurry or speak too fast; do not give the impression you know everything.
  • Listen throughout; answer as if responding, not imposing.

Bolstering confidence through appearance

  • Appearance covers dress, sitting posture, facial emotions, enthusiasm and preparations.
  • Appearances can be deceptive and experienced interviewers can detect it; do not beguile; present yourself as you are but positively. Quoted: ‘A false face must have a false heart within’ (Shakespeare, Macbeth).

Vocal characteristics and mannerisms

  • Neither too loud nor too fast; balance speed, rate, pitch.
  • Mannerisms (how you look at, bend towards or turn from different interviewers) can offend; stay composed.

Personality counts

  • Many selections include a personality test after the written test.
  • Dale Carnegie: personality is one of the important attributes for success; a New York department-store employment manager would rather hire a salesgirl who had not finished grade school but had a lovely smile than a PhD with a sober smile.
  • Employers prefer better communicators with good traits over degrees alone.

Dress and grooming (general)

  • Dress to suit the formality of the occasion: not gaudy, flamboyant or too loose; need not be expensive but should be simple, pleasant and clean.
  • Richard Bolles, What Color Is My Parachute?, is cited on dress and career choice.
  • Hair: dignified, not trendy or imitating celebrities (‘until you become a celebrity, be your own self’); short hair fares better; no long sideburns.
  • Face: warm, sincere; clean-shaven preferred, or neatly trimmed; trimmed nails.
  • Minimal cologne; minimal or no jewellery (no chains, bands, necklaces, earrings for men; rings kept minimum).

Dress for men

  • Shirt: good quality, button-down or classic colour, properly washed and ironed.
  • Suit: navy blue, dark brown or grey; natural-fibre blend; properly tailored, not designer; fresh and clean; two-piece.
  • Tie: good-quality blend, conservative stripes, simple pattern; tip should end near the centre of the belt.
  • Shoes: black (or brown), polished, not pointed or designer; dark socks.
  • Black or blue, not red; carry a small briefcase or portfolio.

Dress for women

  • Conservative business suit or full dress in natural woven blend and soft colour matching skin and hair.
  • Sensible polished pumps, not high heels.
  • Small purse; minimal nail polish; minimal jewellery: one set of earrings and no more than one ring on each hand.

Voice and pronunciation

  • Voice is the vehicle of expression: audible, clear, distinct, neither too fast nor too slow.
  • Use standard, understandable pronunciation, articulated clearly.
  • Use silence and pauses; interviewers are observing, not trying to eliminate you.

Tough questioning and mindset

  • Interviewers may rotate or twist questions; stay polite, flexible and tactful.
  • Each interview, even a failure, has meaning; the door is never closed. ‘You can fare well only when you can dare well.’

Types, classifications and characteristics

Theories, principles, models and contributors

Personality over degrees — Dale Carnegie

Personality is vital to career success; a lovely smile may outweigh a PhD with a sober smile.

Dress as indicator — Richard Bolles (cited)

Dress indicates background and attitude; author of What Color Is My Parachute?

Examples and applications

Carnegie’s example of the New York department store employment manager.

Example

Interviewer rotates questions to puzzle the candidate.

Example

Candidate answers before the question ends and gets into trouble.

Candidate with celebrity-style hair or jewellery distracted from the interview.

Important facts for MCQ revision

Exam tip
  • Preparation is the best way to face an interview.
  • Question types: open, closed, probing, reflective, loaded, hypothetical, leading.
  • Let the interviewer lead; do not answer before the question ends.
  • Half the battle is won by nonverbal behaviour.
  • Carnegie’s smile example.
  • Men: navy blue, dark brown or grey suit; black polished shoes.
  • Tie tip near the centre of the belt.
  • Women: low-heeled polished pumps; minimal jewellery.
  • Use pauses and clear, audible voice.
  • Failure in one interview is not the end.

Full question text and answers: Quiz analysis.

Summary

Facing an interview calls for preparation, patience, positivity and nerve control. Let the interviewer lead, listen fully, pause before answering and use tact. Appearance, personality, dress, grooming, voice and mannerisms all communicate; Carnegie stresses that personality (even a lovely smile) can outweigh degrees. Men should wear a well-tailored navy, brown or grey suit, button-down shirt, simple tie ending at the belt centre and polished black shoes; women a conservative business suit or full dress, low-heeled pumps and minimal jewellery. Speak clearly with controlled rate, use pauses, remain polite with tricky questions, and remember that every interview is a learning experience.

Lecture 58: Facing Job Interviews: Part II

Week 12 · Course book PDF pages 966–989 (printed pages 965–988)

Learning objectives

  • Define and exemplify the seven question types: open, closed, probing, reflective, loaded, hypothetical, leading.
  • Answer common interview questions about self, experience, strengths, weaknesses, money and ethics.
  • Handle inappropriate personal questions tactfully.
  • List what to do before, during and after the interview.
  • Recall Charles Dickens’ closing quotation.

Important definitions

Open question

A question inviting a free, varied, elaborate answer, usually asked at the start to make the candidate comfortable (e.g. Tell us briefly about yourself).

Closed question

A question with limited answers such as yes/no or a date (e.g. When did you complete your B.Tech?); often followed by a follow-up needing justification.

Probing question

A question that tests the candidate’s opinion, bias and attitude (e.g. Should students be allowed to take part in politics, including state and central politics?).

Reflective question

A question that mirrors the candidate’s statement or view to test rationale, logic and forward thinking (e.g. Am I right in thinking you are against opening many IITs and IIMs?).

Loaded question

A question in which the interviewer’s own bias or assumption is built in (e.g. Your GPA after first semester has been too low; how do you justify that?).

Hypothetical question

A question that places the candidate in an imagined unforeseen situation to test decision making (e.g. What would you do if disgruntled employees wanted to damage company property?).

Leading question

A question that suggests the answer the interviewer expects (e.g. Don’t you think our company is the best in reputation?).

Teetotaler

A person who does not drink alcohol.

Core concepts

Why know question types

  • Interviews include general questions, not only subject questions: integrity, honesty, sincerity, resourcefulness, initiative, leadership, communication.
  • The interview is a discourse in which meaning is negotiated; a wrong hire hurts the organisation for years.
  • Boards include subject experts and psychologists; how you answer matters as much as what.
  • Interviews last about 10–20 minutes.

Open questions

  • Asked at the beginning to create a comfortable atmosphere.
  • Examples: Tell us briefly about yourself; What is your area of expertise? How did you come to know about this job? What prompted you to apply? Why do you consider yourself suitable?
  • Answering is an art: indicate your qualities and experience briefly.

Closed questions

  • Answer limited to yes/no or exact facts: When did you complete graduation? Where did you receive first professional training? Do you know data processing? Do you have an advanced degree in electronics?
  • A follow-up such as ‘What is the relevance of electronics today?’ requires justification.

Probing questions

  • Test opinion, bias, attitude: ‘I believe students should be allowed to take part in politics. Should this include state and central politics?’
  • Give a reasoned opinion (e.g. politics develops leadership traits).

Reflective questions

  • Test rationale and forward thinking: ‘Am I rightly thinking you are against opening many IITs and IIMs?’; ‘So you want public sector companies to be totally privatised?’
  • Answers may differ (like the blind men and the elephant) but must be justified.

Loaded questions

  • The interviewer’s bias is embedded: ‘Your GPA is too low—how do you justify that?’; ‘Should places of religious disputes be converted into public parks?’
  • Responsibility for the reaction lies with the candidate; respond logically and calmly.

Hypothetical questions

  • Test decisions in unforeseen crises: ‘What if disgruntled employees want to damage company property?’; ‘How would you handle employees misusing company assets?’
  • Answer: first dialogue to understand the problem, then take action if needed; prevent others from being instigated.

Leading questions

  • Suggest the expected answer: ‘Don’t you think our company is the best in reputation?’; ‘Should the company be free to retrench people in a market tumble?’
  • Agree tactfully where harmless; where ethics is involved, respond ethically (e.g. call a meeting, share the burden, avoid retrenchment).

Experience and accomplishment questions

  • Why should we hire you when others have more experience? – state your qualities and fit.
  • You change jobs frequently? – give a positive, non-blaming reason; do not criticise a boss.
  • Team or alone? – prefer teams: save time, build relationships, better solutions.
  • Where do you see yourself after 5 years? – be modest; do not say ‘in your position’.
  • Boss dissatisfied with your work? – modest, receptive.
  • Foreign assignment? – be affirmative.
  • Co-worker supplying false data? – verify quietly from various sources.

Strengths, weaknesses, personal and money questions

  • Strength: state it affirmatively. Weakness: present it so it becomes a merit (e.g. perfectionism).
  • Alcohol: be honest (‘I am a teetotaler’ or ‘occasionally, not habituated’).
  • What if you are not selected? – hopeful, affirmative; learn from mistakes for the next interview.
  • Salary: do not disclose; refer to the advertised structure and your qualification.
  • Importance of money? – important, but not the only need; belonging matters.
  • Also hobbies, extracurriculars, general awareness (new products, nuclear policy, demonetisation), ethics (misuse of assets).

Inappropriate personal questions

  • Examples: divorced/separated? own house? maiden name? religious preference?
  • Do not overreact; answer rationally and positively: ‘I believe in humanity’; ‘I have lived in organisation accommodation and will own a house in due course’.

Before the interview

  • Select a comfortable, sober, clean, ironed dress; do not buy a new outfit obsessively.
  • Polished formal shoes; testimonials in order.
  • Read newspapers weeks ahead; anticipate current and general topics.

On the day

  • Reach before time; read the morning newspaper.
  • Talk to other candidates to ease nervousness; think positively and recall joyful memories.
  • Enter as if you belong; greet all; sit only when permitted; thank them.
  • Maintain eye contact with the questioner.
  • Do not accept an offer until all interviews are over, but never say no outright (e.g. lower position: ‘first let me get selected’).

After the interview

  • Do not turn back to look at the members; close the door gently; thank all and leave with a confident impression.
  • Quote: Charles Dickens – ‘Whatever I have tried to do in my life, I have tried to do well…I have always been thoroughly in earnest.’ Honesty is the best policy.
  • Interviews are continuous; ‘Prepare well, do well, perform well, and succeed well.’

Types, classifications and characteristics

  • Closed questions are often followed by justification questions.
  • Hypothetical questions test decision making in crises.
  • Do not disclose salary; do not criticise past employers.
  • Present weakness as a merit.
